file-storage documentation

file-storage

file-storage is a production-grade hybrid chunked content-addressed file storage microservice written in Rust. Files are split with FastCDC content-defined chunking, hashed with BLAKE3, compressed with zstd, optionally encrypted with AES-256-GCM, and stored as content-addressed blobs with a redb ACID metadata index and a three-tier moka cache. Two transports expose the same storage core: a REST API (axum) and a gRPC API (tonic), served by one process on two ports.

What this book covers

SectionAudienceContents

Migrationearly adoptersv0 → v1 migration notes

The 60-second version

cargo run --release -- --config config/default.yaml

Two listeners come up: REST on :8080, gRPC on :9090.

# REST
TOKEN=$(curl -s -X POST localhost:8080/api/v1/auth/login \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"username":"admin","password":"admin123"}' | jq -r .access_token)

echo "hello" | curl -s -X POST "localhost:8080/api/v1/files?name=hello.txt" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" --data-binary @-

# gRPC — discovery works without any proto files thanks to reflection
grpcurl -plaintext localhost:9090 list

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
// Rust client SDK
let mut c = file_storage::grpc::FileStorageClient::connect("http://127.0.0.1:9090").await?;
c.login("admin", "admin123").await?;
let resp = c.upload(
    file_storage::grpc::generated::pb::UploadHeader {
        name: "hello.txt".into(),
        ..Default::default()
    },
    std::io::Cursor::new(b"hello".to_vec()),
).await?;
}

A file uploaded over gRPC and downloaded over REST (or the reverse) is byte-identical — both transports share one AppState, one block store, one index.

Architecture Overview

This page maps every component of the file-storage service, how they connect, and the design principles that shaped them. It is the entry point to the architecture section: start here, then follow the links into the deep-dive pages for the area you care about (data flow, storage layout, chunking, caching, replication, GC, security).

Overview

file-storage is a single Rust process that exposes one storage core through two wire protocols: a REST API built on axum 0.7 listening on server.port (default 8080) and a gRPC API built on tonic 0.12 listening on server.grpc_port (default 9090). Both listeners share one AppState — index, block store, caches, upload pipeline, retrieval engine, auth, tenants, audit, replicator, and GC — so a file uploaded over gRPC is byte-identical when downloaded over REST, and vice versa.

Files are never stored whole. Every upload is split by FastCDC content-defined chunking, hashed with BLAKE3, compressed with zstd, optionally encrypted with per-tenant AES-256-GCM, and written as content-addressed blobs into a sharded on-disk block store. A redb database holds the metadata (manifests, chunk refcounts, name/tag indexes, audit log, tenants) in a single ACID file.

flowchart LR
    Client["Client (curl / SDK / grpcurl)"]
    REST["REST listener — axum 0.7 :8080"]
    GRPC["gRPC listener — tonic 0.12 :9090"]
    Auth["Auth middleware<br/>JWT HS256 + api-key + rate limit"]
    Validator["Validator<br/>limits + metadata checks"]
    Pipeline["UploadPipeline<br/>FastCDC → BLAKE3 → zstd → AES-256-GCM"]
    BlockStore["BlockStore<br/>ab/cd/&lt;64-hex&gt;.bin"]
    IndexDb["IndexDb — redb<br/>chunks / files / indexes / audit / tenants"]
    CacheLayer["CacheLayer — moka<br/>L1 plaintext, L2 blobs, L3 manifests"]
    Retrieval["RetrievalEngine<br/>range slicing + read-ahead + verify"]
    Replicator["Replicator<br/>WAL-style async shipper"]
    Replica["Replica node"]
    Gc["GcService<br/>refcount / reachability passes"]
    Metrics["metrics.rs<br/>Prometheus file_storage_*"]

    Client --> REST
    Client --> GRPC
    REST --> Auth --> Validator --> Pipeline
    GRPC --> Auth
    Pipeline --> BlockStore
    Pipeline --> IndexDb
    Pipeline --> Replicator
    Replicator -->|batched ops| Replica
    Retrieval --> CacheLayer
    CacheLayer --> BlockStore
    Retrieval --> IndexDb
    Gc --> IndexDb
    Gc --> BlockStore
    Gc -->|purge ops| Replicator
    REST -.-> Metrics
    GRPC -.-> Metrics

Figure 1: Component diagram. Solid arrows are data paths on the write/read paths; the dashed arrows are metrics exposition from both listeners.

How it works

Design principles

  1. Content addressing. Every blob is keyed by its BLAKE3 hash (or a tenant-scoped derivation of it under encryption). Identical content is stored once and reference-counted, which makes dedup a structural property rather than a background job. See Chunking & Dedup and ADR 0001.
  2. Crash safety through blob-before-index ordering. Blobs are fully written and fsynced (temp file → fsync → rename → directory fsync) before the single redb transaction that references them. A crash can therefore leave an orphan blob that GC sweeps later — never a dangling index reference to missing data. See Data Flow and Storage Layout.
  3. Tenant isolation. Every manifest carries a tenant_id; the name_index and tag_index tables are tenant-prefixed, so lookups physically cannot cross tenant boundaries. Cross-tenant access requires the admin role. See Security.
  4. Immutability of stored blobs. Blobs are never rewritten in place. Same-name replacement writes a new manifest and soft-deletes the old one; compression and encryption changes produce new content, hence new hashes. Immutability is what makes the caches, replication, and pull-repair safe.
  5. Soft delete + grace period. Deletes write a tombstone (deleted_at_ms); a background collector purges only after retention.grace_period_secs (default 86400), keeping accidental-delete recovery possible. See Garbage Collection.

Configuration

All runtime knobs live in a YAML file (see config/default.yaml) and can be overridden with environment variables using the FS_ prefix plus __ nesting (for example FS_SERVER__GRPC_PORT=9091). There is no hot reload: every key requires a restart.

SectionWhat it controls
serverBind host, REST port (8080), grpc_port (9090), grpc_tls
storageroot (block store), temp_dir (multipart spool), shard_depth (0–4)
databasepath to the redb file
chunkingFastCDC min / avg / max (8192 / 32768 / 131072)
compressionzstd level (3) and min_size (1024)
cacheL1/L2 byte budgets and TTLs, manifest capacity/TTL
authjwt_secret, access/refresh TTLs, static users
encryptionenabled, keys_dir
rate_limitper_second, burst
replicationenabled, mode (async/sync), target_url, upstream_url, queue/batch/flush tuning
retentiongc_mode, grace_period_secs, gc_interval_secs, orphan_min_age_secs
limitsmax_file_size (10 GiB), max_body (1 GiB)
tenantsSeeded tenant IDs and names

The full key-by-key reference lives in the Configuration reference; environment-variable syntax is covered under Environment Variables.

Examples

Log in, upload, and read back over both transports:

TOKEN=$(curl -s -X POST localhost:8080/api/v1/auth/login \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"username":"admin","password":"admin123"}' | jq -r .access_token)

# upload (raw body, streamed)
curl -s -X POST "localhost:8080/api/v1/files?name=f.bin&tags=demo" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/octet-stream' \
  --data-binary @/tmp/f.bin

# download over gRPC after uploading over REST — same bytes
grpcurl -plaintext -d '{"service": "file_storage.v1.FileService"}' \
    localhost:9090 grpc.health.v1.Health/Check

A successful upload returns the dedup report inline:

{
  "file_id": "01930000-0000-7000-8000-0000000000ab",
  "size": 67108864,
  "size_stored": 67102208,
  "chunk_count": 2048,
  "dedup_chunks": 12,
  "dedup_bytes": 393216,
  "content_hash": "9f2c…64-hex…"
}

Data Flow

This page walks bytes through the service end to end: the upload pipeline from HTTP request to 201 Created, the retrieval path through the three cache tiers, and the sequence diagrams for normal uploads, dedup hits, and ranged downloads. Application developers and anyone debugging latency or data-integrity issues should read it.

Overview

Every write and every read passes through one pipeline in src/core/pipeline.rs (write) and one engine in src/storage/retrieval.rs (read), regardless of transport. Chunking and whole-file hashing are strictly sequential; compression, encryption, and blob writes run up to 8 tasks wide (PIPELINE_CONCURRENCY, buffer_unordered); the index transaction is a single redb commit. The ordering invariant — blob fsynced before index transaction — is what makes crash recovery trivial.

How it works

Upload path

HTTP body (multipart spooled to storage.temp_dir, or raw stream) / gRPC data frames
        |
        v
+------------------+  rejects early:  +--------------------------------------+
| validate         |----------------->| 400 validation_failed / 413 too large|
+--------+---------+   max_file_size  +--------------------------------------+
         |           max_body
         v
FastCDC v2020 stream chunker  (min 8 KiB / avg 32 KiB / max 128 KiB)
   rolling carry buffer -> cut points depend on content, not packetisation
         |  sequential
         v
for each chunk, up to 8 in flight (buffer_unordered):
   BLAKE3(plaintext) --> storage hash
        |              (encryption on: storage hash = BLAKE3(tenant_id || plain_hash))
        v
   zstd level 3        -- skip when chunk < compression.min_size (1024 B)
        |                 or compression does not shrink (stored raw)
        v
   AES-256-GCM (optional, per-tenant key)   blob = nonce(12) || ct || tag(16)
        |
        v
   blob write to BlockStore:  .tmp-<uuidv7> -> fsync file -> rename -> fsync dir
        |                     (idempotent: returns false when blob already exists)
        v
ONE redb transaction (Durability::Immediate, single fsync):
   chunks     : refcount++ per unique storage hash  -> per-chunk dedup report
   files      : put Manifest (MessagePack, new UUIDv7 file_id)
   name_index : upsert tenant||0x00||name -> file_id
                (previous same-name manifest soft-deleted, refcounts kept)
   tag_index  : one row per tag
        |
        v
replication queue: chunk ops first, then manifest op (order preserved)
        |            async mode: fire and forget; sync mode: wait_flushed barrier
        v
201 Created  { file_id, name, mime, size, size_stored, chunk_count,
               content_hash, dedup_chunks, dedup_bytes, created_at_ms }

Figure 1: The upload pipeline. The only fsync-ordered boundary is blob-before-index; everything else is concurrency-friendly.

Retrieval path

GET /api/v1/files/:id            gRPC Download{file_id, start?, end?}
        |                                 |
        v                                 v
manifest lookup:  L3 (moka, 10 000 entries / 60 s TTL)  -- miss --> redb `files`
        |            soft-deleted manifests read as 404 not_found
        v
for each chunk in order (read-ahead prefetches chunks N+1..N+4 into L2):
   L1 plaintext (256 MiB / 300 s)  -- hit --> stream to client
        | miss
        v
   L2 stored blob (512 MiB / 3600 s) -- hit --> decrypt -> decompress
        | miss                                      -> verify -> insert into L1
        v
   disk: read ab/cd/<64-hex>.bin
        |  replica with replication.upstream_url and blob missing:
        |     pull blob from primary, write it locally, continue
        v
   decrypt (if encrypted) -> decompress (if compressed)
        |
        v
   BLAKE3 verify plaintext against plain_hash
        |  mismatch -> HashMismatch/Corruption (500, DATA_LOSS),
        |             file_storage_hash_mismatch_total++
        v
   insert into L1 (+ L2 holds the raw blob) --> slice to requested range
        |
        v
   stream to client  (200 with ETag / 206 with Content-Range / gRPC DownloadChunk)

Figure 2: The retrieval path. Verification happens after every disk load; cache hits skip it because they were verified before insertion.

Full upload request

sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    participant C as Client
    participant H as REST handler
    participant P as UploadPipeline
    participant B as BlockStore
    participant I as IndexDb (redb)
    participant X as Replicator

    C->>H: POST /api/v1/files (Authorization: Bearer JWT)
    H->>H: auth, rate limit, validate name/mime/limits
    H->>P: process(tenant, name, mime, tags, body stream)
    loop per chunk, up to 8 in flight
        P->>P: FastCDC cut, BLAKE3, zstd-3, AES-GCM (optional)
        P->>B: write(storage_hash, blob)
        B-->>P: ok (false if blob already existed)
    end
    P->>I: register_chunks + put_manifest — ONE transaction
    I-->>P: per-chunk dedup report (refcount bumps)
    P->>X: enqueue chunk ops, then manifest op
    alt replication.mode = sync
        P->>X: wait_flushed (bounded by sync_timeout_ms)
        X-->>P: batch acknowledged
    end
    P-->>H: UploadOutcome { manifest, dedup_chunks, dedup_bytes }
    H-->>C: 201 Created + JSON report

Figure 3: A full upload request over REST. The gRPC Upload RPC follows the same pipeline behind the shared AppState.

Dedup-hit upload

sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    participant C as Client
    participant P as UploadPipeline
    participant B as BlockStore
    participant I as IndexDb (redb)

    C->>P: upload stream (bytes identical to an already-stored file)
    loop per chunk
        P->>B: write(storage_hash, blob)
        B-->>P: false — blob exists, nothing new persists
    end
    P->>I: register_chunks — ONE transaction
    I-->>P: report: true per chunk (refcount++ only, no blob row created)
    P->>I: put_manifest (new file_id referencing the existing chunks)
    P-->>C: dedup_chunks = chunk_count, dedup_bytes = sum of size_stored
    Note over B,I: file_storage_dedup_hits_total and dedup_bytes_saved_total increase; no blob bytes are written

Figure 4: A dedup hit. The blob write is still attempted (it is idempotent), but the index reports the chunk as existing, so only refcounts move.

Ranged download with cache misses

sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    participant C as Client
    participant H as Range handler
    participant M as Manifest cache (L3)
    participant I as IndexDb (redb)
    participant K as Chunk cache (L1/L2)
    participant D as BlockStore (disk)
    participant U as Primary (upstream_url)

    C->>H: GET /api/v1/files/:id/range  Range: bytes=start-end
    H->>M: manifest(file_id)
    M->>I: load (L3 miss after 60 s TTL)
    I-->>M: Manifest (MessagePack)
    H->>H: chunks_in_range(start, end) — half-open slice plan
    loop per chunk intersecting the range
        H->>K: get_plain — L1 miss
        H->>K: get_or_load_blob — L2 miss
        K->>D: read ab/cd/&lt;hash&gt;.bin
        alt replica and blob missing
            K->>U: pull chunk (GET /internal/chunk/:hash or gRPC PullChunk)
            U-->>K: blob, written locally before use
        end
        K->>K: decrypt -> decompress -> BLAKE3 verify against plain_hash
        K-->>H: plaintext (inserted into L1; raw blob cached in L2)
    end
    H-->>C: 206 Partial Content, Content-Range: bytes start-end/size

Figure 5: A ranged download where every tier misses once. Read-ahead has meanwhile warmed L2 for chunks N+1..N+4. Multi-range requests are served as their first range.

Configuration

The data flow is shaped by these keys (defaults in parentheses):

KeyEffect on the flow
chunking.min/avg/maxCut-point spacing; max is capped at 16 MiB
compression.level / compression.min_sizezstd effort; chunks below min_size (1024) stored raw
encryption.enabled / encryption.keys_dirAdds the AES-GCM stage and switches the storage-hash derivation
limits.max_file_size / limits.max_bodyRejection thresholds (10 GiB file, 1 GiB body)
cache.*L1/L2 byte budgets and TTLs, L3 manifest capacity/TTL
replication.mode / replication.sync_timeout_msWhether uploads wait for the replication flush barrier
storage.temp_dirSpool location for multipart bodies before streaming

See the Configuration reference for the complete list.

Examples

Range semantics differ slightly between transports. REST uses an inclusive end (RFC 7233) and answers 206 with Content-Range (or 416 with bytes */size); the gRPC Download request uses a half-open exclusive end that is clamped to the file size and returns OUT_OF_RANGE when start >= size.

# REST: bytes 0..1023 inclusive
curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" -H 'Range: bytes=0-1023' \
     localhost:8080/api/v1/files/<id>/range -o part.bin    # 206

# suffix form: last 512 bytes
curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" -H 'Range: bytes=-512' \
     localhost:8080/api/v1/files/<id>/range -o tail.bin

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
// gRPC client SDK: stream bytes 1024..4096 into a writer
let mut client = file_storage::grpc::FileStorageClient::connect("http://127.0.0.1:9090").await?;
client.download_range(&file_id, &mut writer).await?;
}

Inspect the per-chunk plumbing of a stored file via the metadata endpoint — each entry shows the flags the pipeline set:

curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
     "localhost:8080/api/v1/files/<id>/meta?chunks=true" | jq '.chunks[0]'

{
  "offset": 0,
  "size_original": 33161,
  "size_stored": 12104,
  "hash": "3f9a…",
  "compressed": true,
  "encrypted": false
}

Storage Layout

This page documents exactly what a running node writes to disk: the sharded content-addressed blob tree, the six redb tables and their binary encodings, the Manifest structure that ties them together, and the crash-atomicity story that binds blobs and index entries. Architects planning backups, capacity, or disaster recovery should read it first.

Overview

There are exactly three kinds of durable state, controlled by three config sections:

Config keyDurable stateContents
storage.root (data/blocks)Blob treeOne immutable .bin file per unique chunk
database.path (data/index.redb)MetadataSingle redb file with six tables
storage.temp_dir (data/tmp)TransientMultipart upload spool (never authoritative)

Blobs carry no metadata of their own — their name is their content hash. All structure lives in redb, which is what keeps the write path atomic and the read path verifiable.

How it works

Sharded blob tree

Blobs live at <storage.root>/<ab>/<cd>/<64-hex>.bin, where ab/cd are the first storage.shard_depth bytes of the 32-byte storage hash rendered as hex. With the default shard_depth: 2, a chunk whose hash begins 3f9a4b… lands at:

data/blocks/
└── 3f/
    └── 9a/
        └── 3f9a4b17c0d8e5f2a1b3c4d5e6f708192a3b4c5d6e7f8091a2b3c4d5e6f70819.bin

Figure 1: Blob placement at the default shard_depth: 2 — two hex-char directories, then the full 64-hex file name.

shard_depth: 0 is a flat directory. The depth is capped at 4, and the recorded relative path (3f/9a/<hash>.bin) is stored in chunk metadata, so reads never re-derive layout assumptions:

shard_depthLayoutDirectories at level 2ⁿBlobs per directory at 1M chunksTrade-off
0<root>/<hash>.bin—1 000 000Simplest; large directories stress some filesystems
1ab/<hash>.bin256~3 900Modest spread; still big directories
2 (default)ab/cd/<hash>.bin65 536~15Balanced; two small stat hops
3ab/cd/ef/<hash>.bin16.7 M<1Deep trees slow the orphan sweeper’s walk
4ab/cd/ef/01/<hash>.bin4.3 B<1Maximum fan-out; directory overhead dominates

Blob writes are idempotent: BlockStore::write returns false without touching anything if the target already exists, so concurrent writers of the same chunk converge safely. The orphan sweeper walks the tree to shard_depth + 1 levels, skipping .tmp-* and .write-probe-* files.

The six redb tables

All keys and values are raw bytes; the encodings are explicit per table:

database.path = data/index.redb            (redb 2.2, single file, WAL + fsync)
+------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| chunks     | key: 32-byte storage hash (raw)                                 |
|            | val: bincode ChunkMeta { refcount: u64,                         |
|            |                          blob_size: u64,   // nonce+tag incl.   |
|            |                          created_at_ms: i64,                     |
|            |                          on_disk_path: String  // "3f/9a/<hex>.bin" |
|            |        }                                                        |
|------------+-----------------------------------------------------------------|
| files      | key: 16-byte file UUID (raw)                                    |
|            | val: MessagePack Manifest                                       |
|------------+-----------------------------------------------------------------|
| name_index | key: tenant(16B) || 0x00 || name (bytes)                        |
|            | val: 16-byte file UUID                                          |
|------------+-----------------------------------------------------------------|
| tag_index  | key: tenant(16B) || 0x00 || tag || 0x00 || file_id (16B)        |
|            | val: empty (the key carries all information)                    |
|------------+-----------------------------------------------------------------|
| audit      | key: UUIDv7 (16B, time-ordered -> cheap range scans)            |
|            | val: JSON AuditEvent                                            |
|------------+-----------------------------------------------------------------|
| tenants    | key: 16-byte tenant UUID (raw)                                  |
|            | val: JSON Tenant                                                |
+------------------------------------------------------------------------------+

Figure 2: The six redb tables. Every write transaction commits with Durability::Immediate; blocking redb work runs on tokio’s blocking pool.

Design notes on the two indexes:

  • name_index makes file names unique per tenant — the 0x00 separator is unambiguous because tenant keys are fixed-width 16 bytes.
  • tag_index keys embed the file_id, so listing by tag is a byte-prefix scan (tenant || 0x00 || tag || 0x00) and removal is a key delete. Tag queries take precedence over name filters in GET /api/v1/files.
  • audit keys are UUIDv7, so the audit log is physically time-ordered — GET /api/v1/admin/audit?limit= walks the tail of the table.
  • The tenant prefix in both indexes is what enforces tenant isolation at the storage layer: a scan scoped to one tenant’s bytes cannot return another tenant’s rows.

Manifest structure

A Manifest is the authoritative description of a stored file, serialised with MessagePack into files:

FieldTypeMeaning
file_idUuid (16 B)UUIDv7, minted at upload; primary key of files
nameStringDisplay name; unique per tenant via name_index
tenant_idUuidOwning tenant; part of all index key prefixes
mimeStringMIME type supplied at upload
size_originalu64Plaintext byte count
size_storedu64Sum of size_stored across chunks
original_hash[u8; 32]BLAKE3 over the whole plaintext file; becomes the HTTP ETag
chunksVec<ChunkRef>Ordered by offset; drives retrieval and ranges
tagsVec<String>Indexed in tag_index
created_at_ms / updated_at_msi64Epoch milliseconds
deleted_at_msOption<i64>Soft-delete tombstone; None while live
versionu64Optimistic-concurrency counter, bumped on mutation

Each ChunkRef records: hash (32-byte block-store key), plain_hash (32-byte plaintext BLAKE3 used for read-back verification), offset, size_original, size_stored, and explicit compressed / encrypted flags — no magic-byte sniffing at read time.

Atomicity: blob before index

The write path is arranged so that any crash leaves recoverable state:

1. write blob to .tmp-<uuidv7>        (same shard directory)
2. fsync the temp file                (data durable)
3. rename .tmp-<uuidv7> -> <hash>.bin (atomic within the directory)
4. fsync the shard directory          (rename survives power loss)
        ---- only now ----
5. ONE redb transaction:
      refcount++ / Manifest insert / index updates
      commit durability = Durability::Immediate (WAL + fsync)

Figure 3: The atomic write sequence. The index transaction runs only after the blob is fully durable.

Consequences of this ordering:

  • No dangling references. The index never points at a blob that is not already on disk.
  • Crash residue is bounded. A crash between steps 4 and 5 leaves an orphan blob with no metadata row. The GC orphan sweep deletes it only after it ages past retention.orphan_min_age_secs (default 3600), which is long enough to exclude blobs that are legitimately mid-upload.
  • One fsync boundary per upload. All of a file’s chunk refcounts and its manifest commit in a single redb transaction, so dedup refcount updates are race-free and a partially-registered file is impossible.
  • Same-name replacement is soft. Re-uploading under an existing name soft-deletes the old manifest (deleted_at_ms set) but keeps its refcounts, so shared blobs stay alive until GC purges the tombstone.

Configuration

KeyDefaultNotes
storage.rootdata/blocksBlock-store root; mount on the volume that must survive
storage.temp_dirdata/tmpMultipart spool; same filesystem as root avoids cross-device renames
storage.shard_depth20–4; changing it affects new writes and the sweeper’s walk
database.pathdata/index.redbSingle-file ACID index; copy for backups while the process is stopped

Environment overrides follow the FS_ + __ convention, for example FS_STORAGE__SHARD_DEPTH=1 or FS_DATABASE__PATH=/var/lib/file-storage/index.redb. There is no hot reload.

Examples

Chunk-level metadata is exposed through the manifest endpoint, showing exactly what landed where:

curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
     "localhost:8080/api/v1/files/<id>/meta?chunks=true" | jq '.chunks[0]'

{
  "hash": "3f9a4b17c0d8e5f2a1b3c4d5e6f708192a3b4c5d6e7f8091a2b3c4d5e6f70819",
  "plain_hash": "7c1e9a04b2d8f6a3c5e1b7d0924f8a3c6e5d1b0a9f8e7d6c5b4a3f2e1d0c9b8a7",
  "offset": 0,
  "size_original": 33161,
  "size_stored": 12104,
  "compressed": true,
  "encrypted": false
}

The hash above is the storage key — the blob’s file name plus its chunks-table row. With encryption enabled the two hashes diverge, because the storage key becomes BLAKE3(tenant_id || plain_hash) (see ADR 0007).

On-disk, the same upload looks like:

$ find data/blocks -type f | head -3
data/blocks/3f/9a/3f9a4b17….bin
data/blocks/ab/cd/abcd1234….bin
data/blocks/0e/5f/0e5f8888….bin

Chunking & Dedup

This page explains how incoming bytes are cut into content-defined chunks, why chunk boundaries survive edits and different transport packetisation, and how that translates into deduplicated storage. Developers tuning dedup behaviour or debugging why two uploads dedup differently should read it.

Overview

file-storage uses the FastCDC 2020 algorithm (the fastcdc crate, version 5, tokio feature) to split every upload into chunks of roughly 32 KiB, bounded between 8 KiB and 128 KiB by default. Each chunk is hashed with BLAKE3; the hash is the chunk’s storage key, so identical content is stored once and reference-counted in the redb chunks table. Dedup is therefore an inline, exact-match operation — not a background scan — and re-uploading the same file reports dedup_chunks = chunk_count with zero new blob bytes.

How it works

FastCDC 2020 in brief

FastCDC is a content-defined chunker: it slides a gear-hash window over the byte stream and declares a cut point when the rolling hash matches a bitmask derived from the configured average size. Three parameters shape the cut-point distribution:

  • min (default 8192) — no cut before this many bytes; protects against degenerate runs of tiny chunks.
  • avg (default 32768) — the target expectation; the bitmask is tuned so the expected distance between cuts is 32 KiB.
  • max (default 131072) — a forced cut ceiling; also the configuration cap is 16 MiB, with the constraint min ≤ avg ≤ max.

The 2020 refinement of the algorithm reworks the hash and mask handling so the scanner runs at memory bandwidth: the measured pure chunking rate on the benchmark rig is 1134 MB/s (~1.1 GB/s) with 4 MiB input at 8/32/128 KiB — see Benchmarks.

flowchart TD
    A["byte stream<br/>(HTTP body / gRPC data frames)"] --> B["StreamAdapter<br/>pulls stream items into a BytesMut carry buffer"]
    B --> C["fastcdc::v2020::AsyncStreamCDC<br/>gear hash over the rolling window"]
    C --> D{"cut condition met?<br/>min ≤ len ≤ max"}
    D -- "no" --> B
    D -- "yes" --> E["yield (absolute_offset, chunk Bytes)"]
    E --> F["pipeline: BLAKE3 -> zstd -> AES-GCM? -> blob write -> index txn"]

Figure 1: chunk_stream in src/core/chunker.rs. The adapter bridges an arbitrary byte stream into tokio’s AsyncRead; the chunker owns its carry buffer, so nothing upstream needs buffering whole files.

Boundaries are independent of packetisation

AsyncStreamCDC maintains its own rolling carry buffer, which gives the pipeline a property the REST and gRPC transports rely on: chunk boundaries depend only on content, never on how the bytes were packetised. The same 2 MB payload fed through chunk_stream in 1000-byte pieces and in 250 KB pieces produces byte-for-byte identical chunk hash sequences. This is asserted in unit tests (boundaries_independent_of_packetisation, matches_single_pass_scanner, respects_min_and_max), which compare streamed output against the synchronous single-pass scanner chunk_slice.

Consequences worth knowing:

  • A multipart upload and a raw-body upload of the same bytes yield the same chunk set — dedup works across transport styles.
  • A gRPC Upload stream (1 MiB client pieces) and a curl --data-binary upload dedup against each other.
  • An upstream stream error aborts the scanner; buffered bytes that never reached a cut point are discarded with it, and the original StorageError is recovered from the io::Error wrapper.

Tuning the chunk sizes

The chunking.min / avg / max keys trade dedup granularity against per-chunk metadata overhead:

Profilemin / avg / maxChunks per GiB (approx)Dedup granularityMetadata costWhen to use
Fine4096 / 16384 / 65536~65 536Very high — small edits dedup wellMore redb rows, larger manifests, slower index txnsVersioned text, VM disk deltas
Default8192 / 32768 / 131072~32 768BalancedBalancedGeneral-purpose workloads
Coarse16384 / 131072 / 1048576~8 192Lower — only large identical regions dedupFewest rows, fastest commitsLarge immutable media
Max cap— / — / 16 777 216———max may not exceed 16 MiB (validated at startup)

Effects to expect when moving between profiles:

  • Smaller avg raises the number of chunks-table rows and manifest entries per file; a 10 GiB file at the default 32 KiB average produces roughly 327 680 chunk refs.
  • Larger min reduces pathological tiny-chunk runs (common in highly compressible data) at the cost of missing some short identical regions.
  • max bounds the worst case for both streaming latency and the 8-wide pipeline’s memory per in-flight chunk.

Dedup mechanics

Dedup is bookkeeping on the chunks table, not a data-movement job:

ConceptWhere it livesBehaviour
Storage hashChunkRef.hash / chunks keyThe blob’s name; plaintext BLAKE3, or `BLAKE3(tenant_id
RefcountChunkMeta.refcountIncremented for every manifest reference in the upload’s single index transaction; decremented when GC purges manifests
Dedup reportupload transaction returnOne boolean per chunk: true = already present
Dedup metricsPrometheusfile_storage_dedup_hits_total, file_storage_dedup_bytes_saved_total

The expected dedup ratio follows from boundary stability. With fixed-size chunking, inserting one byte at offset p shifts every boundary after it — a 1 GiB file chunked at 64 KiB loses essentially all 16 384 chunks. With content-defined chunking, the re-synchronising gear hash confines the damage to the chunks overlapping the edit: for random insertions the expected number of disturbed chunks is proportional to the inserted length divided by the average chunk size (plus the two chunks at the seam), so a 4 KB edit into a 32 KiB-average file disturbs on the order of a couple of chunks out of tens of thousands. The measured behaviour matches: re-uploading the identical 512 KiB file yields dedup_chunks = chunk_count, and uniform content collapses to a single chunk per distinct hash.

Under encryption, random AES-GCM nonces make ciphertext-addressed dedup impossible, so the storage key is derived as BLAKE3(tenant_id || plain_hash). Dedup stays exact within a tenant and is impossible across tenants by construction — the details and security reasoning are in ADR 0007.

Configuration

KeyDefaultConstraint
chunking.min8192min ≤ avg
chunking.avg32768target expectation
chunking.max131072avg ≤ max ≤ 16777216 (16 MiB)

Override at startup, for example FS_CHUNKING__AVG=65536. There is no hot reload; changing chunking affects only new uploads (existing manifests keep their recorded chunk hashes, so reads are unaffected).

Examples

The upload response reports dedup inline:

$ curl -s -X POST "localhost:8080/api/v1/files?name=daily.tar" \
       -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" -H 'Content-Type: application/octet-stream' \
       --data-binary @daily.tar
{
  "chunk_count": 2048,
  "dedup_chunks": 1984,
  "dedup_bytes": 63963136,
  "size": 67108864,
  "size_stored": 3145728,
  "content_hash": "…"
}

Here 1984 of 2048 chunks were already present — a typical nightly backup that changed ~3% of its blocks. The 64 unused bytes-per-chunk gap between size and size_stored reflects the 12 new chunks plus compression of the rest.

Verify chunk stability yourself from Rust with the synchronous helper used by the tests and benchmarks:

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
use file_storage::core::chunker::chunk_slice;

let cfg = ChunkingConfig { min: 8192, avg: 32768, max: 131072 };
let a = chunk_slice(&data, &cfg);                  // one pass
let b = chunk_slice(&data_with_append, &cfg);      // appended 100 bytes
// all chunks before the tail region keep identical hashes
}

  • ADR 0002 — FastCDC over fixed chunking — the decision record
  • ADR 0007 — Encrypted dedup key derivation — dedup under encryption
  • Storage Layout — the chunks table and refcount encodings
  • Data Flow — where chunking sits in the upload pipeline
  • Benchmarks — measured chunking and pipeline throughput

Request Validation Contract

Status: normative since V5 Wave 2 (audit finding M-04). Enforced by CI rule no-handler-validation (.github/workflows/ci.yml + scripts/check_no_handler_validation.sh).

The rule

There is exactly one place that decides whether an upload request is well-formed: src/core/validation.rs. REST and gRPC handlers reduce to three steps:

  1. Extract fields from the wire type (Request<Body> query/multipart fields, or the gRPC UploadHeader) into an UploadRequestView.
  2. Validate by calling validation::validate_upload(&view, &limits). No handler contains validation logic — only this call.
  3. Process by handing the already-validated fields to the upload pipeline (whose head separately enforces magic-byte/MIME content checks — audit finding H-03 — and the size ceiling during streaming).

Transport-level code that appears to need a new validation rule is a design smell: the rule belongs in core::validation, where both transports inherit it automatically. CI fails the build if validation logic (validate_name|validate_tags|validate_mime|check_size|len() </> NNN) re-appears under src/api/ or src/grpc/services/.

The rules themselves

#RuleCloses
1name: 1..=255 bytes after trim; C0 (0x00-0x1F) and C1 (0x7F-0x9F) control characters rejected. V4 §5.5 says “chars”; the byte cap is the conservative encoding-safe superset (chars ≤ bytes).M-04b
2tags: at most 32 tags, each 1..=64 bytes after trim, no control characters.L-06
3mime: at most 128 bytes, no control characters. Content vs. magic-byte parity is the pipeline head’s job (H-03).M-04 helpers
4expected_size, when present and non-zero: ≤ limits.max_file_size. Checked before admission control on both transports, so an over-cap declaration is a clean 400 Bad Request / INVALID_ARGUMENT, never a budget 413 / RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED. REST gained the optional ?expected_size= query param in the same move.M-04e
5include_deleted=true requires the admin role — refused with 403 Forbidden / PERMISSION_DENIED on both transports (no silent coercion).M-04a
6Chunk lists served by read endpoints are capped at 1000 entries with an explicit chunks_truncated flag (REST meta DTO and gRPC GetManifest); replication transfers are never truncated.M-04c
7List total values come from one shared implementation
(IndexDb::list_filtered), so REST and gRPC totals cannot drift.M-04d

Error mapping parity

Validation failureRESTgRPC
name / tags / mime / expected_size400 Bad Request (validation_failed)INVALID_ARGUMENT
include_deleted without admin403 Forbidden (forbidden)PERMISSION_DENIED

The mapping is the standard StorageError table (error.rs::status_code, grpc::proto_auth::status_from_error); the validation layer only produces Validation and Forbidden variants.

What does NOT live here

  • Magic-byte / declared-MIME content checks — core::validator (FileValidator::validate_head), run by the pipeline head (H-03).
  • Size ceilings during streaming — FileValidator::validate_size plus the H-01 UploadGate byte budget.
  • AuthN / authZ — security::auth and the middleware/interceptors.
  • Cross-tenant access — api::middleware::tenant.

Regression gates

tests/regression/m04_validation.rs pins the contract:

  • parity_validation_matrix — one table, both transports, identical verdicts for six malformed-input classes.
  • viewers_cannot_list_deleted_both_transports — 403 / PERMISSION_DENIED parity, admins unaffected.
  • list_total_matches_rest — same total from both transports, page smaller than the match set.
  • rest_accepts_expected_size_query — accepted in-range, 400 over-cap.
  • getmanifest_truncates_chunks — 1000-entry cap + chunks_truncated.

Caching

This page documents the three-tier cache hierarchy — what each tier stores, how moka weighs and evicts entries, how read-ahead warms the tiers, and how hit rates are measured. Developers tuning read performance or reasoning about memory budgets should read it.

Overview

The read path consults three independent moka caches before touching disk:

TierValue shapeDefault budgetTTLBacked by
L1Plaintext chunk bytes, ready to stream256 MiB (cache.l1_max_bytes = 268435456)300 s (l1_ttl_secs)moka::future::Cache, byte weigher
L2Stored blobs (compressed / encrypted), ready to decode512 MiB (cache.l2_max_bytes = 536870912)3600 s (l2_ttl_secs)moka::future::Cache, byte weigher
L3Arc<Manifest> descriptors10 000 entries (manifest_capacity)60 s (manifest_ttl_secs)moka::future::Cache, entry-count cap

All three are built once at startup in src/cache/ and shared through AppState by both the REST and gRPC listeners. The measured difference is substantial: full downloads run at 399 MB/s cold (disk → verify → decompress) versus 1107 MB/s warm on L1 plaintext hits, and an in-process 2 MiB retrieval through the cache hierarchy costs about 29 µs.

flowchart LR
    R["RetrievalEngine.load_plain"] --> L1{"L1 plaintext<br/>256 MiB / 300 s"}
    L1 -- hit --> OUT["stream to client"]
    L1 -- miss --> L2{"L2 stored blob<br/>512 MiB / 3600 s"}
    L2 -- hit --> DEC["decrypt -> decompress<br/>-> BLAKE3 verify -> insert L1"]
    L2 -- miss --> DISK["disk: ab/cd/&lt;hash&gt;.bin<br/>(replica: pull-repair from upstream first)"]
    DISK --> DEC
    DEC --> OUT
    M["manifest lookup"] --> L3{"L3 manifests<br/>10k entries / 60 s"}
    L3 -- miss --> REDB["redb files table"]
    REDB --> L3

Figure 1: The read path through the cache tiers. L1 hits return immediately; L2 hits pay decode once; disk loads are always verified before insertion.

How it works

Byte weighers and eviction

L1 and L2 use byte weighers — each entry contributes value.len() (min 1) towards its tier’s max_capacity, so a 256 MiB L1 holds a mix of chunk sizes rather than a fixed entry count. moka 0.12 (future feature) provides concurrent access with TinyLFU-style admission and TTL expiry; expired or evicted entries free their weighed bytes. L3 caps by entry count instead, because manifests vary little in size and the working set is “currently interesting files” rather than bytes.

Two details of the load path matter for correctness:

  • Single-flight loads. L2 lookups use try_get_with, so concurrent requests for the same blob trigger exactly one disk load; the winner’s result is shared. Errors are propagated, never cached.
  • Verified insertion only. A plaintext chunk enters L1 only after BLAKE3 verification against plain_hash succeeds on the L2/disk path. A mismatch aborts with HashMismatch/Corruption (HTTP 500, gRPC DATA_LOSS) and increments file_storage_hash_mismatch_total.

Read-ahead (N+1..N+4)

While streaming a file, the retrieval engine fires background prefetch tasks for chunks N+1 through N+4, guarded by a 10-second timeout and skipped when peek_blob shows the blob is already in L2. Prefetch warms L2 only: it loads the stored blob bytes but deliberately leaves decryption and decompression on the hot path, so an encrypted workload does not pay decode work for chunks the client may never reach. Range requests prefetch within the sliced chunk list using the same window.

Hit-rate accounting

Cache hits are counted in the Prometheus metric file_storage_cache_hits_total{level} with labels l1 and l2; download duration is histogrammed by status and source in file_storage_download_duration_seconds{status,source}. The L3 manifest tier has no dedicated hit counter — its effectiveness shows up as lower GET /files/:id/meta latency and in the diagnostic stats exposed by the retrieval engine (L1/L2 entry counts and weighted sizes after run_pending_tasks). See Monitoring for dashboard guidance.

ETag / 304 is a separate layer

HTTP conditional requests are handled by the HTTP cache layer in src/cache/http_cache.rs, which is logically independent of the byte caches:

  • The ETag is a strong validator: the quoted, 64-character lowercase-hex BLAKE3 digest of the original file content, so it is stable across chunking config changes and identical across replicas.
  • If-None-Match is evaluated with full RFC semantics: *, comma-separated lists, quoted and unquoted forms, and weak W/"…" tags matched by opaque value. A match short-circuits to 304 Not Modified with ETag and Cache-Control: private, max-age=3600 — no manifest chunk loads, no cache tiers touched beyond L3.
  • Successful downloads (200/206) also carry ETag, Cache-Control, Content-Disposition, and X-Content-Hash (the unquoted hash).

Because content is immutable once stored (same-name replacement creates a new file_id), a 304 is always safe for the lifetime of the manifest.

Configuration

KeyDefaultNotes
cache.l1_max_bytes268435456 (256 MiB)Plaintext chunk budget; raise for read-heavy, dedup-light workloads
cache.l1_ttl_secs300Short TTL keeps plaintext reclaims prompt
cache.l2_max_bytes536870912 (512 MiB)Blob budget; the read-ahead target tier
cache.l2_ttl_secs3600Blobs are immutable, so a long TTL is safe
cache.manifest_capacity10000L3 entry cap
cache.manifest_ttl_secs60Deliberately short so soft-deletes propagate quickly

Defaults total up to ~768 MiB of cache memory plus manifest storage; size your container accordingly. Overrides use the FS_CACHE__* environment convention (for example FS_CACHE__L2_MAX_BYTES=1073741824). Guidance on matching cache budgets to workload is in Tuning. GC deletes and same-name replacement invalidate affected entries explicitly, but replicas that learn about deletes lazily rely on the 60 s manifest TTL.

Examples

Reading a range across a cold cache exercises every tier once (see the sequence diagram in Data Flow); the second request to the same bytes should hit L1. Watch it happen:

# before: baseline
curl -s localhost:8080/metrics | grep cache_hits_total
# file_storage_cache_hits_total{level="l1"} 1042
# file_storage_cache_hits_total{level="l2"} 87

# request the same 1 MiB range twice
curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{time_total}\n' -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
     -H 'Range: bytes=0-1048575' localhost:8080/api/v1/files/<id>/range
curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{time_total}\n' -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
     -H 'Range: bytes=0-1048575' localhost:8080/api/v1/files/<id>/range

# after: l1 counter grows by one per served chunk
curl -s localhost:8080/metrics | grep cache_hits_total

A conditional download that stays cold:

ETAG=$(curl -s -D- -o /dev/null -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
       localhost:8080/api/v1/files/<id> | tr -d '\r' | awk -F'"' '/^[Ee][Tt]ag:/{print $2}')
curl -s -D- -o /dev/null -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
     -H "If-None-Match: \"$ETAG\"" localhost:8080/api/v1/files/<id>   # -> 304

  • Chunking — why chunk sizes determine cache-entry shapes
  • Data Flow — the retrieval sequence diagrams with cache misses
  • Storage Layout — the blobs and manifests being cached
  • Benchmarks — cold/warm download and range-slice numbers
  • Tuning — cache sizing recipes
  • Monitoring — cache and download metrics in dashboards

Replication

This page describes the WAL-style replication shipper, its queue and backpressure behaviour, the sync/async consistency models, the two internal transports, and how replicas repair themselves on read misses. Architects designing multi-node deployments and operators running replicas should read it; day-2 procedures live in the Replication Ops guide.

Overview

A primary streams operations to exactly one replica target through an in-memory, bounded queue drained by a background worker. Ops are idempotent and strictly ordered per file (chunk blobs first, then the manifest), which lets the replica apply them safely even after replays. Replicas can additionally pull missing blobs on demand from the primary when a read hits a gap, and run a reachability-mode GC instead of refcount GC. Replication is disabled by default; enabling it requires replication.enabled: true plus a target_url and shared api_key.

How it works

The queue and worker

The Replicator (in src/replication/replicator.rs) owns an mpsc channel of capacity replication.queue_capacity (default 10 000). Upload paths send ops into it; when the queue is full, send awaits a slot — backpressure propagates to uploads rather than dropping ops. A single worker task drains the channel:

ParameterDefaultBehaviour
queue_capacity10000Bounded in-memory queue; senders backpressure when full
batch_size64Ops shipped per batch (minimum internal drain of 8)
flush_interval_ms5000Maximum linger before a partial batch is flushed
Backoff100 ms → 500 ms → 2 sExponential on upstream failure, then held at 2 s until recovery

The queue holds four op kinds: Chunk (a full blob payload with hashes, sizes, flags), Manifest (a file upsert), Purge (hard-delete propagation), and Flush — a sentinel carrying a oneshot reply that implements the sync-mode barrier.

Op ordering and idempotency

The upload pipeline enqueues each chunk payload before the manifest op, and the channel preserves order, so a replica never sees a manifest referencing blobs it has not received. Ops are idempotent by construction: put_chunk writes a content-addressed blob (a repeated write is a no-op), put_manifest upserts by file_id, and delete_file with purge=true removes the manifest. Replays after a crash or timeout are therefore safe, and the replica can acknowledge batches without deduplication logic.

Consistency model

  • Async mode (default): uploads succeed as soon as the local index transaction commits; ops ship within one batch window. The model is eventual consistency: a crash on the primary loses the un-flushed tail of the queue — a bounded window of at most queue_capacity ops, typically the last ≤5 seconds of writes. In-memory queueing is a deliberate trade (see ADR 0006); the replica’s pull-repair and reachability GC reconcile the residue.
  • Sync mode (replication.mode: sync): the upload blocks in wait_flushed until the worker has shipped a batch containing the file’s ops and the replica acknowledged it, bounded by sync_timeout_ms (default 5000). A timeout surfaces as Unavailable (HTTP 503 service_unavailable, gRPC UNAVAILABLE), giving acked durability at the cost of upload latency.

Metrics make queue health visible: file_storage_replication_queue_depth (gauge), file_storage_replication_failures_total, file_storage_replication_ops_applied_total, and file_storage_replication_flush_seconds{status}.

Purge propagation and replica GC

When GC on the primary purges a past-grace tombstone, it enqueues a Purge op, so hard deletes propagate to the replica instead of lingering as orphans. Replicas are expected to run retention.gc_mode: reachability: live manifests define the reachable blob set, expired tombstones are dropped, and any blob outside the reachable set (and older than orphan_min_age_secs) is deleted — no refcounts required.

Replica pull-repair

A replica configured with replication.upstream_url heals read gaps on demand: when a disk read misses a blob, the retrieval engine fetches it from the upstream primary (REST GET /internal/chunk/:hash or gRPC PullChunk), writes it locally, and serves the read. Admin resync (POST /api/v1/admin/resync/:id, gRPC Resync) pushes a full file bundle proactively.

The two transports

REST /internal/*gRPC ReplicationService
AuthX-Api-Key headerx-api-key metadata
Batch sendPOST /internal/batch with the binary frame codecReplicateBatch — bidi stream of ReplicationOp{put_chunk | put_manifest | delete_file | flush} → ReplicationAck{ops_acked, success, error}
Blob fetchGET /internal/chunk/:hashPullChunk — header then data pieces of ≤64 KiB
Manifest fetchGET /internal/file/:idPullManifest → ManifestBundle{manifest}
Used byThe built-in WAL shipper and its client codecsExternal integrations and custom shippers

The frame codec (in src/replication/client.rs) is compact and self-describing per batch: a u32 item count followed by hash(32) | plain_hash(32) | u64 size_original | u64 size_stored | u8 flags (bit0 compressed, bit1 encrypted) | u32 blob_len | blob for each chunk. Manifests and file bundles use MessagePack framed by a u32 length prefix.

sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    participant C as Client
    participant P as Primary (upload path)
    participant Q as Replicator queue (cap 10000)
    participant W as Worker task
    participant R as Replica

    C->>P: POST /api/v1/files
    P->>P: chunk, hash, compress, encrypt, blob write
    P->>P: ONE redb transaction (manifest + refcounts)
    P->>Q: enqueue Chunk ops (one per new/existing blob)
    P->>Q: enqueue Manifest op
    P-->>C: 201 Created (async mode returns here)
    Q->>W: drain up to batch_size=64 ops or 5000 ms linger
    W->>R: POST /internal/batch (frames: chunks first, then manifest)
    R->>R: write blobs (idempotent), upsert manifest
    R-->>W: 200 OK
    W->>Q: mark batch flushed (resumes sync waiters)
    alt replication.mode = sync
        Note over C,P: upload waits in wait_flushed until this ack, bounded by sync_timeout_ms
    end

Figure 1: A replicated upload in async mode. On failure the worker retries the same batch after the 100 ms → 500 ms → 2 s backoff ladder.

Configuration

KeyDefaultNotes
replication.enabledfalseMaster switch; also gates /internal/* acceptance on replicas
replication.modeasyncsync blocks uploads until flush ack
replication.target_urlnullPrimary → replica shipping endpoint (e.g. http://replica:8080)
replication.upstream_urlnullReplica → primary pull-repair endpoint
replication.api_key—Shared secret for both X-Api-Key and x-api-key
replication.queue_capacity10000In-memory; raise cautiously (memory)
replication.batch_size64Ops per batch
replication.flush_interval_ms5000Max linger of a partial batch
replication.sync_timeout_ms5000Sync-mode barrier budget

Environment overrides: FS_REPLICATION__ENABLED=true, FS_REPLICATION__TARGET_URL=http://replica:8080, and so on. Internal traffic is plain HTTP by design; terminate TLS at your ingress, or front the gRPC listener with server.grpc_tls (mTLS via client_ca_path is recommended for node-to-node links).

Examples

Minimal primary + replica compose topology (the repository’s docker-compose.yml ships this as profiles — primary :8080/:9090, replica :8081/:9091, Prometheus :9099):

# primary
replication:
  enabled: true
  mode: async
  target_url: "http://replica:8080"
  api_key: "change-me-internal"

# replica
retention:
  gc_mode: reachability
replication:
  upstream_url: "http://primary:8080"
  api_key: "change-me-internal"

Check shipping health from the metrics endpoint:

curl -s localhost:8080/metrics | grep replication

# file_storage_replication_ops_applied_total 14213
# file_storage_replication_flush_seconds_count{status="ok"} 218

A manual resync of one file (admin):

     localhost:8080/api/v1/admin/resync/<file_id>
# {"file_id":"…","replicated":true}

Garbage Collection

This page explains the file lifecycle from soft delete through the grace period to purge, the two collection modes (refcount on primaries, reachability on replicas), the orphan sweeper, and the admin triggers. Operators scheduling maintenance and planning capacity should read it alongside the GC operations runbook.

Overview

Deletes in file-storage are tombstones: DELETE /api/v1/files/:id sets deleted_at_ms on the manifest and returns 204 immediately. Nothing is reclaimed at delete time — shared blobs may still be referenced by other files, and the grace period (default retention.grace_period_secs = 86400, i.e. 24 hours) is the undo window. A background collector wakes every gc_interval_secs (default 300) and reclaims space according to the configured mode. Every pass is bounded to 5 000 work items, and every reclaimed byte is reported in a GcReport{manifests_purged, chunks_removed, orphans_removed, bytes_reclaimed}.

flowchart TD
    A["DELETE /api/v1/files/:id<br/>(or same-name replacement of old manifest)"] --> B["soft delete:<br/>manifest.deleted_at_ms = now — tombstone"]
    B --> C{"grace period elapsed?<br/>deleted_at_ms &lt; now - grace_period_secs"}
    C -- "no" --> D["file stays recoverable<br/>(reads: 404; include_deleted listing: admin only)"]
    C -- "yes (GC pass)" --> E["purge: remove manifest,<br/>decrement chunk refcounts"]
    E --> F{"refcount reaches 0?"}
    F -- "yes" --> G["delete blob ab/cd/&lt;hash&gt;.bin<br/>bytes_reclaimed += blob_size<br/>enqueue Purge op to replica"]
    F -- "no" --> H["blob stays for remaining manifests"]
    I["orphan sweep (refcount mode)"] --> J{"blob on disk, not in chunks table,<br/>mtime older than orphan_min_age_secs?"}
    J -- "yes" --> G
    J -- "no" --> K["keep (may be mid-upload: blob-before-index)"]

Figure 1: The soft-delete lifecycle and the two reclamation paths (tombstone purge and orphan sweep). GC runs both in refcount mode; replicas run reachability instead.

How it works

Refcount mode (primaries)

retention.gc_mode: refcount is the default and the right mode wherever the full chunks table is authoritative (primaries, and any replica that applies refcount-affecting ops). Each pass performs two steps:

  1. Purge past-grace tombstones. Scan manifests for deleted_at_ms < now − grace_period_secs, up to the 5 000-item batch limit. For each: remove the manifest, decrement every chunk’s refcount, and delete blobs whose refcount reaches 0 (adding their blob_size to bytes_reclaimed). A purge is also enqueued to the replica so hard deletes propagate.
  2. Orphan sweep against the index. Load all chunk hashes from redb, list every blob on disk, and delete blobs that are on disk but absent from the index — but only if their modification time is older than retention.orphan_min_age_secs (default 3600). The age guard is what makes the blob-before-index write order safe: a blob being written right now is not yet in the index and must not be swept.

Refcounts explain why blobs are never deleted eagerly: a chunk shared by a live file and a tombstoned file keeps its blob until every referencing manifest is purged. Same-name replacement relies on this — the old manifest is soft-deleted while its chunks stay alive.

Reachability mode (replicas)

retention.gc_mode: reachability is designed for replicas, which apply replicated ops and pull-repaired blobs without maintaining authoritative refcounts. A pass builds the reachable set — the union of chunk hashes across all live manifests — after first purging expired tombstones (same grace-period rule). Any blob outside the reachable set and older than orphan_min_age_secs is deleted, again bounded by the 5 000-item limit. This mode has no refcount bookkeeping at all; correctness rests on the primary having shipped all Purge ops and the grace period having elapsed.

Disabled mode

retention.gc_mode: disabled turns the background loop into a no-op: tombstones accumulate, refcounts are never decremented, and orphans persist. Storage grows monotonically. This mode exists for controlled environments (snapshot-and-restore testing, migrations); a manual trigger can still run an explicit pass.

Batching, scheduling, and observability

  • The batch limit is 5 000 items per pass (manifest purges and orphan deletions each), so a backlog after a large delete storm drains over several ticks instead of blocking one long pass.
  • The background loop runs every gc_interval_secs (default 300), implemented as a tokio interval with missed ticks delayed — a slow pass never stacks up concurrent passes.
  • Metrics: file_storage_gc_files_purged_total, file_storage_gc_bytes_reclaimed_total, file_storage_gc_orphans_removed_total. Each pass also records an audit event with action storage.gc, visible to admins via GET /api/v1/audit.
  • A pass that reclaimed nothing is free — the sweep is two index scans plus a directory walk.

Configuration

KeyDefaultMeaning
retention.gc_moderefcountrefcount | reachability | disabled
retention.grace_period_secs86400Tombstone age before purge (24 h undo window)
retention.gc_interval_secs300Background loop cadence (minimum 1 s)
retention.orphan_min_age_secs3600Minimum blob age before an orphan may be swept

There is no hot reload; mode changes apply at restart.

Examples

Manual trigger over REST (admin):

curl -s -X POST -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" localhost:8080/api/v1/admin/gc
{
  "manifests_purged": 12,
  "chunks_removed": 340,
  "orphans_removed": 2,
  "bytes_reclaimed": 11042304
}

Manual trigger over gRPC — note that the dry_run flag of AdminService/TriggerGC is currently not supported and responds UNIMPLEMENTED; run real passes instead:

grpcurl -plaintext -H "authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  -d '{"mode": "REFCOUNT"}' \
  localhost:9090 file_storage.v1.AdminService/TriggerGC

# dry_run is rejected rather than silently ignored:
grpcurl -plaintext -d '{"mode": "REFCOUNT", "dry_run": true}' \
  localhost:9090 file_storage.v1.AdminService/TriggerGC
# grpc-status 12 (UNIMPLEMENTED)

Omitting mode (or sending MODE_UNSPECIFIED) uses the node’s configured mode; explicitly passing REFCOUNT or REACHABILITY overrides it for that single pass, which is useful for draining a replica with a one-off refcount sweep.

Watch a pass happen:

curl -s localhost:8080/metrics | grep gc_
# file_storage_gc_files_purged_total 12
# file_storage_gc_bytes_reclaimed_total 11042304
# file_storage_gc_orphans_removed_total 2

See also

  • GC operations — scheduling, draining backlogs, and emergency purges
  • Storage Layout — refcount and blob_size encodings being manipulated
  • Replication — purge propagation and replica reachability GC
  • Data Flow — the blob-before-index ordering the orphan guard protects
  • REST API — DELETE /files/:id?purge= and POST /admin/gc semantics
  • Monitoring — GC metrics and alerting suggestions

Security

This page is the architecture-level summary of the security model: authentication (JWT + refresh tokens), password hashing, per-tenant encryption at rest, tenant isolation, the audit trail, and transport TLS. Security engineers should read it first and then continue into the dedicated pages of the Security section for the threat model and hardening checklist.

Overview

Security is layered at four boundaries. Authentication verifies humans: users are defined in configuration, passwords are hashed with argon2id, and login issues a short-lived HS256 access token plus a rotating refresh token. Authorization scopes every request to a tenant, enforced by middleware on REST and interceptors/in-handler checks on gRPC, with a role ladder admin > user (write) > viewer (read). Data protection encrypts blobs at rest with per-tenant AES-256-GCM keys stored as 0600 files, and derives tenant-scoped storage keys so dedup never leaks cross-tenant equality. Accountability records audit events into an append-only redb table.

How it works

Authentication: JWT HS256 + rotating refresh tokens

POST /api/v1/auth/login (or AuthService/Login) verifies the password and returns:

{
  "access_token": "eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiIs…",
  "refresh_token": "opaque-random-128-bit",
  "token_type": "Bearer",
  "expires_in": 900
}
  • Access tokens are JWTs signed HS256 with auth.jwt_secret (must be ≥16 chars) and carry claims sub (username), tenant (tenant UUID), role, iat, exp, jti. Default lifetime: auth.access_ttl_secs = 900 (15 minutes).
  • Refresh tokens are opaque random values held in memory (never persisted), single-use, with TTL auth.refresh_ttl_secs = 604800 (7 days). POST /auth/refresh rotates the pair and revokes the old token; POST /auth/logout is idempotent.
  • Password hashing uses argon2id via PHC strings in config (password_hash), generated with file-storage hash-password. Unknown usernames still burn a full argon2 verification against a dummy hash, so login timing does not enumerate users.
  • gRPC auth: unary interceptors parse authorization: Bearer <jwt> into an AuthContext; streaming RPCs (Upload, Download) verify the token in-handler, because tonic interceptors only run for unary calls. Metadata is the same shape as REST headers: authorization, x-api-key, x-request-id (propagated or minted UUIDv7).
sequenceDiagram
    autonumber
    participant C as Client
    participant A as AuthService
    participant M as Middleware / interceptor
    participant S as Storage services

    C->>A: Login {username, password}
    A->>A: argon2id verify (timing-equalized for unknown users)
    A-->>C: access_token (JWT, 900 s) + refresh_token (7 d, single-use)
    C->>M: request with authorization: Bearer &lt;jwt&gt;
    M->>M: verify HS256, check exp, extract tenant + role
    M->>S: call with AuthContext {username, tenant_id, role}
    S->>S: role check (admin > user > viewer) + tenant scope
    C->>A: Refresh {refresh_token}
    A-->>C: new pair; old refresh token revoked

Figure 1: Token issuance and per-request verification. The same AuthContext reaches both the REST middleware chain and the gRPC service handlers.

Tenant isolation

Isolation is enforced at three depths, so no single bug exposes another tenant’s data:

  1. Request scope — every JWT binds one tenant; user and viewer roles can only see their tenant’s files. Cross-tenant access requires admin.
  2. Index scoping — the name_index and tag_index redb tables are tenant-prefixed (tenant(16B) || 0x00 || …), so a listing query physically scans only the tenant’s byte range.
  3. Encryption scoping — with encryption enabled, blob storage keys are BLAKE3(tenant_id || plain_hash) (see ADR 0007), so even dedup equality is invisible across tenants.

Encryption at rest

Per-tenant AES-256-GCM with 32-byte keys (see ADR 0005):

  • Blob layout is self-describing: nonce(12) || ciphertext || tag(16), a fresh random 96-bit nonce per encryption.
  • Keys live as hex-encoded files at <encryption.keys_dir>/<tenant>.key (default data/keys), created with 0600 permissions and cached in memory; a permissions check (key_file_permissions_ok) verifies owner-only access on Unix.
  • Chunks carry explicit encrypted and compressed flags in their refs — decoding never relies on magic-byte sniffing.
  • Key files are never rewritten by the service; a key_age_ms diagnostic exposes the key file’s modification time so operators can monitor key age. Rotation is currently an out-of-band procedure (re-encrypt blobs externally); KMS/envelope encryption is documented future work.

Details, key-management guidance, and the file-permissions checklist live in Encryption at Rest.

Audit trail

Every consequential action appends a JSON AuditEvent to the redb audit table, keyed by UUIDv7 so the log is physically time-ordered. Actions: file.upload, file.download, file.delete, file.purge, auth.login.success, auth.login.failure, tenant.create, replication.apply, storage.gc, replication.resync. Each event records id, ts_ms, actor (system or a username), tenant_id, object, ip, status, and detail. Admins read it via GET /api/v1/audit?limit= or AdminService/QueryAudit (which also filters by tenant). See Audit Log.

Transport and request hygiene

  • gRPC TLS: server.grpc_tls enables TLS on the :9090 listener (cert_path, key_path); setting client_ca_path turns on mTLS with client certificates — recommended for node-to-node replication links. Deployment details in TLS.
  • REST TLS: the service intentionally ships plain HTTP on :8080 (internal traffic); terminate TLS at your ingress or load balancer for external exposure.
  • Rate limiting: REST via tower_governor keyed by client IP (rate_limit.per_second 500, burst 1000); gRPC via a per-tenant token bucket with the same knobs, disabled per tenant if unset. Streaming RPCs are deliberately not rate limited (documented behaviour).
  • Internal endpoints: /internal/* and the gRPC ReplicationService are guarded by the shared api_key, not JWTs — they authenticate nodes, not users.

Known limits

The honest list: static users from config (no user management API), in-memory refresh tokens (restart logs everyone out), no KMS integration, no per-user quota enforcement (limits.max_file_size and max_body are the only size controls), and streaming RPCs bypass the rate limiter. These are tracked in the Threat Model.

Configuration

KeyDefaultNotes
auth.jwt_secret—≥16 chars; rotating it invalidates all access tokens
auth.access_ttl_secs900Access-token lifetime
auth.refresh_ttl_secs604800Refresh-token lifetime (7 days)
auth.users[]dev fixturesusername + password (dev) or password_hash (PHC argon2id), tenant_id, role
encryption.enabledfalseEnables the AES-GCM pipeline stage and tenant-scoped storage keys
encryption.keys_dirdata/keysPer-tenant key files, created 0600
server.grpc_tls.*disabledenabled, cert_path, key_path, client_ca_path (mTLS)
rate_limit.*500 / 1000Token-bucket rate and burst

Secrets handling (including FS_AUTH__JWT_SECRET via environment) is covered in Secrets; the production template with an argon2id hash is config/production.yaml.

Examples

Generate a password hash and configure a user:

file-storage hash-password --password 'correct-horse-battery'
# $argon2id$v=19$m=19456,t=2,p=1$…PHC string…

auth:
  users:
    - username: alice
      password_hash: "$argon2id$v=19$m=19456,t=2,p=1$…"
      tenant_id: "01930000-0000-7000-8000-000000000002"
      role: user

Confirm tenant isolation from a client’s point of view:

# bob (tenant …003) cannot read alice's (tenant …002) file:
curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $BOB_TOKEN" localhost:8080/api/v1/files/<alice_file_id>
# {"error":{"code":"forbidden","message":"…"}}

# an admin can cross tenants explicitly via admin-only listings
curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN_TOKEN" \
     "localhost:8080/api/v1/files?include_deleted=true"

Inspect key-file hygiene on the host:

ls -l data/keys/
# -rw------- 1 app app 64 Nov 10 09:14 01930000-0000-7000-8000-000000000002.key
