ADR 0001: Content-addressed storage over a block store

This decision record explains why every chunk is stored as its own immutable, hash-named blob rather than inside per-file block containers. It is the foundation decision of the storage engine; chunking (ADR 0002) and the metadata design build directly on it.

Overview

The block store is a flat map from 32-byte hash to blob bytes on the local filesystem, sharded by hash prefix. Nothing else — no per-file grouping, no offsets, no headers — lives in the blob layer; all structure lives in the redb index. Choosing content addressing made dedup, integrity checking, and replication consequences of the design rather than features bolted on top.

Status

Accepted — 2026-11-10.

Context

The service is a hybrid chunked store: files are split into content-defined chunks (32 KiB average), and the dominant workloads are duplicate-heavy (repeated uploads, nightly backups, version churn, many similar VM images). The blob layer had to satisfy, in combination:

  • Cross-file dedup — identical chunks appearing in different files (or different versions of one file) must occupy storage once.
  • End-to-end integrity — bit rot, partial writes, and replication bugs must be detectable without external tooling.
  • Crash safety — a kill at any instant must never leave the system referring to data that was never durably written.
  • Simple replication — the replica must be able to converge with the primary without a locking protocol.

Decision

Chunks are stored as individual immutable blobs keyed by their content hash, at <storage.root>/<ab>/<cd>/<64-hex>.bin (shard depth configurable 0–4, default 2). Writing is atomic — unique temp file, fsync, rename, directory fsync — and idempotent: writing an existing hash returns false without modifying anything. The index side records per-hash metadata (refcount, blob_size, on_disk_path) in the redb chunks table, and manifests reference blobs purely by hash.

How it works

  • src/storage/block_store.rs implements write / read / delete / list_all over the sharded tree; list_all walks to shard_depth + 1 levels for the orphan sweeper.
  • The upload pipeline in src/core/pipeline.rs writes every blob before the single index transaction that registers its refcount, so a crash leaves at most an orphan blob, never a dangling reference.
  • The retrieval engine (src/storage/retrieval.rs) re-hashes every disk-loaded chunk with BLAKE3 and compares it to the manifest’s plain_hash; a mismatch aborts the read with HashMismatch/Corruption and increments file_storage_hash_mismatch_total.
  • Replication ships blobs by hash; a replica missing a blob simply pulls it from upstream by hash. Because blobs are immutable, there is nothing to invalidate.

Consequences

Positive

  • Global dedup for free: identical plaintext chunks (or tenant-scoped derivations under encryption, per ADR 0007) collapse to one blob and a refcount bump.
  • Integrity self-check: the blob’s name is its content, so verification needs no checksum sidecar — read back, hash, compare.
  • Immutable blobs: caches never need invalidation, replication is append-only, and same-name replacement never rewrites bytes.
  • Simple garbage collection: refcount to zero means delete; anything on disk without an index row is an orphan.
  • Idempotent, replay-safe writes: concurrent or repeated writes of one hash converge.

Negative

  • One small file per chunk: hundreds of thousands of inodes for large datasets, mitigated by sharding (storage.shard_depth) and bounded by the chunk-size floor.
  • No partial blob updates or compaction: storage changes only through create/delete; reorganising data means re-writing blobs through the pipeline.
  • GC must exist: refcounting plus an orphan sweep is real machinery (Garbage Collection), not free.
  • fsync on the hot write path: durability costs latency (measured as the dominant cost in the 119 MB/s HTTP upload figure on the benchmark rig).

Alternatives considered

  • Per-file block containers (one file per stored file, chunked internally with local offsets). Chosen against because identical chunks across files would duplicate storage unless a shared container format were invented; containers also make same-name replacement a rewrite of a large file, and replication would need container-level delta logic instead of shipping blobs by hash.
  • External object store (S3 and friends) as the blob layer. Adds a network dependency and per-request latency to a component that is local by design; also breaks the single-process, single-volume deployment model. Rejected for the core; a bolt-on remote tier remains a possible future backend.
  • Key-value store for blob bytes (blob bytes inside redb or another KV). Mixes large-value IO with metadata IO in one file, hurting both; keeps the atomicity story murkier. Rejected.

Usage

Operational knobs for this decision:

KeyDefaultEffect
storage.rootdata/blocksRoot of the blob tree
storage.shard_depth2Directory fan-out (0–4)
storage.temp_dirdata/tmpSpool for multipart bodies (blobs themselves temp inside their shard dir)

Examples

The layout and its idempotency contract:

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
// blob at data/blocks/3f/9a/3f9a4b17….bin — name == BLAKE3 (or tenant-scoped derivation)
let first  = blocks.write(&hash, b"payload").await?; // true  — created
let second = blocks.write(&hash, b"payload").await?; // false — already present
}

Re-uploading the same 512 KiB file therefore reports dedup_chunks = chunk_count while writing zero new blob bytes.

See also

  • Storage Layout — the tree and index encodings in full
  • Chunking & Dedup — how chunk boundaries create the dedup opportunity
  • ADR 0002 — FastCDC — the chunking decision feeding this one
  • ADR 0003 — redb — the metadata side of the split
  • Garbage Collection — how un-referenced blobs are reclaimed
  • Benchmarks — the fsync-dominated upload numbers

ADR 0002: FastCDC over fixed chunking

This decision record explains why the service cuts data with the FastCDC 2020 algorithm instead of fixed-size splits. It is the reason a one-byte edit to a gigabyte file re-uploads kilobytes rather than re-uploading the file.

Overview

Dedup quality is determined before hashing ever runs: it depends on where chunk boundaries land. Fixed-size boundaries are cheap but hostile to edits; content-defined boundaries move with the data. The service uses fastcdc 5.x’s v2020 module (gear-hash based, tokio-aware) with default bounds of 8 KiB / 32 KiB / 128 KiB, achieving a measured ~1.1 GB/s pure chunking rate.

Status

Accepted — 2026-11-10.

Context

The target workloads — backups, VM images, media, repeated uploads — share one property: successive versions differ by small insertions at arbitrary offsets. The chunking layer must therefore:

  • Survive edits — inserting or deleting B bytes must disturb only the local neighbourhood of chunks, not every chunk after the edit point.
  • Be transport-agnostic — the same bytes arriving over different transports (multipart spooling, raw HTTP streaming, gRPC 1 MiB frames) must produce identical chunk sequences, or dedup would depend on packetisation.
  • Stream with bounded memory — chunking happens while the request body is still arriving; nothing may require buffering whole files.
  • Be fast — chunking sits in the hottest part of the upload pipeline ahead of BLAKE3 and zstd.

Decision

Adopt the FastCDC 2020 algorithm via the fastcdc crate (version 5, tokio feature), configured by chunking.min (8192), chunking.avg (32768), chunking.max (131072) — with the validated constraint min ≤ avg ≤ max and a hard cap of 16 MiB on max. The async wrapper chunk_stream in src/core/chunker.rs feeds the body stream through an AsyncRead adapter into fastcdc::v2020::AsyncStreamCDC, which maintains its own rolling carry buffer; the pipeline then processes chunks through the 8-wide concurrent stage.

How it works

  • The gear hash slides a window over the stream; a cut is declared when the rolling hash hits a mask derived from avg, never before min, and forcibly at max.
  • Because AsyncStreamCDC buffers across stream items, cut points depend only on content: unit tests feed the same pseudo-random 2 MB payload in 1000-byte and 250 KB pieces and assert identical BLAKE3 hash sequences (boundaries_independent_of_packetisation), and compare streamed output against the synchronous single-pass scanner (matches_single_pass_scanner).
  • Tests also assert the bounds (respects_min_and_max), exact reassembly at odd packet sizes (997 / 4096 / 65533), single-chunk behaviour for tiny inputs, and empty-input no-ops.
  • Upstream stream errors abort the scanner and surface as the original StorageError variant, recovered from the io::Error boundary.
payload:  |-------A-------||---B---|  insert 4 KB at offset p
fixed 64 KiB:  every boundary after p shifts  ->  (size / 64 KiB) chunks change
FastCDC 32 KiB: re-synchronises near p        ->  ~B/32 KiB + 2 chunks change

Figure 1: Edit locality. The gear hash re-anchors boundaries shortly after the disturbed region, which is what preserves dedup for versioned content.

Consequences

Positive

  • Boundary stability under edits: shifted content dedups at the expected B/avg + 2 disturbed-chunk rate instead of losing everything downstream of an edit.
  • Transport-independent dedup: identical bytes uploaded via multipart, raw HTTP, or gRPC yield identical chunk sets and dedup against each other.
  • ~GB/s throughput: measured 1134 MB/s pure chunking on the benchmark rig (4 MiB input, default bounds); see Benchmarks.
  • Streaming by construction: constant memory regardless of upload size (up to the 10 GiB limit).
  • Tunable: chunking.min/avg/max let operators trade dedup granularity against metadata overhead (Chunking & Dedup has the tuning table).

Negative

  • CPU cost versus memcpy splits: rolling-hash work is real, though hidden behind the compress/encrypt stages’ concurrency.
  • Chunk-size variance: content-defined cuts produce a distribution around avg, so manifests and the chunks table hold more, smaller entries than a fixed scheme at the same average.
  • Dedup is exact-match only: near-duplicate content (recompressed media) dedups poorly; CDC improves edit locality, not fuzzy similarity.
  • Encryption interplay: boundaries dedup fine, but ciphertext-addressed storage is impossible with random nonces — resolved separately in ADR 0007.

Alternatives considered

  • Fixed 64 KiB chunking — trivially fast and simple, but edit-hostile: one inserted byte shifts every subsequent boundary, so versioned files dedup to ~0%. Rejected as the primary scheme.
  • Rabin fingerprinting — the classic content-defined approach; correct but slower (string polynomial rolling hash without the gear/normalisation refinements) and no better at boundary stability than FastCDC. Rejected on throughput.
  • Custom rolling hash — a hand-rolled hash and mask scheme could shave CPU, but it abandons the peer-reviewed algorithm and the maintained, tested crate for marginal gain. Rejected on risk.
  • Larger fixed chunks plus whole-file dedup — a hybrid where identical whole files (matched by original_hash) shortcut to refcount bumps. The pipeline already gets this behaviour for free: every chunk of a duplicate file dedups, and the same-name replacement path soft-deletes the old manifest. No separate mechanism needed.

Usage

KeyDefaultConstraint
chunking.min8192min ≤ avg
chunking.avg32768Target cut distance
chunking.max131072≤ 16777216 (16 MiB hard cap)

Changing the values affects new uploads only; existing manifests reference their already-recorded chunk hashes, so reads are unaffected. Overrides: FS_CHUNKING__MIN=4096 FS_CHUNKING__AVG=16384 FS_CHUNKING__MAX=65536.

Examples

Reproduce the packetisation-independence property outside the test suite:

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
use file_storage::core::chunker::chunk_slice;

let cfg = ChunkingConfig { min: 8192, avg: 32768, max: 131072 };
let one_pass = chunk_slice(&data, &cfg);
// hashing each chunk of `one_pass` equals hashing each chunk of the
// streamed version, regardless of how the body was packetised
}

A typical result on versioned content (from the upload report):

{ "chunk_count": 2048, "dedup_chunks": 1984, "dedup_bytes": 63963136 }

See also

  • Chunking & Dedup — algorithm mechanics, tuning table, and dedup math
  • ADR 0001 — CAS over block store — what chunk hashes address
  • ADR 0007 — Encrypted dedup key derivation — dedup under encryption
  • Data Flow — chunking’s place in the pipeline
  • Tuning — matching chunk profile to workload

ADR 0003: redb over sled

This decision record explains why the metadata index (manifests, chunk refcounts, name/tag indexes, audit log, tenants) is a single redb file rather than sled, SQLite, or RocksDB. The index is the transactional heart of the service, so this choice shapes crash safety and latency.

Overview

The service needs an embedded, ACID, crash-safe key-value store holding six byte-keyed tables, accessed from an async runtime. redb 2.2 was chosen: a single-file, pure-Rust B-tree store with an explicit durability control (Durability::Immediate), an active maintenance record, and a synchronous API that slots cleanly into spawn_blocking.

Status

Accepted — 2026-11-10.

Context

The index must provide:

  • ACID transactions with fsync-able commits — the write path collapses a whole upload’s refcount changes and manifest insert into one transaction; a torn commit must be impossible.
  • Six byte-string-keyed tables — chunks, files, name_index, tag_index, audit, tenants; raw byte keys and values keep encodings (bincode / MessagePack / JSON) explicit per table.
  • Embedded operation — the deployment model is a single process with a data directory; requiring a networked database service contradicts it.
  • Bounded latency tails — background compaction pauses or GC stalls in the write path would show up directly in upload latency.
  • Single-file durability story — WAL-backed writes whose commit survives power loss without external tooling.

Decision

Use redb (2.2) with the database file at database.path (default data/index.redb). Every write transaction sets txn.set_durability(Durability::Immediate) before commit — WAL plus fsync. All blocking redb work runs on tokio’s blocking pool behind async wrappers (IndexDb::write/read), and table guards are always scoped before mutating the same table, since redb borrows are exclusive. Keys and values are raw bytes; serialisation is bincode for ChunkMeta, MessagePack for Manifest, JSON for audit events and tenants.

How it works

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
const T_CHUNKS: TableDefinition<&[u8], &[u8]> = TableDefinition::new("chunks");
// ... files, name_index, tag_index, audit, tenants

async fn write<T, F>(&self, f: F) -> Result<T, StorageError> {
    tokio::task::spawn_blocking(move || {
        let mut txn = db.begin_write()?;
        txn.set_durability(Durability::Immediate);   // WAL + fsync on commit
        let out = f(&txn)?;
        txn.commit()?;
        Ok(out)
    }).await
}
}

Figure 1: The canonical write pattern in src/storage/index_db.rs. One upload = one such transaction covering all chunk refcounts plus the manifest.

The upload pipeline depends on exactly this behaviour: register_chunks (refcount increments with a per-chunk dedup report) and put_manifest commit atomically, so a partially-registered file can never be observed.

Consequences

Positive

  • Real ACID semantics with an explicit durability knob — the crash-safety story (blob-before-index, then one fsynced transaction) is provable rather than hopeful.
  • Single-file embeddability: no daemon, no network, no connection pool; the whole metadata state is one file, which simplifies backup and restore (Backup & Restore).
  • Synchronous, simple API: no async-safety surprises; blocking calls are confined to spawn_blocking where they belong.
  • Pure Rust: no C/C++ FFI build surface; the release binary stays ~11.7 MB and distroless-friendly.
  • Actively maintained upstream, with a stable 2.x API.

Negative

  • Single-writer file lock: the database is opened by one process; no multi-process sharing of one index.
  • B-tree over LSM trade: sustained, write-heavy churn (very high upload rates with heavy dedup refcount updates) gives more headroom to LSM engines, at the cost of compaction stalls.
  • One file = mixed IO: audit appends and hot-path writes share the same WAL/fsync budget.
  • Ecosystem tooling is thin compared with SQLite; inspection means the service’s own API rather than a universal CLI.

Alternatives considered

  • sled — the natural Rust-native candidate when the project started, but upstream maintenance stalled (unreleased, unfinished guarantees); building an archive format on a frozen project was unacceptable. Rejected on project health.
  • SQLite (rusqlite) — battle-tested ACID, superior tooling (sqlite3 CLI), but heavier: C FFI build, SQL/schema layer for what is pure byte-keyed access, and per-query parsing overhead for hot refcount paths. Rejected as unnecessary weight for these access patterns; a defensible choice if rich ad-hoc querying ever becomes a requirement.
  • RocksDB — excellent write throughput, but LSM background compaction introduces latency stalls directly in the upload path, the C++ FFI complicates the distroless build, and its operational knobs far exceed the need for six small tables. Rejected on tail latency and build complexity.
  • LMDB (via bindings) — strong read performance and crash safety, but the mmap model and C dependency complicate the deployment; write concurrency limits would bottleneck the 8-wide pipeline. Rejected.

Usage

KeyDefaultNotes
database.pathdata/index.redbSingle file; back up while the process is stopped

Startup creates or opens the file and initialises all six tables. There are no tunables beyond the path by design — durability is fixed at Immediate for correctness.

Examples

The six tables and their encodings are documented with a layout diagram in Storage Layout. A minimal read through the API:

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
let meta = index.get_chunk_meta(&hash).await?;   // Option<ChunkMeta>, bincode
let manifest = index.get_manifest(&file_id).await?; // Option<Manifest>, msgpack
}

Reads run without durability overhead (no fsync), so L3-cache misses on the manifest path cost one blocking-pool round trip.

See also

  • Storage Layout — the six tables and their encodings
  • ADR 0001 — CAS over block store — why blobs live outside the database
  • Data Flow — the single-transaction commit in sequence
  • Garbage Collection — the refcount sweeps that read these tables
  • Backup & Restore — operating on the single index file

ADR 0004: moka cache hierarchy

This decision record explains why the read path uses three independent moka caches with different value shapes, budgets, and TTLs — instead of one general-purpose cache. It also records why moka was chosen over lru and cached.

Overview

The retrieval path has three distinct hot values: decrypted plaintext ready to stream, raw stored blobs ready to decode, and file manifests that route every request. Collapsing them into one cache would force a single TTL and eviction policy onto incompatible shapes. The service therefore runs three moka::future::Cache instances — L1 plaintext (256 MiB / 300 s), L2 blobs (512 MiB / 3600 s), L3 manifests (10 000 entries / 60 s) — built once in src/cache/ and shared by both listeners through AppState.

Status

Accepted — 2026-11-10.

Context

Cache requirements by tier differ in every dimension:

  • L1 (plaintext chunks) — values are decompressed/decrypted bytes that took real CPU to produce; correctness requires that only verified plaintext be cached; the right budget is bytes, since chunk sizes vary 8 KiB–128 KiB; a short TTL bounds stale-plaintext risk after hypothetical corruption events and keeps memory reclaimable.
  • L2 (stored blobs) — values are compressed/encrypted blobs as written to disk; they are immutable, so a long TTL is safe; budget is again bytes; this is the tier read-ahead warms, because decode must stay on the hot path.
  • L3 (manifests) — values are Arc<Manifest> routing records; budget is naturally entry count; the TTL must be short because soft-deletes must become visible promptly, even where explicit invalidation cannot reach (for example replicas learning about deletes lazily).

Concurrency matters as much as policy: hundreds of concurrent range requests may need the same blob, and thundering-herd disk loads would defeat the cache entirely.

Decision

Adopt moka 0.12 (future feature) and build three caches in src/cache/chunk_cache.rs and src/cache/manifest_cache.rs:

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
let plain = Cache::builder()
    .max_capacity(cfg.l1_max_bytes)                       // 268435456
    .weigher(|_k, v: &Bytes| (v.len() as u32).max(1))     // byte weigher
    .time_to_live(Duration::from_secs(cfg.l1_ttl_secs))   // 300
    .build();
}

Figure 1: The L1 builder. L2 is identical with l2_* values; L3 drops the weigher and caps by manifest_capacity.

The blob tier exposes get_or_load_blob, which consults L2 and, on miss, loads via try_get_with — moka’s single-flight primitive, so concurrent requests for one hash share one disk load. Load errors are propagated and never cached. L1 insertion happens only after BLAKE3 verification of the decoded plaintext.

How it works

  • Read order — L1 → L2 → disk, then decode and verify, then insert into L1 (the raw blob is already in L2 via the loader). See Caching for the full path and hit accounting (file_storage_cache_hits_total{level}).
  • Read-ahead — the retrieval engine prefetches chunks N+1..N+4 into L2 only (peek_blob skips already-cached entries; tasks time out after 10 s), deliberately leaving decode work off the prefetch path.
  • Invalidation — deletes and same-name replacement invalidate affected manifest entries explicitly; the 60 s L3 TTL bounds staleness everywhere else.
  • Diagnostics — stats() runs moka’s pending tasks and reports per-tier entry counts and weighted bytes.

Consequences

Positive

  • Per-tier tuning: bytes-versus-entries, long-versus-short TTLs, and different eviction pressures are independent, so each tier can be sized for its workload instead of compromising.
  • Warm-read performance: measured 1107 MB/s warm downloads versus 399 MB/s cold; an in-process 2 MiB retrieval on L1 hits costs ~29 µs (Benchmarks).
  • Single-flight loads: try_get_with turns concurrent misses into one disk read, protecting disk and index under hot ranges.
  • Verified-only L1: integrity checking cannot be bypassed by the cache; a corrupt disk load fails before it is cached.
  • Concurrency-native: moka’s future API matches the tokio stack of both listeners without locks around the cache.

Negative

  • Three knobs to tune (six config keys), and a misconfigured pair of budgets can over-commit memory: defaults add up to ~768 MiB before manifests.
  • No cross-process coherence: caches are per-process; a replica repaired by pull-repair cannot warm a primary’s cache, and TTLs (not invalidation) bound staleness between nodes.
  • No persistence: restarts start cold; the first requests after a deploy always hit disk.
  • TinyLFU admission is probabilistic: occasionally-evicted-then-readmitted entries cause minor variance in hit rates, which complicates naive hit-rate alerting.

Alternatives considered

  • Single shared cache — one TTL and one weigher for plaintext, blobs, and manifests. Rejected: the manifest TTL must be short for delete visibility, while blob TTLs want to be long; a single policy either wastes L2 or risks serving from stale manifests.
  • lru crate — simple and popular, but synchronous and lock-based, with no byte weighers, TTLs, or single-flight loads; wrapping it in async primitives would reintroduce the bugs moka already solved. Rejected.
  • cached crate — function-memoisation macros with TTL support, but limited control over per-entry weighing and no try_get_with-style concurrent-load collapsing for async functions. Rejected for the byte-weighted tiers.
  • No cache — every read pays disk IO plus BLAKE3 verification plus decode. Rejected: the measured cold-versus-warm gap (399 vs 1107 MB/s) makes this a 2.8× regression on read-heavy workloads.

Usage

KeyDefaultControls
cache.l1_max_bytes268435456L1 plaintext byte budget
cache.l1_ttl_secs300L1 entry lifetime
cache.l2_max_bytes536870912L2 blob byte budget
cache.l2_ttl_secs3600L2 entry lifetime
cache.manifest_capacity10000L3 entry cap
cache.manifest_ttl_secs60L3 entry lifetime

Sizing recipes and the interaction with PIPELINE_CONCURRENCY are in Tuning; hit-rate dashboards in Monitoring.

Examples

Observe tier behaviour under a repeated range read (second request should hit L1):

curl -s localhost:8080/metrics | grep cache_hits_total
# file_storage_cache_hits_total{level="l1"} 1042
# file_storage_cache_hits_total{level="l2"} 87

The cache types as exposed to the rest of the system:

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
pub struct CacheLayer {
    pub chunks: Arc<ChunkCache>,       // L1 plain + L2 blob
    pub manifests: Arc<ManifestCache>, // L3
}
}

See also

  • Caching — the tiers, read-ahead, and hit accounting in depth
  • Data Flow — cache misses in the retrieval sequence diagrams
  • Storage Layout — what L2 caches on disk
  • ADR 0003 — redb — the L3-miss fallback store
  • Benchmarks — cold/warm numbers
  • Tuning — budget sizing

ADR 0005: AES-256-GCM with per-tenant keys

This decision record explains the at-rest encryption design: AES-256-GCM with one 32-byte key per tenant, a self-describing nonce || ciphertext || tag blob layout, and key files with 0600 permissions. It also records honestly what is not provided — KMS integration and in-place key rotation — and why ChaCha20-Poly1305 was passed over.

Overview

encryption.enabled: true adds one pipeline stage: after compression, every chunk is sealed with the owning tenant’s AES-256-GCM key. Keys are generated per tenant on first use and stored as hex files under encryption.keys_dir with owner-only permissions. The design favours operational simplicity (no external key service) and hardware throughput (AES-NI), while keeping per-tenant blast-radius isolation.

Status

Accepted — 2026-11-10.

Context

Encryption at rest had to satisfy:

  • Tenant blast-radius isolation — compromise of one tenant’s key must not expose other tenants’ blobs.
  • Throughput — encryption sits inline in the upload pipeline between zstd and the blob write; a slow cipher would cap upload throughput on commodity servers.
  • Self-describing blobs — the reader must know how to decode a blob from its manifest alone, without probing formats.
  • No external dependencies in the default deployment — a KMS or HSM requirement would break the single-process, single-volume model and complicate air-gapped installs.
  • Random-nonce AEAD, since chunks are encrypted concurrently by the 8-wide pipeline and there is no shared nonce counter.

Decision

Use AES-256-GCM from the aes-gcm 0.10 crate, with one 32-byte key per tenant:

  • Blob layout: nonce(12) || ciphertext || tag(16) — a fresh random 96-bit nonce per encryption, the GCM tag appended; the layout is written as-is to the block store, so blob size grows by 28 bytes.
  • Key storage: 32 random bytes per tenant, hex-encoded at <keys_dir>/<tenant>.key, created with create_new under 0600 (owner-only) and fsynced; a key_file_permissions_ok check verifies Unix permissions. Keys are cached in a DashMap after first load.
  • Explicit flags: ChunkRef.encrypted records the state per chunk — no magic-byte sniffing at decode time.
  • Key lifetime: the service never rewrites key files. An Encryptor::key_age_ms diagnostic exposes the key file’s modification time so operators can track key age and drive external rotation; rotating a key today means re-encrypting that tenant’s blobs out of band. Full envelope encryption and KMS integration are documented as future work, not implemented features.

How it works

  • Write: Encryptor::encrypt(tenant, compressed_bytes) → nonce || ct || tag → BlockStore::write under the tenant-derived storage key (see ADR 0007 for why the key derivation changes).
  • Read: the retrieval engine decrypts (GCM authentication failure → Encryption error, surfaced as HTTP 500 / gRPC INTERNAL with code encryption_error), then decompresses, then verifies BLAKE3 against plain_hash — so tampering is caught twice: by AEAD and by content hash.
  • Concurrent safety: random nonces per encrypt call are the correct choice for concurrent pipelines; the guideline ceiling for random 96-bit nonces is to keep per-key encryption counts well below 2³² (collision probability ~2⁻³² at that scale), which this service’s chunk counts stay far under.
plaintext chunk ──► zstd-3 ──► AES-256-GCM(seal)  ──► nonce(12) || ct || tag(16) ──► blob
                                  key: data/keys/<tenant>.key (0600, hex)

Figure 1: Where the cipher sits in the pipeline. Encryption is applied after compression, so incompressible random data still benefits from ciphertext size reduction upstream.

Consequences

Positive

  • Per-tenant isolation: one key file per tenant; losing or rotating one tenant’s key affects only that tenant’s blobs.
  • Hardware speed: AES-NI makes AES-256-GCM the fastest AEAD in the Rust ecosystem on typical server CPUs; encryption adds little to the measured 273 MB/s in-process pipeline rate.
  • Authenticated integrity: GCM tags plus post-decrypt BLAKE3 verification give two independent tamper checks per chunk.
  • Simple ops: keys are plain files with OS-enforced permissions; no KMS contract, no network hop, works air-gapped.
  • Self-describing storage: manifest flags say how to decode; the blob needs no headers.

Negative

  • Key loss is data loss: there is no escrow, no envelope, no recovery path if a key file is destroyed; backup discipline for data/keys is mandatory (see Secrets).
  • Rotation is manual and heavy: replacing a key requires re-encrypting every blob of the tenant out of band; the service exposes key_age_ms as a diagnostic but no rotation workflow.
  • No KMS/HSM: key material lives on the same host as the data (mitigated by 0600 and host hardening); organisations requiring centralized key custody must wait for the documented envelope-encryption future work.
  • 28-byte blob overhead per chunk, and one extra AEAD pass per chunk on both paths.
  • Encryption scopes dedup: ciphertext-addressed dedup is impossible with random nonces; resolved via the tenant-scoped key derivation in ADR 0007.

Alternatives considered

  • ChaCha20-Poly1305 — an excellent AEAD, but its performance advantage exists only on hardware without AES-NI; on the AES-capable servers this service targets, AES-256-GCM is faster. Rejected for the default; remains a reasonable choice for AES-less platforms.
  • XChaCha20-Poly1305 — a 192-bit nonce removes collision concerns entirely, but it is not in the standard AEAD interface set of the crate family and the throughput argument of ChaCha still applies. Rejected with ChaCha.
  • Full envelope encryption with KMS/HSM integration — data keys wrapped by a master key held in an external service; the right long-term design for regulated environments, but it introduces an availability dependency (KMS outage = no reads) and deployment complexity. Documented as future work, deliberately not half-implemented.
  • Tenant-supplied keys (client-side encryption) — shifts key management to clients and forfeits server-side dedup entirely under the naive scheme; also complicates every SDK. Rejected for now.
  • No encryption, rely on volume encryption (LUKS/dm-crypt) — protects against device theft only, not against host-level readers; provides no per-tenant isolation. Rejected as insufficient for the multi-tenant model.

Usage

KeyDefaultNotes
encryption.enabledfalseEnables the pipeline stage and the tenant-scoped storage-key derivation
encryption.keys_dirdata/keysOne <tenant-uuid>.key hex file per tenant, 0600

Environment overrides: FS_ENCRYPTION__ENABLED=true, FS_ENCRYPTION__KEYS_DIR=/etc/file-storage/keys. Keys are created lazily per tenant on first encrypted upload; pre-provision them for replicas so both nodes derive the same storage keys.

Examples

On-disk evidence of the design:

$ ls -l data/keys/
-rw------- 1 app app 64 Nov 10 09:14 01930000-0000-7000-8000-000000000002.key

$ head -c 56 data/keys/<tenant>.key; echo
3f9a4b17c0d8e5f2a1b3c4d5e6f708192a3b4c5d6e7f8091a2b3c4d5e6f70819   # 64 hex chars = 32 bytes

A chunk ref under encryption shows both hashes diverging:

{
  "hash": "9d31c0ffee…(storage key = BLAKE3(tenant_id || plain_hash))",
  "plain_hash": "7c1e9a04b2d8…(plaintext BLAKE3, used for verify)",
  "compressed": true,
  "encrypted": true,
  "size_stored": 12132
}

size_stored includes the 28 bytes of nonce plus tag; the read path strips and checks them before decompression.

  • Security — the security architecture summary
  • Encryption at Rest — operational key management
  • ADR 0007 — Encrypted dedup key derivation — the storage-key design under encryption
  • Threat Model — what these controls do and do not cover
  • Secrets — handling keys_dir and other secret material
  • Configuration reference — the encryption.* keys

ADR 0006: WAL-style replication shipping

This decision record explains why replication is an in-memory bounded queue drained by a batched background worker, rather than a durable on-disk journal, a per-operation call stream, or an external log broker. It also records the durability trade this buys — and the bounded loss window operators must understand.

Overview

Every upload enqueues idempotent operations (chunk blobs, then the manifest) into a bounded in-memory channel; a worker ships batches of up to 64 ops or every 5 seconds to the replica over POST /internal/batch, retrying with a 100 ms → 500 ms → 2 s backoff ladder. A Flush sentinel with a oneshot reply turns the same queue into the sync-mode barrier that lets uploads wait for replica acknowledgement.

Status

Accepted — 2026-11-10.

Context

The replication design had to fit the deployment model: one primary, one replica target, shared-secret authenticated HTTP between them, and no external infrastructure. Additional constraints:

  • Ordering per file — a replica must never apply a manifest whose chunk blobs have not arrived.
  • Idempotent ops — retries after timeouts and crashes must be safe without a dedup protocol.
  • Bounded memory — a stalled replica must not let the queue grow without limit; backpressure should propagate to uploads.
  • Low upload-path overhead — enqueueing must be cheaper than the upload itself; the queue sits after the local commit.
  • A durability escape hatch — some deployments want the upload to wait for replica ack; the mechanism should be a mode, not a second implementation.

Decision

Implement a WAL-style shipper in src/replication/replicator.rs:

  • An mpsc channel of capacity replication.queue_capacity (10 000) holds Op::Chunk, Op::Manifest, Op::Purge, and Op::Flush{reply}. Sends await a slot when the queue is full — backpressure by design.
  • A single worker task drains opportunistically, ships when it holds batch_size (64) ops or after flush_interval_ms (5000) of lingering, and retries failed batches after 100 ms, then 500 ms, then 2 s (held at 2 s until recovery).
  • Transport: the built-in shipper POSTs to the replica’s /internal/batch endpoint with the compact frame codec (u32 count, then per chunk hash(32) | plain_hash(32) | u64 size_original | u64 size_stored | u8 flags | u32 blob_len | blob; manifests as length-prefixed MessagePack), authenticated by the X-Api-Key header. The gRPC ReplicationService (ReplicateBatch bidi stream, PullChunk, PullManifest) exposes the same operations for external shippers, authenticated by x-api-key metadata.
  • Sync mode: Op::Flush carries a oneshot sender; the worker replies when every previously queued op is applied. Uploads in replication.mode: sync await this reply, bounded by sync_timeout_ms (5000); a timeout fails the upload with Unavailable (503 / gRPC UNAVAILABLE).

How it works

upload commit ──► enqueue Chunk(op)* ──► enqueue Manifest ──► [sync: wait_flushed]
                     |                              |
                     v                              v
        queue (mpsc, cap 10 000, backpressure on send)
                     |
                     v
        worker: drain ≤ 64 ops or 5000 ms linger
                     |
                     v
        POST /internal/batch (chunks first, then manifest)
                     |
             ok ─────┴───── fail ──► backoff 100 ms → 500 ms → 2 s → retry batch

Figure 1: The shipper loop. Order is preserved by the channel; ops are idempotent, so a retried batch re-writes blobs as no-ops.

Observability comes from file_storage_replication_queue_depth (gauge), replication_failures_total, replication_ops_applied_total, and replication_flush_seconds{status}.

Consequences

Positive

  • Zero-disk queue: enqueueing costs an atomic counter increment and a channel send — no fsync on the replication path, so uploads are never slowed by replication bookkeeping.
  • Batching amortises HTTP overhead: 64 ops per request keeps per-chunk latency negligible on LANs.
  • Bounded memory with graceful degradation: 10 000 ops is the worst-case buffer; beyond that, uploads slow down instead of the process OOMing.
  • Replay safety: idempotent ops mean a retried or duplicated batch converges — the replica does the same writes twice and the second is a no-op.
  • One mechanism, two modes: the Flush sentinel reuses the queue for sync mode instead of forking a second code path.
  • Crash residue is reconcilable: the replica’s pull-repair on read misses (upstream_url) and its reachability GC close whatever gaps the lost queue tail left.

Negative

  • Un-flushed ops are lost on a primary crash: the queue is memory only, so async mode is eventual consistency with a bounded loss window (at most the queue contents; typically the last few seconds of writes). This is the core trade of the design — sync mode closes it at the cost of upload latency.
  • No fan-out: exactly one target_url; multi-replica topologies need chained replicas or an external shipper speaking the gRPC protocol.
  • Queue depth is not observable across restarts: the gauge resets; after a crash, reconciliation is pull-based, not replay-based.
  • Backoff caps reactivity: while the replica is down, retries pause up to 2 s; recovery drains at batch speed, so large gaps take many batch windows.

Alternatives considered

  • On-disk queue file (real WAL) — fsyncing a journal per enqueue would survive primary crashes and enable true replay; rejected for v1 because it adds fsyncs to the hot upload path, plus segment-rotation and compaction machinery, for a benefit that only matters in async mode (sync-mode users already get acked durability). Revisit if async durability requirements harden.
  • Direct gRPC stream per operation — one RPC per chunk is chatty (thousands of round trips per large upload) and forfeits batch acknowledgment semantics; rejected. The bidi ReplicateBatch RPC exists for external shippers that want streaming, but the built-in shipper batches.
  • Kafka / NATS as the op log — solves durability and fan-out elegantly, but imports a broker cluster, its operational burden, and an availability dependency into a single-replica design; the service would no longer run from one binary and one data directory. Rejected for now.
  • Outbox table in redb — durable ops inside the existing index transactions would give replayable replication without a new component; rejected for v1 because it couples replication throughput to the metadata store’s write path and complicates GC of applied entries. A plausible future upgrade path.

Usage

KeyDefaultEffect
replication.enabledfalseSpawns the worker and client
replication.modeasyncsync enables the flush barrier per upload
replication.target_urlnullReplica base URL (REST transport)
replication.upstream_urlnullReplica-side pull-repair source
replication.api_key—Shared secret, both transports
replication.queue_capacity10000Backpressure threshold
replication.batch_size64Ops per shipped batch
replication.flush_interval_ms5000Max linger of a partial batch
replication.sync_timeout_ms5000Sync-mode barrier budget

Runbook procedures (failover, draining, reconciliation) are in Replication Ops.

Examples

Watch the shipper’s health:

curl -s localhost:8080/metrics | grep replication
# file_storage_replication_queue_depth 0
# file_storage_replication_failures_total 0
# file_storage_replication_ops_applied_total 14213
# file_storage_replication_flush_seconds_count{status="ok"} 218

The gRPC equivalent of a batch, for custom shippers:

grpcurl -plaintext -H "x-api-key: $KEY" \
  -d '{"put_chunk": {"hash": "…32 bytes…", "data": "…", "compressed": true}}' \
  localhost:9091 file_storage.v1.ReplicationService/ReplicateBatch

See also

  • Replication — the full replication architecture
  • Data Flow — where ops enter the queue in the upload sequence
  • Garbage Collection — purge propagation into the same queue
  • gRPC API — ReplicationService contract details
  • Replication Ops — operational procedures

ADR 0007: Encrypted dedup key derivation

This decision record explains the tension between encryption and dedup, and the derivation storage_key = BLAKE3(tenant_id || plain_hash) that resolves it. It is the single most security-sensitive design choice in the system, so the reasoning and the rejected alternatives are spelled out in full.

Overview

AES-256-GCM sealing (ADR 0005) uses a random nonce per encryption, so encrypting the same chunk twice produces different ciphertext — dedup by ciphertext hash is impossible. The service solves this by keying blobs on BLAKE3(tenant_id || plain_hash) when encryption is enabled: dedup remains exact within a tenant, becomes structurally impossible across tenants, and leaks nothing about plaintext equality to anyone outside the tenant.

Status

Accepted — 2026-11-10.

Context

Without encryption, the block store is addressed by BLAKE3(plaintext), and dedup falls out of content addressing (ADR 0001). With encryption enabled, the naive pipeline breaks:

  1. Chunk plaintext is hashed → plain_hash.
  2. Compression then encryption with a random nonce → ciphertext blob.
  3. If the blob were addressed by BLAKE3(ciphertext), the random nonce would make every encryption unique — no chunk would ever dedup, even re-uploading the identical file.

The alternatives each carry security costs. Deriving an encryption key from plaintext (convergent encryption) enables dictionary attacks. Deterministic encryption (fixed nonce per plaintext) fails catastrophically for GCM, where nonce reuse is fatal. The requirement set was therefore: keep dedup for the duplicate-heavy, same-tenant workloads this service targets; guarantee zero cross-tenant information leakage, including equality (existence) probes; and never weaken AEAD nonce uniqueness.

Decision

When encryption.enabled: true, the block-store key for a chunk is derived as:

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
// src/security/encryption.rs
pub fn storage_key(tenant: &Uuid, plain_hash: &[u8; 32]) -> [u8; 32] {
    let mut buf = Vec::with_capacity(16 + 32);
    buf.extend_from_slice(tenant.as_bytes());   // 16 raw bytes
    buf.extend_from_slice(plain_hash);          // 32 raw bytes
    hash_bytes(&buf)                            // BLAKE3
}
}

The upload pipeline computes storage_hash = Encryptor::storage_key(&tenant, &plain_hash) per chunk; ChunkRef.hash carries the storage key (the blob name and chunks-table row) while ChunkRef.plain_hash still carries the plaintext BLAKE3 for read-back verification. Dedup is exact within a tenant — identical plaintext under one tenant maps to the same storage key — and impossible across tenants by construction, because a different tenant UUID yields a different key.

How it works

same tenant, same plaintext:
    BLAKE3(tenant_A || H)  ──►  one blob, refcount++   (dedup works)
different tenants, same plaintext:
    BLAKE3(tenant_A || H) ≠ BLAKE3(tenant_B || H)      (two blobs, no linkage)

Figure 1: The derivation’s effect. Tenant identity is mixed into the address, so equality of storage keys implies equality of tenant and plaintext — nothing else.

On the read path, the retrieval engine decrypts with the tenant’s key, decompresses, and verifies the plaintext against plain_hash; the storage key’s derivation plays no role in verification, so the two hashes serve independent purposes.

Consequences

Positive

  • Dedup survives encryption within a tenant — the dominant case (repeated uploads, backups, same-name replacement) keeps full storage savings; measured behaviour is unchanged from the unencrypted path.
  • Zero cross-tenant leakage by construction: no equality oracle exists across tenants. A malicious tenant cannot probe whether another tenant stored a given file, because blob addresses never collide across tenants regardless of content.
  • No cryptographic compromises: nonces stay random and unique (GCM safety intact); no convergent-encryption weaknesses are introduced.
  • Simple implementation: one 48-byte BLAKE3 input per chunk; no additional secrets, keys, or protocols beyond the per-tenant key of ADR 0005.

Negative

  • tenant_id becomes part of the key derivation — tenant UUIDs must never change. Renaming a tenant is fine (names live in the tenants table), but re-issuing a tenant UUID would orphan every blob that tenant ever stored, and no in-place migration exists. Treat tenant UUIDs as immutable identifiers.
  • No cross-tenant dedup under encryption: two tenants storing the same ISO pay for it twice. Accepted: the storage saving is real but small next to the leakage risk, and deployments that need cross-tenant sharing should model it as one tenant.
  • Tenant-scoped, not user-scoped: all users of one tenant share the dedup domain, since the derivation uses the tenant UUID — consistent with the tenancy model, but worth stating.
  • Mixed-mode replication hazard: a primary and replica must agree on encryption.enabled and share key files, or derived storage keys diverge and reads fail with decryption errors. Pre-provision data/keys on replicas.

Alternatives considered

  • Convergent encryption (derive the AEAD key from the plaintext hash) — restores global dedup, but is vulnerable to confirmed-dictionary attacks: an attacker who can guess a candidate plaintext can verify it against stored ciphertext. The whole point of per-tenant keys is to prevent exactly this. Rejected.
  • Per-tenant fixed tweak only (for example, binding tenant_id into GCM’s associated data while keeping ciphertext addressing) — still requires deterministic encryption to make ciphertext hashes match, which is unsafe for GCM (nonce reuse) or any AEAD. Rejected as technically unsound, not merely risky.
  • No dedup under encryption (address purely by ciphertext hash) — the safest option and the simplest, at the cost of losing all dedup for encrypted workloads; duplicate-heavy tenants would see multi-fold storage growth. Rejected after weighing the leakage-free alternative above.
  • Dup-less encryption schemes from the literature (Message-locked encryption with server-aided key generation) — interesting research direction, but no audited, production-grade Rust implementation exists; the complexity and attack surface outweigh the cross-tenant savings. Rejected; revisit only with a credible implementation.

Usage

The derivation is implicit in encryption.enabled — there is no separate toggle:

KeyDefaultEffect on this decision
encryption.enabledfalsefalse → storage key = plain_hash; true → `BLAKE3(tenant_id
encryption.keys_dirdata/keysTenant keys; must match across primary and replica

Operational rule: tenant UUIDs are immutable. They are minted at tenant creation (or seeded from tenants[] in config) and must never be re-generated; renaming via POST /api/v1/tenants semantics and config edits is safe.

Examples

Verify the scoping property on a running pair of tenants:

# upload the identical file as alice (tenant …002) and bob (tenant …003)
# with encryption enabled on both:
#   alice's report: hash = BLAKE3(tenant_002 || H)   -> blob 1
#   bob's   report: hash = BLAKE3(tenant_003 || H)   -> blob 2 (no shared blob)
# re-upload as alice again:
#   dedup_chunks = chunk_count                        -> refcount on blob 1

The manifest makes both derivations visible:

{
  "hash": "9d31c0ffee…(tenant-scoped storage key)",
  "plain_hash": "7c1e9a04b2d8…(plaintext BLAKE3 for verification)",
  "encrypted": true
}

See also

  • ADR 0005 — AES-GCM per-tenant key — the cipher and key-file design this builds on
  • Security — where this sits in the security model
  • Chunking & Dedup — the unencrypted dedup mechanics being scoped
  • Storage Layout — ChunkRef.hash versus ChunkRef.plain_hash encodings
  • Threat Model — attacker assumptions about leakage and probing

ADR 0008: gRPC alongside REST

This decision record explains why the service runs a second, tonic-based gRPC listener next to the REST API rather than replacing REST or extending it. It covers what the two listeners share, where their semantics intentionally differ, and the operational costs of maintaining two API surfaces.

Overview

file-storage exposes one storage core through two protocols in one process: REST (axum 0.7) on server.port (8080) and gRPC (tonic 0.12) on server.grpc_port (9090), both backed by the same AppState. gRPC exists for streaming uploads/downloads with typed contracts, the internal replication protocol, and lower per-RPC overhead; REST remains the universal surface for humans, scripts, and browsers. Neither is a shim over the other — both call the same pipeline, retrieval engine, index, and caches.

Status

Accepted — 2026-11-10.

Context

REST served the first users well (curl-able, browser-friendly, ubiquitous tooling), but four pressures motivated a second protocol:

  • Streaming semantics — large uploads and downloads want first-class client- and server-streaming with HTTP/2 flow control; hand-rolled chunked JSON is awkward, and multipart framing is a poor fit for programmatic clients.
  • Typed contracts — SDKs (Rust today, Go/Python documented) want generated types instead of hand-maintained JSON mappings; a schema also freezes the internal replication protocol between nodes of different versions.
  • Internal replication — node-to-node traffic (ReplicateBatch, PullChunk, PullManifest) benefits from binary framing, bidi streaming, and health/reflection for orchestration probes.
  • Per-RPC overhead — JSON encoding plus header-heavy requests cost measurable latency on the hot path compared with protobuf over HTTP/2.

At the same time, dropping REST would break the simplest integrations: curl one-liners, browser downloads with Range and ETag, and every HTTP-native client.

Decision

Run a second listener in the same process:

  • tonic 0.12 on server.grpc_port (9090), sharing AppState with the axum stack; graceful shutdown covers both.
  • Four project services in file_storage.v1 (protos under proto/file_storage/v1/): FileService (client-streaming Upload, server-streaming Download with start/end, GetManifest, ListFiles, DeleteFile), AuthService, AdminService (including TriggerGC, Resync, QueryAudit), and ReplicationService — plus grpc.health.v1.Health (background probe every 10 s flips NOT_SERVING within ≤15 s) and grpc.reflection.v1.ServerReflection for grpcurl discovery without proto files.
  • Unary interceptors: JWT → AuthContext, admin gate, per-tenant rate limiting, request-id propagation. Streaming RPCs verify auth in-handler, because tonic interceptors only execute for unary calls. Router-level layers add gRPC metrics and panic recovery (grpc-status 13).
  • Server tuning mirrors the REST limits: 256 MiB max message, HTTP/2 keepalive 30 s/timeout 20 s, 64 concurrent requests per connection, 4 MiB stream / 8 MiB connection windows, TCP keepalive 30 s, gzip compression both directions.
  • Proto field discipline: hashes are raw 32-byte fields; ReplicationOp uses a oneof envelope; field numbers are frozen once released, so proto evolution is additive only.

How it works

The two surfaces are intentionally equivalent at the storage layer and intentionally different at the wire layer:

AspectREST (/api/v1, axum)gRPC (file_storage.v1, tonic)
UploadPOST /files — multipart or raw body, streamedUpload client-streaming: UploadChunk{header first | data}
DownloadGET /files/:id — full body, ETag/304Download server-streaming: header, then data frames
RangesRange: bytes=start-end (inclusive end; suffix -N; multi-range → first range) → 206/416Download{start, end} half-open exclusive end, clamped to size; OUT_OF_RANGE when start ≥ size
Browser supportNative (fetch, <a download>, service workers)None directly; gRPC-Web needs a translating proxy
Toolingcurl, httpie, every HTTP clientgrpcurl (via reflection), codegen’d SDKs
Internal replication/internal/* with X-Api-Key (the built-in WAL shipper’s transport)ReplicationService with x-api-key metadata
Rate limitingtower_governor per client IPPer-tenant token bucket; streaming RPCs not rate limited (documented)
ErrorsJSON {"error":{"code","message"}}gRPC status codes mapped from StorageError

Both listeners emit the same file_storage_* metrics with their own dimensions (http_requests_total{method,route,status} versus grpc_requests_total{rpc,code}), and a file uploaded over either is byte-identical when downloaded over the other.

Consequences

Positive

  • Typed, versioned contracts: generated clients, reflection-driven discovery, and a frozen replication wire format that tolerates version skew between nodes.
  • First-class streaming: client-streaming uploads and server-streaming ranged downloads with real HTTP/2 flow control (4 MiB / 8 MiB windows) and gzip framing.
  • Cheaper internal traffic: protobuf framing keeps node-to-node replication compact; health + reflection make Kubernetes probes and grpcurl-based debugging trivial.
  • One storage core: transport parity is structural, not aspirational — the same pipeline, caches, and index serve both listeners, so behaviour drift is unlikely.
  • Incremental adoption: existing REST integrations keep working while performance-sensitive clients move to gRPC.

Negative

  • Two API surfaces to document and test: the suite carries separate REST (17) and gRPC (18) E2E tests plus duplicate reference pages; every feature must land on both sides or be explicitly documented as asymmetric.
  • tonic’s interceptor limitation: interceptors run only for unary RPCs, so streaming handlers duplicate auth logic in-handler; a new streaming RPC that forgets the check is a plausible bug class — mitigated by review checklists, not by the type system.
  • Proto evolution discipline: field numbers are permanent; renames and type changes are breaking. API changes require additive fields and a version bump of file_storage.v1.
  • Two listeners to operate: two ports in firewalls and containers, TLS configured per listener (server.grpc_tls), and per-listener dashboards.

Alternatives considered

  • Replace REST with gRPC — one protocol, one test suite; but it breaks curl workflows, browser downloads, and every HTTP-native integration on day one, and Range/ETag semantics have no natural gRPC equivalent. Rejected as needlessly destructive.
  • GraphQL — good for shaped reads, poor for binary streaming: file bytes do not fit resolver semantics well, and the upload path would fight the query layer. Rejected.
  • Pure gRPC-Web — browsers could talk gRPC directly, but it requires a translating proxy (Envoy or equivalent), still lacks true bidirectional streaming from browsers, and adds a moving part to every deployment. Rejected for now.
  • Keep REST only, add chunked-JSON streaming — avoids a second listener but reimplements framing, flow control, and contracts poorly; the SDK would hand-map JSON fragments. Rejected after prototyping costs were compared with tonic codegen.

Usage

KeyDefaultEffect
server.port8080REST listener
server.grpc_port9090gRPC listener
server.grpc_tls.enabledfalseTLS on the gRPC listener (cert_path, key_path)
server.grpc_tls.client_ca_pathnullSet to require client certificates (mTLS)

The Rust client SDK (file_storage::grpc::FileStorageClient) speaks to the gRPC listener with a built-in RetryPolicy (3 attempts, 100 ms → 2 s backoff, retrying Unavailable/DeadlineExceeded/Aborted); see Rust Client.

Examples

Discover and call the API without a proto file on disk:

grpcurl -plaintext localhost:9090 list
grpcurl -plaintext -d '{"username":"admin","password":"admin123"}' \
    localhost:9090 file_storage.v1.AuthService/Login
grpcurl -plaintext -d '{"service": "file_storage.v1.FileService"}' \
    localhost:9090 grpc.health.v1.Health/Check

The same file from both transports:

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
let mut client = file_storage::grpc::FileStorageClient::connect("http://127.0.0.1:9090").await?;
client.login("admin", "admin123").await?;
let resp = client.upload(
    pb::UploadHeader { name: "demo.bin".into(), ..Default::default() },
    std::io::Cursor::new(payload),
).await?;
}
curl -s -X POST "localhost:8080/api/v1/files?name=demo.bin" \
     -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" --data-binary @demo.bin

See also

  • gRPC API — the complete service reference
  • REST API — the HTTP surface, including Range/ETag semantics
  • Errors — the shared StorageError mapping table
  • Replication — the internal protocol’s use of both transports
  • TLS — terminating TLS for each listener

Quickstart

This page takes you from zero to a working file-storage service in about five minutes: start the server, log in, upload a file, download it back, and confirm the gRPC listener is alive. Everything runs locally with the development configuration — no TLS, no encryption, and the seeded demo users.

Overview

file-storage is a single Rust binary that exposes two listeners from one process: a REST API (axum) on port 8080 and a gRPC API (tonic) on port 9090. Both transports share the same storage core, so a file uploaded over REST is byte-identical when downloaded over gRPC. The development configuration ships with three tenants and four users, including the administrator admin / admin123.

  you ──► REST :8080 ─┐
                      ├─► chunk ─► hash ─► compress ─► block store + redb index
  you ──► gRPC :9090 ─┘

How it works

You can start the service from source or from Docker Compose. Either way the server boots, seeds the tenants from configuration, opens the redb index at data/index.redb, and begins serving both listeners. Data lands under data/blocks/ (sharded content-addressed blobs) unless overridden.

Usage

Option A — run from source

Requires Rust 1.80+ (see Installation):

git clone <your-repo-url> file-storage && cd file-storage
cargo run --release -- --config config/default.yaml

You should see the REST listener on 0.0.0.0:8080 and the gRPC listener on 0.0.0.0:9090.

Option B — run with Docker Compose

Starts a primary (8080/9090), a replica (host ports 8081/9091) and Prometheus (host port 9099, because 9090 on the host already belongs to the primary’s gRPC listener):

docker compose up --build

For the full walkthrough see Docker Compose. The rest of this page assumes Option A (or the primary container).

1. Log in

TOKEN=$(curl -s -X POST localhost:8080/api/v1/auth/login \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"username":"admin","password":"admin123"}' | jq -r .access_token)

2. Upload a file

head -c 4M /dev/urandom > /tmp/demo.bin
curl -s -X POST "localhost:8080/api/v1/files?name=demo.bin&tags=demo,quickstart" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/octet-stream' \
  --data-binary @/tmp/demo.bin | jq

Note the content_hash in the response — a 64-character hex BLAKE3 digest of your file.

3. Download it back and verify the hash

FILE_ID=<file_id from the upload response>

curl -s -D headers.txt -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  localhost:8080/api/v1/files/$FILE_ID -o /tmp/demo.out

grep -i x-content-hash headers.txt
b3sum /tmp/demo.out   # must equal the content_hash above

The download carries an X-Content-Hash header with the same 64-hex digest that content_hash returned at upload. The digest is BLAKE3, not SHA-256: sha256sum /tmp/demo.out will not match. If you have the b3sum utility installed (cargo install b3sum), its output matches byte-for-byte.

4. List files

curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  'localhost:8080/api/v1/files?limit=10' | jq

5. Verify the gRPC listener

gRPC serves reflection, so grpcurl can discover the API without any .proto files:

# list services
grpcurl -plaintext localhost:9090 list

# health check
grpcurl -plaintext \
  -d '{"service": "file_storage.v1.FileService"}' \
  localhost:9090 grpc.health.v1.Health/Check

Both should succeed; the health check returns {"status": "SERVING"}.

Examples

A minimal end-to-end script combining the steps above:

set -e
TOKEN=$(curl -s -X POST localhost:8080/api/v1/auth/login \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"username":"admin","password":"admin123"}' | jq -r .access_token)
echo hello-quickstart > /tmp/hello.txt
RESP=$(curl -s -X POST "localhost:8080/api/v1/files?name=hello.txt" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" --data-binary @/tmp/hello.txt)
ID=$(echo "$RESP" | jq -r .file_id)
curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" localhost:8080/api/v1/files/$ID

See also

  • Installation — building from source, systemd, release profile.
  • Docker — image layout, running a single container.
  • Your First File — the full upload/download/delete walkthrough, including multipart uploads, ranges, ETags and dedup.
  • REST API and gRPC API — complete endpoint reference.
  • Configuration reference — every key and default.
  • Authentication — tokens, roles and refresh rotation.

Installation

This page covers building file-storage from source, the release profile that produces the small production binary, running it as a systemd service, and an optional static-musl build. If you prefer containers, skip ahead to Docker; for a five-minute evaluation see Quickstart.

Overview

file-storage compiles to a single stripped binary of roughly 12 MB with no runtime dependencies beyond libc — no OpenSSL, no system protoc, no external database. The build intentionally keeps the toolchain footprint small: the protobuf compiler is vendored into the build via the protoc-bin-vendored crate, and all TLS is handled by rustls in-process.

How it works

cargo drives everything. The build.rs script compiles the protobuf definitions using a vendored protoc binary (so protoc does not need to be installed on the build host), and code generation happens entirely offline. The release profile applies aggressive optimisation settings that trade compile time for a compact, fast binary:

Profile settingValueEffect
opt-level3Maximum optimisation
ltothinCross-crate inlining without full-LTO build times
codegen-units1Better optimisation, slower compile
stripsymbolsStrips debug symbols from the binary

The measured result of cargo build --release is a ~11.7 MB binary.

Configuration

Requirements

RequirementDetail
Rust toolchain1.80 or newer, installed via rustup
Build space~1 GB for the cargo target directory
protocNot required — build.rs vendors it via protoc-bin-vendored
OpenSSLNot required — TLS uses rustls; only pkg-config may be needed in minimal container images

Build from source

git clone <your-repo-url> file-storage && cd file-storage
cargo build --release --locked
./target/release/file-storage --config config/default.yaml

The project enforces quality gates that you can reproduce locally:

cargo fmt --check          # formatting must be clean
cargo clippy --all-targets # 0 warnings expected
cargo test                 # 131 unit + 18 gRPC + 17 REST integration tests

Running as a systemd service

Create /etc/file-storage/ with your configuration, then install a unit such as:

# /etc/systemd/system/file-storage.service
[Unit]
Description=file-storage content-addressed file service
After=network-online.target
Wants=network-online.target

[Service]
Type=simple
User=file-storage
Group=file-storage
ExecStart=/usr/local/bin/file-storage --config /etc/file-storage/default.yaml
Restart=on-failure
RestartSec=5
StateDirectory=file-storage
Environment=RUST_LOG=info

# Hardening
NoNewPrivileges=true
ProtectSystem=strict
ReadWritePaths=/var/lib/file-storage
PrivateTmp=true

[Install]
WantedBy=multi-user.target

StateDirectory=file-storage makes systemd create and own /var/lib/file-storage, which is where the production configuration points storage.root, storage.temp_dir, database.path and encryption.keys_dir (see the production template in config/production.yaml). Enable and start:

sudo systemctl daemon-reload
sudo systemctl enable --now file-storage
systemctl status file-storage

Optional: static musl build

For a fully static binary (useful in scratch containers or minimal images), build against musl. This is documented as optional; the glibc build is the supported default.

sudo apt-get install musl-tools          # Debian/Ubuntu
rustup target add x86_64-unknown-linux-musl
cargo build --release --target x86_64-unknown-linux-musl
# binary: target/x86_64-unknown-linux-musl/release/file-storage

Examples

Post-install smoke checklist

Run through this list after any fresh install before declaring it healthy:

  1. GET /health returns {"status":"ok", ...} with the expected version and a rising uptime_secs.
  2. GET /ready returns {"status":"ok","database":"ok","storage":"ok"}.
  3. Login as a configured user succeeds and returns both an access and a refresh token.
  4. A small upload returns HTTP 201 with a content_hash; the same file downloaded again carries a matching X-Content-Hash header.
  5. GET /metrics exposes file_storage_* Prometheus metrics.
  6. grpcurl -plaintext localhost:9090 list shows the four file_storage.v1 services plus health and reflection.
  7. Journal logs (journalctl -u file-storage) show no error lines at info level.

See also

  • Quickstart — first commands after installing.
  • Docker — the containerised equivalent of this page.
  • Configuration reference — every key, default and FS_ env override.
  • Monitoring — metrics and dashboards for a running install.
  • TLS — enabling TLS on the gRPC listener and mTLS for replication.

Docker

This page explains the project Dockerfile, how to build and run a single container, and the runtime characteristics of the image (size, user, volumes, health probes, logging). For a multi-container primary/replica stack, continue to Docker Compose.

Overview

The image is a two-stage build: compilation happens in rust:1.83-slim, and the runtime stage is gcr.io/distroless/cc-debian12:nonroot. The final image contains only the ~12 MB binary and the bundled configuration — no shell, no package manager, no debug tools — and it runs as a non-root user. Total image size lands in the ~30 MB range. Because build.rs vendors the protobuf compiler through protoc-bin-vendored, neither the builder nor the runtime image needs a system protoc or OpenSSL.

How it works

The Dockerfile in the repository root:

# syntax=docker/dockerfile:1

# ---------- build stage ----------
# protoc is unnecessary on the build host: build.rs uses the vendored
# compiler from the `protoc-bin-vendored` crate.
FROM rust:1.83-slim AS build
WORKDIR /src
RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends pkg-config && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*
COPY Cargo.toml Cargo.lock* build.rs ./
COPY proto ./proto
COPY src ./src
RUN cargo build --release --locked 2>/dev/null || cargo build --release

# ---------- runtime stage ----------
FROM gcr.io/distroless/cc-debian12:nonroot
COPY --from=build /src/target/release/file-storage /usr/local/bin/file-storage
COPY --from=build /src/config /etc/file-storage
ENV RUST_LOG=info
EXPOSE 8080 9090
WORKDIR /var/lib/file-storage
VOLUME ["/var/lib/file-storage/data"]
ENTRYPOINT ["/usr/local/bin/file-storage", "--config", "/etc/file-storage/default.yaml"]

Key properties:

PropertyValue
Builder imagerust:1.83-slim (plus pkg-config only)
Runtime imagegcr.io/distroless/cc-debian12:nonroot
Listener portsEXPOSE 8080 (REST) and 9090 (gRPC)
Configurationbaked into /etc/file-storage/ (default, production templates)
Entry point/usr/local/bin/file-storage --config /etc/file-storage/default.yaml
Data volume/var/lib/file-storage/data (blocks, temp dir, redb index, keys)
Image size~30 MB range (12 MB binary + distroless cc base)
Process usernonroot (uid 65532)

Configuration

Override any configuration key with FS_-prefixed environment variables (double underscore for nesting), which merge on top of the baked-in YAML:

Environment variablePurpose
FS_SERVER__PORTREST port (default 8080)
FS_SERVER__GRPC_PORTgRPC port (default 9090)
FS_STORAGE__ROOTBlock store root inside the container
FS_DATABASE__PATHredb index path
FS_ENCRYPTION__KEYS_DIRPer-tenant key directory
FS_LOGGING__FORMATpretty or json logs

The full override syntax is documented in the Configuration reference.

Examples

Build the image

docker build -t file-storage:1.0.0 .

Run a single node with a persistent volume

docker volume create fs-data
docker run -d --name file-storage \
  -p 8080:8080 -p 9090:9090 \
  -v fs-data:/var/lib/file-storage/data \
  -e FS_LOGGING__FORMAT=json \
  file-storage:1.0.0

Volume permissions. The process runs as uid 65532 (nonroot). A freshly created named volume starts root-owned, so either initialise ownership once with a helper container, or prepare a bind mount with the right owner:

# one-time fix for an existing volume
docker run --rm -v fs-data:/data alpine chown -R 65532:65532 /data

For bind mounts, create the host directory and chown 65532:65532 it before starting the container. Fresh named volumes used from the first boot are simplest.

Health probes

The runtime exposes two unauthenticated endpoints on the REST port:

EndpointSuccess responseFailure behaviour
/health{"status":"ok","version":"...","uptime_secs":N}— (liveness)
/ready{"status":"ok","database":"ok","storage":"ok"}HTTP 503 while the database or storage root is unavailable

Use /health as the container liveness probe and /ready as the readiness probe:

curl -fsS localhost:8080/ready && echo ready

Logging

The service logs to stdout in pretty or json format (logging.format). Use the standard json-file driver with rotation, or any log shipper:

docker run -d --name file-storage \
  --log-driver json-file \
  --log-opt max-size=10m --log-opt max-file=5 \
  -p 8080:8080 -p 9090:9090 \
  -v fs-data:/var/lib/file-storage/data \
  file-storage:1.0.0

  • Docker Compose — primary + replica + Prometheus stack.
  • Quickstart — first commands against a running container.
  • Configuration reference — all FS_ environment overrides.
  • TLS — TLS for the gRPC listener and mTLS for replication peers.
  • Monitoring — scraping /metrics from the container.

Your First File

This page walks a single file through its entire lifecycle — upload, inspect, read back, cache-verify, partially read, soft-delete, purge — and then demonstrates the same operations over gRPC. All commands assume the service from Quickstart is running with the default development configuration.

Overview

Every file becomes a manifest in the index plus one or more content-addressed chunks in the block store. The upload response already tells you how the content was cut into chunks and whether any of them were duplicates. Reads are served through a three-level cache and verified against the BLAKE3 plaintext hash after every disk load, so the bytes you get back are provably the bytes you put in.

How it works

The lifecycle below maps one-to-one onto REST endpoints; each step shows the exact command and response. The gRPC equivalents operate on the same storage core — a file uploaded over REST is visible over gRPC and vice versa.

 login ─► upload ─► 201 manifest ─► meta ─► download ─► 304/206 ─► delete ─► 404 ─► purge
                                                              │
              dedup: same bytes re-uploaded ──► dedup_chunks = chunk_count

Usage

1. Log in

TOKEN=$(curl -s -X POST localhost:8080/api/v1/auth/login \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"username":"admin","password":"admin123"}' | jq -r .access_token)

2. Upload — multipart variant

echo "quarterly report" > /tmp/report.txt
curl -s -X POST localhost:8080/api/v1/files \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  -F name=report.txt \
  -F tags=q1,finance \
  -F file=@/tmp/report.txt | jq

3. Upload — raw body variant

The raw variant streams the body directly (no multipart parsing) and takes metadata as query parameters:

curl -s -X POST "localhost:8080/api/v1/files?name=report-raw.txt&mime=text/plain&tags=q1" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/octet-stream' \
  --data-binary @/tmp/report.txt | jq

4. The upload response, field by field

{
  "file_id": "01930000-0000-7000-8000-0000000000ab",
  "name": "report.txt",
  "mime": "text/plain",
  "size": 17,
  "size_stored": 17,
  "chunk_count": 1,
  "content_hash": "5f2c9c4b8d3e1a7f6b0c4d8e2f9a1b3c5d7e9f0a2b4c6d8e0f2a4b6c8d0e2f4a",
  "dedup_chunks": 0,
  "dedup_bytes": 0,
  "created_at_ms": 1762700000000
}
FieldMeaning
file_idUUID of the manifest; use it in every subsequent request
nameLogical name you supplied; uploading the same name again replaces the old file (soft delete of the previous manifest)
mimeMIME type (from the field, or inferred/default)
sizeOriginal byte count
size_storedBytes actually on disk after compression; equal to size here because zstd did not shrink this tiny payload (it is stored verbatim). With encryption enabled each blob additionally carries 28 bytes of nonce + tag
chunk_countNumber of content-defined chunks (FastCDC, avg 32 KiB)
content_hash64-hex BLAKE3 digest of the original bytes
dedup_chunksChunks that were already stored and re-used this upload
dedup_bytesBytes not written because they were duplicates
created_at_msEpoch milliseconds

5. Inspect metadata with chunks

curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  "localhost:8080/api/v1/files/$ID/meta?chunks=true" | jq

With ?chunks=true the response includes a chunks array where each entry reports offset, size_original, size_stored, hash, compressed and encrypted. The hash values are the BLAKE3 plaintext digests of each chunk; compressed=false appears when a chunk was stored verbatim because zstd did not shrink it.

6. Download and verify

curl -s -D headers.txt -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  localhost:8080/api/v1/files/$ID -o /tmp/report.out
grep -iE 'etag|x-content-hash' headers.txt
b3sum /tmp/report.out

The response carries ETag: "<content_hash>", Cache-Control: private, max-age=3600, Content-Disposition: inline; filename="report.txt" and X-Content-Hash: <content_hash>. The hash is BLAKE3 — sha256sum will not match; use b3sum (installable with cargo install b3sum) or simply compare against the content_hash returned at upload.

7. Conditional download (304)

ETAG=$(grep -i '^etag:' headers.txt | cut -d' ' -f2 | tr -d '\r')
curl -s -D- -o /dev/null -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  -H "If-None-Match: $ETAG" \
  localhost:8080/api/v1/files/$ID

Because the ETag still matches, the server returns 304 Not Modified with ETag and Cache-Control: private, max-age=3600 and no body.

8. Partial download (206)

curl -s -D- -o /dev/null -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  -H 'Range: bytes=0-7' \
  localhost:8080/api/v1/files/$ID/range

This returns 206 Partial Content with Content-Range: bytes 0-7/17 and the first eight bytes. The end offset is inclusive; a suffix form (Range: bytes=-4) and multi-range requests (first range wins) are also supported. An unsatisfiable range yields 416 with Content-Range: bytes */17.

9. Soft delete

curl -s -X DELETE -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" localhost:8080/api/v1/files/$ID -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n'
# 204
curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n' -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  localhost:8080/api/v1/files/$ID
# 404

A soft delete only sets a tombstone (deleted_at_ms); the chunks remain on disk until garbage collection removes them after the grace period (86400 s by default).

10. Purge (admin)

curl -s -X DELETE -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  "localhost:8080/api/v1/files/$ID?purge=true" -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n'
# 204 (admin only)

Purge hard-deletes the manifest immediately; blob storage is reclaimed by the next GC pass.

11. Observe deduplication

Upload the same file twice and compare responses:

curl -s -X POST "localhost:8080/api/v1/files?name=dedup-a.txt" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" --data-binary @/tmp/report.txt | jq '.chunk_count, .dedup_chunks'
curl -s -X POST "localhost:8080/api/v1/files?name=dedup-b.txt" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" --data-binary @/tmp/report.txt | jq '.chunk_count, .dedup_chunks, .dedup_bytes'

The second upload reports dedup_chunks == chunk_count and dedup_bytes == size: every chunk was already stored, so no new bytes were written — both files now reference the same physical blobs.

Examples

The same lifecycle over gRPC

Discover and log in with grpcurl (reflection means no local proto files are needed):

grpcurl -plaintext localhost:9090 list
grpcurl -plaintext -d '{"username":"admin","password":"admin123"}' \
  localhost:9090 file_storage.v1.AuthService/Login

grpcurl -plaintext -H "authorization: Bearer $JWT" \
  -d '{"limit": 10}' localhost:9090 file_storage.v1.FileService/ListFiles

grpcurl -plaintext -H "authorization: Bearer $JWT" \
  -d '{"file_id": "'$ID'", "include_chunks": true}' \
  localhost:9090 file_storage.v1.FileService/GetManifest

grpcurl -plaintext -H "authorization: Bearer $JWT" \
  -d '{"file_id": "'$ID'", "purge": false}' \
  localhost:9090 file_storage.v1.FileService/DeleteFile

Streaming upload/download is easiest through the Rust SDK:

use file_storage::grpc::FileStorageClient;
use file_storage::grpc::generated::pb;

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
    let mut client = FileStorageClient::connect("http://127.0.0.1:9090").await?;
    client.login("admin", "admin123").await?;          // token stored on the client

    let payload = std::fs::read("/tmp/report.txt")?;
    let header = pb::UploadHeader {
        name: "report.txt".into(),
        mime_type: "text/plain".into(),
        tags: vec!["q1".into()],
        ..Default::default()
    };
    let resp = client.upload(header, std::io::Cursor::new(payload)).await?;
    let file_id = resp.manifest.unwrap().file_id;

    let mut sink = Vec::new();
    let header = client.download(&file_id, &mut sink).await?;   // full read-back
    println!("{} bytes, hash {:?}", sink.len(), header.original_hash);
    Ok(())
}

The client streams 1 MiB pieces, retries transient failures (3 attempts, exponential backoff), and attaches the JWT as authorization metadata automatically after login.
