Production Configuration

config/production.yaml is the batteries-included template for a real deployment: TLS on the gRPC listener, encryption at rest, argon2id password hashes, replication enabled and JSON logging. This page walks through the file section by section and lists the three secrets you must replace before rollout. There is no hot reload — plan a rolling restart for any change.

Overview

The template differs from config/default.yaml in these ways: server.grpc_tls.enabled=true with mTLS (client_ca_path set), paths under /var/lib/file-storage/data/, auth.users reduced to a single admin with password_hash (no plaintext), encryption.enabled=true, higher rate limits (per_second: 1000, burst: 2000), replication enabled against http://replica:8080, and logging.format: json. Everything else (chunking, caches, retention, limits) matches the defaults.

How it works

Secrets to replace before first boot — the file’s header comment names them too:

PlaceholderReplace withHow
auth.jwt_secret: REPLACE_WITH_OPENSSL_RAND_HEX_3264 hex charsopenssl rand -hex 32
auth.users[].password_hashargon2id PHC stringfile-storage hash-password --password '…'
replication.api_key: REPLACE_WITH_CLUSTER_API_KEYshared peer secretopenssl rand -hex 24

Boot the server with the template and rely on the validation rules (secret length, cert paths present, user credentials) to catch mistakes early; see the Configuration reference for every key.

Configuration

Section-by-section walkthrough of the actual file:

server:
  host: "0.0.0.0"
  port: 8080
  grpc_port: 9090
  grpc_tls:
    enabled: true
    cert_path: "/etc/file-storage/tls/server.crt"
    key_path: "/etc/file-storage/tls/server.key"
    client_ca_path: "/etc/file-storage/tls/client-ca.crt"   # mTLS for peers

TLS terminates at the gRPC listener; clients connecting without TLS fail immediately. With client_ca_path set, peer certificates must chain to that CA — this is how replicas authenticate each other. Certificate provisioning is covered in TLS.

storage:
  root: "/var/lib/file-storage/data/blocks"
  temp_dir: "/var/lib/file-storage/data/tmp"
database:
  path: "/var/lib/file-storage/data/index.redb"
encryption:
  enabled: true
  keys_dir: "/var/lib/file-storage/data/keys"

Put storage.root, database.path and encryption.keys_dir on a persistent volume — the block store, the redb index and the per-tenant AES keys must survive restarts together; losing the keys directory with encryption.enabled=true makes all blobs unreadable (see Secrets).

auth:
  jwt_secret: "REPLACE_WITH_OPENSSL_RAND_HEX_32"
  access_ttl_secs: 900
  refresh_ttl_secs: 604800
  users:
    - username: admin
      password_hash: "$argon2id$v=19$m=65536,t=3,p=4$…"
      tenant_id: "01930000-0000-7000-8000-000000000001"
      role: admin

Exactly one admin user with an argon2id hash — no plaintext passwords in production files. The example hash in the template is for admin123 and exists only so the file parses; generate your own.

rate_limit:
  per_second: 1000
  burst: 2000
replication:
  enabled: true
  mode: async
  target_url: "http://replica:8080"
  api_key: "REPLACE_WITH_CLUSTER_API_KEY"
  queue_capacity: 50000
retention:
  gc_mode: refcount
  grace_period_secs: 86400
limits:
  max_file_size: 10737418240
  max_body: 1073741824
logging:
  format: json
  level: info

Retention tuning: lower grace_period_secs (default 86400 = 24 h) if you reclaim space faster, or raise it to widen the undelete window; gc_interval_secs 300 keeps background sweeps frequent. queue_capacity: 50000 widens the replication WAL buffer for bursty write loads. JSON logs with level: info are grep- and ingest-friendly; the Monitoring page maps them to metrics.

Examples

Generate the three secrets and inject them:

openssl rand -hex 32            # -> auth.jwt_secret
./file-storage hash-password --password 'S3cret-hunter2'   # -> password_hash
openssl rand -hex 24            # -> replication.api_key (same value on both peers)

Deployment targets: the repository’s docker-compose.yml runs a primary (8080/9090) and a replica (8081/9091) with Prometheus on host port 9099, plus a grpc-test profile with grpcurl — see Docker Compose. For cluster deployments, Kubernetes mounts the YAML and the TLS/key material as secrets/volumes and sets overrides via FS_* environment variables (Environment variables).

Finish with the hardening checklist — it has 50+ items (firewalling /internal/*, secret permissions, mTLS, backups, audit review cadence) in Hardening checklist, and the Threat model explains what each control buys you.

See also

  • Configuration reference — every key with constraints
  • Environment variables — FS_* overrides
  • Secrets — jwt_secret / api_key / key-file handling
  • TLS — certificate generation and rotation
  • Docker Compose, Kubernetes
  • Monitoring, Hardening checklist

Secrets

file-storage keeps exactly four classes of secret: the JWT signing secret, the cluster (peer) API key, per-tenant AES-256-GCM key files, and user password hashes. There is no external KMS integration — secrets live in config, environment variables or key files under encryption.keys_dir, so this page focuses on handling and rotating them with the mechanisms that actually exist.

Overview

SecretWhere it livesUsed for
auth.jwt_secretconfig / FS_AUTH__JWT_SECRETSigning & verifying HS256 access tokens
replication.api_keyconfig / FS_REPLICATION__API_KEY/internal/* X-Api-Key and gRPC x-api-key peer auth
Per-tenant AES keys<encryption.keys_dir>/<tenant>.key (hex, 0600)Encrypting/decrypting blobs at rest
password_hashauth.users[].password_hashargon2id login verification

How it works

The JWT secret and peer API key are read once at startup and held in memory; the API key is compared with a constant-time equality check on every internal request. Tenant key files are created on first use: when a tenant’s first blob is encrypted, 32 random bytes are written hex-encoded to <keys_dir>/<tenant-simple>.key with 0600 permissions (mode is enforced at creation). Key files are cached in memory per tenant after first load.

Configuration

Relevant keys (full constraints in the Configuration reference):

  • auth.jwt_secret — ≥ 16 chars at boot; treat as root-equivalent.
  • replication.api_key — must be identical on primary and replica.
  • encryption.enabled + encryption.keys_dir — when enabled, the keys directory is mandatory and must be on a persistent volume; losing it makes every encrypted blob unreadable.
  • auth.users[].password_hash — argon2id PHC from file-storage hash-password --password '…'.

Usage

Rotating auth.jwt_secret

  1. Generate openssl rand -hex 32, set it as the new auth.jwt_secret (or FS_AUTH__JWT_SECRET).
  2. Restart. All previously issued access tokens become invalid immediately — verification uses the new secret.
  3. Clients simply log in again. Refresh tokens are not derived from the JWT secret; those held in memory remain valid across the rotation, but they are cleared by the restart itself (the store is in-memory), so in practice a rotation-with-restart logs everyone out.

Rotating the peer api_key

Both sides must agree; the key is used for X-Api-Key (REST /internal/*) and x-api-key (gRPC ReplicationService) metadata:

  1. Update replication.api_key on the replica, restart it.
  2. Update the primary and restart it. Between the two restarts replication fails (async: queued with backoff; sync: uploads get 502 replication_error) and resumes automatically once both sides match.

Per-tenant AES keys

  • Naming/creation: <keys_dir>/<tenant-simple>.key (UUID without dashes), hex-encoded 32 bytes, written 0600 on the tenant’s first encrypted upload. Verify permissions with key_file_permissions_ok semantics — owner-only (0600) is expected.
  • Diagnostics: the key file’s modification time is exposed as key_age_ms (epoch ms) via the encryptor for tooling/audits.
  • Rotation is a manual procedure by design (no KMS): the key file name is the tenant id, so a rotation means creating a new key file for the tenant. Old blobs were encrypted under the old key and become unreadable unless re-encrypted. The supported procedure:
    1. Pause writes for the tenant.
    2. Move/rename the current key file (e.g. <tenant>.key.old) so a fresh key is generated on next use.
    3. Re-encrypt data: download each file through the API with the old key still loadable (e.g. run the old key path via a temporary keys_dir), then re-upload it under the new key. There is no automated re-encryption command.
    4. Trigger GC (POST /api/v1/admin/gc) to reclaim blobs that are no longer referenced by any manifest.
    5. Restore 0600 on the new key file and keep backups of both keys until step 3 completes.

File permissions and env caveats

  • chmod 600 on the config file and key files; run the service as a dedicated user with read access only where needed.
  • Secrets in environment variables are visible in /proc/<pid>/environ to the process owner and to anyone who can inspect container runtime state, and FS_* values in compose files tend to leak via .env files committed to git. Prefer mounted secret files (K8s Secrets as volumes, docker secrets) where the deployment platform supports it; the config file itself is currently the primary secret carrier, so protect its mount.
  • Never log the secret values; the service does not print config secrets, but crash logs with debug verbosity may include surrounding context.

Examples

Locking down a production keys directory:

sudo install -d -m 700 -o file-storage -g file-storage /var/lib/file-storage/data/keys
sudo -u file-storage ./file-storage hash-password --password 'S3cret-hunter2'
sudo chmod 600 /etc/file-storage/config.yaml /etc/file-storage/tls/server.key

Checking key-file exposure and age:

stat -c '%a %U %n' /var/lib/file-storage/data/keys/*.key   # expect 600
ls -l --time-style=full-iso /var/lib/file-storage/data/keys/ # mtime = key_age_ms source

See also

  • Configuration reference — key constraints (jwt_secret ≥ 16 chars)
  • Production — the three placeholders to replace
  • Environment variables — env-visibility trade-offs
  • Encryption at Rest — how tenant keys are applied
  • Threat model, Hardening checklist
  • Backup & Restore — including keys in backups

Docker Compose

The repository ships a docker-compose.yml that stands up a two-node stack: a primary with replication enabled, a replica that pulls from it, and a Prometheus instance scraping both. This page walks through the file exactly as it ships, verifies end-to-end replication, and lists the pitfalls to avoid. (The single-container equivalent is covered on the Docker page in the Getting Started section.)

Overview

The compose stack demonstrates the recommended deployment shape — one writable primary plus one read replica — with everything wired through FS_ environment overrides on top of the baked-in configuration. Both services keep their data in named volumes, so docker compose down preserves the block store and index across restarts.

flowchart LR
    C[client] -->|"REST :8080 / gRPC :9090"| P[primary]
    P -->|"REST :8081 / gRPC :9091"| R[replica]
    P -->|"async WAL batch 64 ops / 5 s"| R
    R -.->|"pull missing blobs on demand"| P
    PR[prometheus :9099] -->|"/metrics on :8080"| P
    PR -->|"/metrics on :8080"| R

Figure 1: the compose topology — primary ships operations to the replica; Prometheus scrapes both REST ports.

How it works

Services

ServiceImagePurpose
primaryfile-storage:1.0.0 (built from .)Writable node, replication producer
replicafile-storage:1.0.0Read-only companion, replication consumer
prometheusprom/prometheus:v2.53.0Scrapes both nodes’ /metrics
grpcurlfullstorydev/grpcurl:v1.9.1One-shot gRPC discovery check (profile grpc-test)

Ports

ServiceContainer portHost portNote
primary REST80808080Public entry point
primary gRPC90909090Reflection + health enabled
replica REST80808081Host 8080 is taken by the primary
replica gRPC90909091Host 9090 is taken by the primary
prometheus90909099Host 9090 belongs to the primary’s gRPC listener

Prometheus scrapes both application containers on their container port 8080 (/metrics is served by the REST listener); the remapped host port only affects your browser.

Environment

Primary (replication producer):

VariableValueMeaning
FS_REPLICATION__ENABLEDtrueStart the WAL shipper
FS_REPLICATION__MODEasyncBatch 64 ops / flush every 5 s; uploads never block
FS_REPLICATION__TARGET_URLhttp://replica:8080Ship operations to the replica’s REST port
FS_REPLICATION__API_KEYcluster-api-key-change-meShared secret for /internal/* and the gRPC ReplicationService
FS_STORAGE__ROOT / FS_DATABASE__PATH / FS_ENCRYPTION__KEYS_DIR/var/lib/file-storage/data/...Keep all state on the named volume

Replica (replication consumer):

VariableValueMeaning
FS_REPLICATION__ENABLEDfalseThe replica does not ship further
FS_REPLICATION__UPSTREAM_URLhttp://primary:8080Where to pull missing blobs for on-demand repair
FS_RETENTION__GC_MODEreachabilityReplica GC keeps only blobs referenced by live manifests
FS_REPLICATION__API_KEYsame as primaryMust match on both sides

Both nodes write FS_LOGGING__FORMAT=json with RUST_LOG=info.

Configuration

Two things must be changed before this stack touches real data:

  1. FS_REPLICATION__API_KEY must be replaced on both services. The shipped value cluster-api-key-change-me is a placeholder; generate one with openssl rand -hex 24 and set it identically on primary and replica (or manage it via secrets — see Secrets).
  2. The demo users in the baked-in default.yaml (including admin/admin123) remain active unless you mount your own configuration or override FS_AUTH__USERS. For anything beyond a demo, bake a production configuration into the image or mount one over /etc/file-storage/default.yaml.

Volumes fs_primary and fs_replica hold blocks, temp files, the redb index and per-tenant keys, keeping primary and replica data strictly separated.

Examples

Start, verify, stop

# start the stack (builds the image first)
docker compose up --build -d

# one-shot gRPC discovery against the primary (profile grpc-test)
docker compose run --rm grpcurl
# runs: grpcurl -plaintext -max-time 5 primary:9090 list

Verify replication end to end:

# 1. log in on the primary
TOKEN=$(curl -s -X POST localhost:8080/api/v1/auth/login \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"username":"admin","password":"admin123"}' | jq -r .access_token)

# 2. upload a file on the primary
RESP=$(curl -s -X POST "localhost:8080/api/v1/files?name=compose-demo.txt" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" --data-binary @/etc/hostname)

# 3. wait for the async flush interval (flush_interval_ms = 5000)
sleep 6

# 4. the file is visible on the replica (host port 8081)
curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" 'localhost:8081/api/v1/files?limit=5' | jq

Stop and downgrade:

docker compose stop        # containers stop, volumes kept
docker compose start       # resume
docker compose down        # remove containers; named volumes are kept
docker compose down -v     # WARNING: also deletes fs_primary and fs_replica

The replica stays independently readable while the primary is down (serve reads from host port 8081); it simply receives no new operations until the primary returns.

Common pitfalls

PitfallSymptomFix
API key mismatchreplica returns 401 on /internal/*; replication_failures_total climbsSet the same FS_REPLICATION__API_KEY on both services
Shipper sends to the wrong portreplication_error / UNAVAILABLE, nothing replicatestarget_url must point at the replica’s REST port (http://replica:8080), not 9090
Expecting instant replicationnew file missing on the replicaAsync mode flushes every flush_interval_ms (5 s); wait at least one interval, or use mode: sync
Port clash in the browserPrometheus unreachable on :9090It is published on host port 9099
Deleting volumes by accidentdata gone after down -vBack up before destructive commands (see Backup & Restore)
Replica GC confusionblobs vanish on the replicaReachability mode is correct for replicas; refcount mode is for primaries

See also

  • Configuration reference — every FS_ override used above.
  • Scaling — extending this topology to many shards.
  • Replication Ops — resync, repair and monitoring of the shipper.
  • TLS — adding mTLS to the replication channel.
  • Secrets — managing the replication API key.
  • Runbook — operating procedures for the running stack.

Kubernetes

This page provides a complete, working set of manifests for running file-storage on Kubernetes: a Secret for credentials, a headless Service, a StatefulSet with a per-pod PVC, and a cluster Service. The default topology is a single writable node; scaling out to three replicas (independent nodes, each with its own data) is shown with its caveats. (The two-node Docker Compose stack is described on the Docker Compose page.)

Overview

file-storage is a stateful, single-process service: all state (block store, redb index, per-tenant keys) lives under one data directory, and the redb index permits exactly one writer per database file. The natural Kubernetes mapping is therefore a StatefulSet with one pod per storage volume — stable identity, stable storage, no shared-writer risk. External TLS terminates at the ingress; inside the cluster the service speaks plain HTTP.

How it works

flowchart LR
    I[Ingress / LB<br/>TLS termination] -->|REST :8080| S[Service fs-storage]
    S --> P0[fs-storage-0]
    S --> P1[fs-storage-1]
    S --> P2[fs-storage-2]
    P0 --- V0[(PVC fs-data-fs-storage-0)]
    P1 --- V1[(PVC fs-data-fs-storage-1)]
    P2 --- V2[(PVC fs-data-fs-storage-2)]

Figure 1: each StatefulSet pod owns its own PVC and its own independent data set.

Important consequence: multiple replicas of the StatefulSet are independent storage nodes, not a shared-disk cluster. There is no shared-block-store mode — each node owns its data directory, and cross-node duplication is achieved with replication, not with NFS or a shared filesystem (see Scaling for shard topologies and Replication Ops for operations).

Configuration

The manifest below expresses the whole deployment in one block:

  • Secret fs-secrets carries the JWT secret, replication API key and the admin password (all injected as FS_* overrides).
  • Headless Service fs-storage-headless gives pods stable DNS names; the normal Service fs-storage load-balances REST traffic.
  • StatefulSet fs-storage uses volumeClaimTemplates so every pod gets its own PVC mounted at /var/lib/file-storage/data; the readiness probe hits /ready, the liveness probe /health.
  • Ports 8080 (REST) and 9090 (gRPC) are declared on the container.
apiVersion: v1
kind: Secret
metadata:
  name: fs-secrets
type: Opaque
stringData:
  jwt-secret: "REPLACE_WITH_OPENSSL_RAND_HEX_32"
  replication-api-key: "REPLACE_WITH_OPENSSL_RAND_HEX_24"
  admin-password: "REPLACE_ME"
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: fs-storage-headless
  labels: { app: fs-storage }
spec:
  clusterIP: None
  selector: { app: fs-storage }
  ports:
    - { name: rest, port: 8080, targetPort: 8080 }
    - { name: grpc, port: 9090, targetPort: 9090 }
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: fs-storage
  labels: { app: fs-storage }
spec:
  selector: { app: fs-storage }
  ports:
    - { name: rest, port: 8080, targetPort: 8080 }
    - { name: grpc, port: 9090, targetPort: 9090 }
---
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: StatefulSet
metadata:
  name: fs-storage
spec:
  serviceName: fs-storage-headless
  replicas: 1            # single-node default; 3 is possible (see notes)
  selector:
    matchLabels: { app: fs-storage }
  template:
    metadata:
      labels: { app: fs-storage }
      annotations:
        prometheus.io/scrape: "true"
        prometheus.io/port: "8080"
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: file-storage
          image: file-storage:1.0.0
          ports:
            - { containerPort: 8080, name: rest }
            - { containerPort: 9090, name: grpc }
          env:
            - name: FS_SERVER__PORT
              value: "8080"
            - name: FS_SERVER__GRPC_PORT
              value: "9090"
            - name: FS_STORAGE__ROOT
              value: /var/lib/file-storage/data/blocks
            - name: FS_STORAGE__TEMP_DIR
              value: /var/lib/file-storage/data/tmp
            - name: FS_DATABASE__PATH
              value: /var/lib/file-storage/data/index.redb
            - name: FS_ENCRYPTION__KEYS_DIR
              value: /var/lib/file-storage/data/keys
            - name: FS_AUTH__JWT_SECRET
              valueFrom: { secretKeyRef: { name: fs-secrets, key: jwt-secret } }
            - name: FS_LOGGING__FORMAT
              value: json
          readinessProbe:
            httpGet: { path: /ready, port: 8080 }
            initialDelaySeconds: 3
            periodSeconds: 10
          livenessProbe:
            httpGet: { path: /health, port: 8080 }
            initialDelaySeconds: 5
            periodSeconds: 15
          resources:
            requests: { cpu: "250m", memory: 256Mi }
            limits:   { cpu: "2",    memory: 2Gi }
          volumeMounts:
            - name: fs-data
              mountPath: /var/lib/file-storage/data
  volumeClaimTemplates:
    - metadata:
        name: fs-data
      spec:
        accessModes: ["ReadWriteOnce"]
        resources:
          requests:
            storage: 50Gi

Notes on the manifest

  • readinessProbe targets /ready, which returns 503 until the database and storage root check out — pods do not receive traffic before they can serve.
  • The image runs as the distroless nonroot user (uid 65532); most CSI-provisioned volumes are writable by any uid by default. If your storage class enforces root ownership, set the pod security context (fsGroup: 65532).
  • User accounts: the baked-in configuration applies unless you supply your own. Set user credentials via your own config file mounted over /etc/file-storage/default.yaml (recommended in production).

Ingress

  • REST: terminate TLS at the ingress (nginx, ALB, Traefik…) and forward plain HTTP to the fs-storage Service on 8080. There is no TLS stack in the REST listener by design — internal traffic is plain HTTP inside the cluster.
  • gRPC: use a gRPC-capable ingress (or the same ingress with HTTP/2 pass-through / grpc backend protocol) targeting port 9090, or expose 9090 via NodePort/LoadBalancer. For TLS on the gRPC listener itself, see TLS.

Examples

Deploy and verify

kubectl apply -f file-storage.yaml
kubectl get pods -l app=fs-storage
kubectl rollout status statefulset/fs-storage

# smoke test through a port-forward
kubectl port-forward svc/fs-storage 8080:8080
curl -s localhost:8080/ready && echo
curl -s -X POST localhost:8080/api/v1/auth/login \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"username":"admin","password":"admin123"}' | jq -r .access_token

Upgrade strategy

With volumeClaimTemplates, each pod owns a dedicated RWO PVC, so the default RollingUpdate works pod-by-pod. Two caveats matter:

  1. Shared PVC: if you instead attach one RWO PVC via claimName to multiple pods, a rolling update stalls — the new pod cannot mount the volume while the old one holds it. Use strategy.type: Recreate, or provision ReadWriteMany storage.
  2. replicas > 1: every pod is an independent node with its own (initially empty) data set. Point replication at the pod DNS names (fs-storage-0.fs-storage-headless...) and treat the pods as shards, not as interchangeable workers — see Scaling.

See also

  • Scaling — sharding, load balancing and capacity planning.
  • TLS — gRPC server TLS and mTLS for replication.
  • Configuration reference — every FS_ override.
  • Secrets — handling JWT secrets and API keys outside git.
  • Backup & Restore — protecting the PVC contents.
  • Runbook — operating procedures once deployed.

Scaling

This page describes how file-storage scales horizontally: sharding into primary/replica pairs, routing tenants to shards, adding read capacity, and the load-balancer setup for both transports. It also gives the capacity math you need for sizing, and the built-in limits you should watch at scale.

Overview

A single file-storage node already saturates typical disks for many workloads (measured: 119 MB/s upload over HTTP on a small 2-vCPU sandbox, ~1.1 GB/s warm downloads). Scale out by sharding: each shard is a stateful primary + replica pair that owns a slice of tenants. There is no shared-storage mode — horizontal growth comes from adding shards, not from making more processes share one disk.

How it works

Shard topology

flowchart TB
    LB["Load balancer<br/>(route by tenant_id)"]
    LB -->|"tenants A, B"| S0[shard 0<br/>primary 0]
    LB -->|"tenants C, D"| S1[shard 1<br/>primary 1]
    S0 --> R0[replica 0]
    S1 --> R1[replica 1]
    R0 -.->|"on-demand pull repair"| S0
    R1 -.->|"on-demand pull repair"| S1

Figure 1: each shard is a primary/replica pair; the balancer pins every tenant to one primary.

The routing contract is simple: all writes for a tenant go to that tenant’s primary. Consistent hashing or a small mapping table at the load balancer both work — the tenant UUID is present in every manifest and in the JWT (tenant claim), so an L7 proxy can route on it once authenticated.

Why a shared block store is NOT supported

Each node exclusively owns its data directory: the block store (storage.root) and the redb index (database.path) are local, and redb is a single-writer database file — one process must own it at a time. Attaching two nodes to the same volume over NFS or a shared block device is unsupported and unsafe (corruptible index, racing blob writes). The supported mechanism for multiple nodes holding the same content is replication: the primary ships operations to replicas, and replicas pull any missing blobs on demand. When you need more capacity, add shards; when you need redundancy, add replicas.

Read scaling

Replicas are full readers: they serve downloads, metadata and listings from their own cache and disk, exactly like a primary. A replica that receives a read for a blob it has not yet received ships a pull request to replication.upstream_url (on-demand pull repair) and serves the bytes after repair. Practical consequence: adding replicas adds linear read capacity and geographic proximity without touching the primary’s write path.

Configuration

Load balancing

TransportLayerGuidance
RESTL7Route by path/tenant; the API is plain HTTP with JWT bearer auth — terminate TLS at the balancer
gRPCL4Forward raw TCP to port 9090 (or HTTP/2 pass-through). Long-lived bidirectional streams (Upload/Download/ReplicateBatch) break if a proxy rebalances mid-stream

Keep sessions sticky per tenant for writes, never per chunk: one upload is a stream of independent chunk operations, but the manifest commit expects a single primary.

Capacity math

Sizing starts from three numbers:

  1. Dedup ratio — the fraction of stored bytes eliminated by re-use. Duplicate-heavy workloads routinely reach 2× or better; check file_storage_dedup_bytes_saved_total after a pilot.
  2. Chunk overhead — each chunk carries metadata in the redb index (ChunkMeta: refcount, blob size, timestamp) plus a row in the manifest. Per-chunk overhead is a few hundred bytes, ~1% at the 32 KiB average chunk size; negligible unless you store billions of tiny files.
  3. Encryption padding — with encryption enabled each blob adds 28 bytes (12-byte nonce + 16-byte GCM tag).

A rough sizing formula per shard: disk ≈ logical_bytes × (1 − dedup_ratio) × 1.01 + 28 B × chunk_count, plus headroom for GC grace-period retention and cache write bursts. Provision RAM for the caches (256 MiB L1 + 512 MiB L2 by default) on top of the working-set manifests.

Examples

Adding a shard

# shard 2 primary
FS_SERVER__PORT=8082 \
FS_REPLICATION__ENABLED=true \
FS_REPLICATION__TARGET_URL=http://replica2:8082 \
FS_REPLICATION__API_KEY=<cluster-key> \
file-storage --config /etc/file-storage/default.yaml

# shard 2 replica
FS_SERVER__PORT=8083 \
FS_REPLICATION__ENABLED=false \
FS_REPLICATION__UPSTREAM_URL=http://primary2:8082 \
FS_RETENTION__GC_MODE=reachability \
FS_REPLICATION__API_KEY=<cluster-key> \
file-storage --config /etc/file-storage/default.yaml

Then register tenant → shard 2 in your balancer. No data migration is needed for new tenants; existing tenants move only if you choose to migrate them (export and re-upload, or let the balancer keep them on their original shard).

Verifying routing

# a tenant-scoped login; the tenant claim decides which shard must serve it
curl -s localhost:8080/api/v1/files -H "Authorization: Bearer $ALICE_TOKEN" | jq .total

If a tenant’s requests reach the wrong shard, uploads succeed but are invisible to the intended shard’s replicas — always verify routing with a canary upload before cutting traffic over.

Limits to watch at scale

LimitWhereGuidance
redb single writerdatabase.pathOne process per index file — this is what makes shards, not replicas, the write-scaling unit
Per-tenant token bucketrate_limit.per_second / burst (gRPC)A hot tenant can exhaust its own bucket without affecting others; tune per workload
REST IP rate limitingtower_governor keyed by client IPBehind a balancer, key on the real client IP or raise the burst
Replication queuequeue_capacity 10000 opsSustained overload backpressures uploads; increase capacity or add shards
gRPC message ceiling256 MiB per messageStreamed uploads are chunk-wise, so this rarely binds

See also

  • TLS — securing the replication channel between shards.
  • Replication Ops — health, resync and repair of replicas.
  • Configuration reference — the knobs referenced above.
  • Runbook — shard failover procedures.

TLS

This page covers the three TLS surfaces of file-storage: terminating TLS for REST at your ingress (the REST listener itself is plain HTTP), native TLS and mTLS on the gRPC listener via server.grpc_tls, and the certificate generation recipes to run both. The implementation uses rustls with the ring crypto provider — there is no OpenSSL dependency.

Overview

file-storage follows the standard cluster pattern: plain HTTP inside the perimeter, TLS at the edge. The REST listener (axum) has no TLS stack by design and expects the ingress or load balancer to terminate TLS. The gRPC listener (tonic) supports server-side TLS and optional mutual TLS natively, which is the recommended way to protect the replication channel between primaries and replicas.

flowchart LR
    U[user] -->|"HTTPS"| I[Ingress / LB]
    I -->|"plain HTTP :8080"| A[REST listener]
    P[primary] -->|"gRPC :9090 with mTLS"| R[replica gRPC :9090]
    P --- C["client cert<br/>(CA-signed)"]

Figure 1: TLS terminates at the ingress for REST; the gRPC listener does its own TLS/mTLS.

How it works

REST: TLS termination at the ingress

Point your ingress at the REST port and let it own the certificate:

  • Kubernetes: an Ingress resource terminates TLS and forwards plain HTTP to the Service on port 8080.
  • Cloud load balancers: an ALB/NLB with an ACM certificate in front of 8080.
  • Self-hosted: nginx/Envoy/Caddy in front of the service.

Documented upstream-TLS options if you must encrypt further than the edge: place a TLS sidecar or local proxy (nginx, Envoy) next to the process and terminate there — the application itself speaks HTTP only. Because REST traffic carries Authorization: Bearer headers, never expose 8080 directly to untrusted networks.

gRPC: server TLS and mTLS

TLS for the gRPC listener is configured in one place:

server:
  grpc_tls:
    enabled: true
    cert_path: "/etc/file-storage/tls/server.crt"
    key_path: "/etc/file-storage/tls/server.key"
    client_ca_path: null     # set to enable mTLS: client certificates REQUIRED
  • enabled: true requires cert_path and key_path (validated at boot).
  • Setting client_ca_path to a PEM CA bundle switches the listener to mutual TLS: every client must present a certificate chaining to that CA. The production template enables both, using mTLS to lock the replication channel between peers.

TLS is implemented with rustls; the process pins the ring provider explicitly (installed once at startup), avoiding rustls provider ambiguity between tonic’s TLS feature and other crypto users of the crate.

Configuration

Generate a CA, server certificate and client certificates

mkdir -p tls && cd tls

# 1. CA
openssl genrsa -out ca.key 4096
openssl req -x509 -new -nodes -key ca.key -sha256 -days 3650 \
  -subj "/CN=file-storage-demo-ca" -out ca.crt

# 2. server certificate with SANs for every address clients will dial
openssl genrsa -out server.key 2048
openssl req -new -key server.key -subj "/CN=file-storage" -out server.csr
cat > server.ext <<'EOF'
subjectAltName = DNS:localhost, DNS:file-storage, DNS:replica, IP:127.0.0.1
extendedKeyUsage = serverAuth
EOF
openssl x509 -req -in server.csr -CA ca.crt -CAkey ca.key -CAcreateserial \
  -days 825 -sha256 -extfile server.ext -out server.crt

# 3. client certificate (for mTLS peers and SDKs)
openssl genrsa -out client.key 2048
openssl req -new -key client.key -subj "/CN=file-storage-client" -out client.csr
cat > client.ext <<'EOF'
extendedKeyUsage = clientAuth
EOF
openssl x509 -req -in client.csr -CA ca.crt -CAkey ca.key -CAcreateserial \
  -days 825 -sha256 -extfile client.ext -out client.crt

Deploy server.crt/server.key (and ca.crt when enabling mTLS) to server.grpc_tls.*_path on the server; distribute client.crt/client.key to every replication peer and trusted client. Protect private keys with file mode 0600 (see Secrets).

grpcurl over TLS

# TLS, skipping certificate verification (quick tests only)
grpcurl -insecure localhost:9090 list

# TLS with a pinned CA
grpcurl --tls-ca tls/ca.crt localhost:9090 list

# mTLS: present a client certificate as well
grpcurl --tls-ca tls/ca.crt --cert tls/client.crt --key tls/client.key \
  localhost:9090 file_storage.v1.AuthService/Login

Rust SDK over TLS

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
use file_storage::grpc::FileStorageClient;

let ca_pem = std::fs::read("tls/ca.crt")?;
let mut client = FileStorageClient::connect_tls("https://file-storage:9090", Some(&ca_pem)).await?;
client.login("admin", "admin123").await?;
}

connect_tls(endpoint, ca_pem) pins the given CA; pass None to rely on the platform trust store. With client_ca_path set on the server, the SDK’s underlying connector must also present a client certificate (configure it in your environment or front the SDK with a TLS-terminating local proxy).

Examples

Enable TLS on a standalone node

FS_SERVER__GRPC_TLS__ENABLED=true \
FS_SERVER__GRPC_TLS__CERT_PATH=/etc/file-storage/tls/server.crt \
FS_SERVER__GRPC_TLS__KEY_PATH=/etc/file-storage/tls/server.key \
FS_SERVER__GRPC_TLS__CLIENT_CA_PATH=/etc/file-storage/tls/ca.crt \
file-storage --config /etc/file-storage/default.yaml

Production checklist pointers

  1. REST behind TLS-terminating ingress only; never expose 8080 publicly.
  2. gRPC listener TLS enabled in every untrusted network segment.
  3. mTLS (client_ca_path) for the replication channel between primaries and replicas.
  4. Rotate server and client certificates before expiry; monitor key file ages.
  5. Verify with grpcurl --tls-ca and one connection attempt without a client certificate (must fail when mTLS is on).

See also

  • Configuration reference — server.grpc_tls keys and validation.
  • Secrets — key file storage and permissions.
  • Replication Ops — the channel the mTLS configuration protects.
  • Runbook — certificate rotation and expiry procedures.

Threat Model

This page identifies what file-storage must protect, who the adversaries are, which surfaces they can reach, and how each concrete threat is mitigated in the implemented code. It is deliberately concrete: every mitigation row cites the source file that implements it, so claims can be verified rather than trusted. The actionable summary of this page is the Hardening Checklist.

Overview

file-storage stores customer content, so the model treats data confidentiality and integrity as primary goals and availability as a strong secondary goal. The service ships without a KMS and without network-level TLS on REST, so several mitigations are explicitly the operator’s responsibility — the model below states exactly which ones are built in and which must be provided by the surrounding deployment.

How it works

Assets

AssetDescriptionNotes
File contentChunk blobs under storage.root (ab/cd/<hash>.bin)Encrypted at rest only when encryption.enabled
Metadata / manifestsredb tables files, name_index, tag_indexNames, tags, sizes, chunk hashes — never encrypted
Tenant identityredb tenants table, UUIDs in configsTenant UUID is part of key derivation — treat as immutable
JWT secretauth.jwt_secretCompromise = arbitrary token forgery
Per-tenant AES keys<keys_dir>/<tenant>.key files, 0600No KMS integration — files must be protected by the OS
Audit logredb audit tableAppend-only; tamper-evidence comes from external backup
Replication api_keyreplication.api_keyGuard for /internal/* and ReplicationService

Adversaries

AdversaryCapabilityPrimary defence
External attackerNetwork access to published portsAuth middleware, rate limits, TLS at ingress
Malicious insider with valid credentialsLegitimate JWT for their tenantTenant scoping in every handler; audit trail
Compromised replicaRuns replication code, holds api_keyLeast privilege: replica stores only what was shipped; mTLS limits forged ops
Curious operator with disk accessReads disks, backups, key filesPer-tenant encryption (blobs), key file 0600; metadata remains readable — documented residual risk
Compromised clientSteals a user’s tokenShort access TTL (900 s), refresh rotation, logout

Attack surface

 external ──► REST /api/v1/*            (JWT; rate-limited per client IP)
 external ──► gRPC :9090                (JWT interceptors; per-tenant token bucket)
 cluster  ──► REST /internal/*          (X-Api-Key; plain HTTP — firewall!)
 cluster  ──► gRPC ReplicationService   (x-api-key metadata; mTLS-capable)
 local    ──► data/blocks/, index.redb, keys/*.key, env vars / config file

Configuration

Mitigation matrix

ThreatMitigationImplementation reference
Plaintext HTTP internal traffic sniffed inside the clustergRPC replication channel runs with server.grpc_tls + client_ca_path (mTLS); firewall the REST /internal/* routes to replication peers onlysrc/grpc/server.rs, src/api/middleware/auth.rs
JWT replay until expiryShort access TTL (900 s default), single-use refresh rotation, jti claim, 5 s clock leewaysrc/security/auth.rs
Tenant enumeration via dedup (cross-tenant existence oracle)With encryption on, storage keys are BLAKE3(tenant_id ‖ plain_hash) — the same plaintext in two tenants lands under different keys and different ciphertexts, so nothing leakssrc/security/encryption.rs, src/core/pipeline.rs
Decompression bombsDecompression is bounded by the recorded size_original; payloads that do not shrink are stored verbatim with an explicit compressed=false flagsrc/core/compressor.rs
Timing-based username enumerationUnknown usernames burn the same argon2 cost via a dummy hash; plaintext comparison is constant-timesrc/security/auth.rs
Audit tampering by a compromised node/operatorAudit lives in the single redb file and is append-only from the API surface — but a disk-level attacker can edit it. Mitigate operationally: back up the database off-host and ship audit events externallysrc/security/audit.rs, src/storage/index_db.rs
DoS via large uploadslimits.max_file_size (default 10 GiB), limits.max_body HTTP body cap, per-IP REST rate limit, per-tenant gRPC token bucket, 256 MiB gRPC message ceilingsrc/api/router.rs, src/core/validator.rs, src/grpc/server.rs
Credential brute forceargon2id hashing (configurable via password_hash), rate limiting on both transports, login failures auditedsrc/security/auth.rs, src/api/middleware/rate_limit.rs
Cross-tenant data accessEvery handler calls tenant access checks; admins alone may cross tenants; name/tag indexes are tenant-prefixedsrc/security/tenant.rs, src/api/middleware/tenant.rs
Key file theftKey files created 0600 at first use; runtime image is distroless/nonrootsrc/security/encryption.rs, Dockerfile
Compromised primary serves poisoned chunk bytes (replica repair)M-07 verify-then-persist: pulled bytes are decrypted + decompressed and BLAKE3-verified against the chunk reference’s plain_hash BEFORE anything is persisted. Mismatches go to quarantine/ (size-bounded, boot-swept), the L2 entry is invalidated, file_storage_repair_hash_mismatch_total{source="repair"} ticks, and the caller gets Corruption. Nothing lands in the CAS or cache unverifiedsrc/storage/retrieval.rs::repair_chunk, src/storage/quarantine.rs
Forged replication operationsShared api_key required (x-api-key metadata / X-Api-Key header), constant-time comparison, mTLS peer certificatessrc/grpc/interceptors/api_key.rs, src/api/middleware/auth.rs
Filenames/metadata exposure on stolen disksResidual risk by design: manifests, names and tags live unencrypted in redb. Mitigate with disk/host-level encryptionsrc/storage/index_db.rs
Panic-based crashesPanic recovery on both stacks converts panics to error responsessrc/grpc/interceptors/recovery.rs, src/api/router.rs

Examples

Verifying two mitigations locally

# 1. username enumeration: same latency for unknown users
time curl -s -X POST localhost:8080/api/v1/auth/login \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"username":"no-such-user","password":"x"}' >/dev/null
time curl -s -X POST localhost:8080/api/v1/auth/login \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"username":"admin","password":"wrong"}' >/dev/null

# 2. tenant isolation: bob cannot see alice's files
BOB=$(curl -s -X POST localhost:8080/api/v1/auth/login -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"username":"bob","password":"bob123"}' | jq -r .access_token)
curl -s localhost:8080/api/v1/files -H "Authorization: Bearer $BOB" | jq .total   # 0

Residual risks (documented honestly)

  • Ciphertext-only replicas skip repair verification: a replica WITHOUT the tenant key (encryption.enabled=false) cannot decrypt what it pulls, so it cannot verify before persisting. Those repairs keep the legacy persist-then-serve order with a loud warn! per event and the file_storage_repair_ciphertext_only_total metric — run the documented compose mode (tenants replicated WITH their keys) to get full M-07 protection. Unverifiable ≠ hostile: these bytes are NOT quarantined, they are classified as untrusted-until-read-verified.
  • Refresh tokens are held in an in-memory store: a restart invalidates all sessions (a crude but effective logout-all), and un-flushed sessions are lost.
  • The audit log is not cryptographically chained; tamper evidence requires external shipping/backup.
  • Quota accounting is informational only (file_count, bytes_used) — there is no enforcement.
  • The login lockout map and RPC-governor map are bounded at 10 000 entries with oldest-first eviction; a sustained flood of >10 000 distinct usernames/tenants churns entries (bounded memory, no unbounded growth).

See also

  • Hardening Checklist — the operational countermeasures in checklist form.
  • Authentication — token lifecycle and role matrix.
  • Encryption at Rest — per-tenant AES-256-GCM design and limits.
  • Tenants — isolation guarantees and accounting.
  • Audit Log — event catalog and retention.
  • Configuration reference — every security-relevant knob.

Authentication

file-storage authenticates users with a login call and issues an HS256 JWT access token plus an opaque rotating refresh token. This page documents the token format, the lifecycle rules, how to present tokens on both transports, and the exact role matrix. The API-facing view of the same material lives in the REST API and gRPC API references.

Overview

Users are defined statically in configuration (auth.users). A successful login returns an access token (default TTL 900 seconds) and a refresh token (default TTL 7 days). Access tokens are self-contained HS256 JWTs verified in-process on every request; refresh tokens are single-use: each refresh revokes the presented token and issues a new pair (rotation). Three roles — admin, user, viewer — gate every operation, and every request is scoped to the tenant embedded in the token.

How it works

Token internals

The access token is signed with auth.jwt_secret using HS256 and carries exactly these claims:

{
  "sub": "alice",
  "tenant": "01930000-0000-7000-8000-000000000002",
  "role": "user",
  "iat": 1762700000,
  "exp": 1762700900,
  "jti": "01930000-0000-7000-8000-0000000000cd"
}
ClaimMeaning
subUsername; becomes the audit actor
tenantTenant UUID; the hard scope for every operation
roleadmin, user or viewer
iat / expIssued-at / expiry epoch seconds (default lifetime 900 s)
jtiUnique token ID (UUIDv7)

Verification uses a 5-second clock leeway for expiry. Because verification is purely local (HMAC), there is no revocation list for access tokens — a leaked access token is valid until exp. That is the reason for the short TTL.

Access vs refresh lifecycle

sequenceDiagram
    participant C as client
    participant S as AuthService
    C->>S: login(username, password)
    S-->>C: access_token (900 s) + refresh_token (7 d)
    C->>S: API calls with access_token
    Note over C,S: 15 minutes later...
    C->>S: refresh(refresh_token)
    S-->>C: NEW pair (old refresh token revoked)
    Note over S: refresh store is in-memory<br/>restart = all sessions gone

Figure 1: login issues a pair; each refresh rotates and revokes.

Key properties:

  • Refresh tokens are single-use — presenting a rotated token fails with 401.
  • The refresh store is an in-memory map; a process restart loses every session. “Logout everywhere” is therefore: restart the service, or rotate auth.jwt_secret (which also invalidates all outstanding access tokens).
  • Logout (POST /auth/logout with the refresh token) is idempotent: it returns 204 whether or not the token existed, and never fails.

Transport differences

RESTgRPC
Where the JWT goesAuthorization: Bearer <jwt> headerauthorization: Bearer <jwt> metadata
Login endpointPOST /api/v1/auth/loginfile_storage.v1.AuthService/Login
EnforcementAuth middleware on /api/v1 (except /auth/*)Unary interceptors; streaming handlers verify the JWT in-handler
Health/metrics/health, /ready, /metrics unauthenticatedgrpc.health.v1.Health + reflection unauthenticated

Configuration

Defining users

auth:
  jwt_secret: "change-me-in-production-0123456789abcdef"  # >= 16 chars
  access_ttl_secs: 900
  refresh_ttl_secs: 604800
  users:
    - username: admin
      password_hash: "$argon2id$v=19$m=65536,t=3,p=4$..."   # preferred
      tenant_id: "01930000-0000-7000-8000-000000000001"
      role: admin
  • password_hash (argon2id PHC string) is preferred in production. Generate one with file-storage hash-password --password '...'.
  • password (plaintext) exists for development convenience only; it must not appear in production configuration.
  • Each user maps to exactly one tenant and one role; cross-tenant access requires the admin role.
  • Every login attempt is audited: auth.login.success and auth.login.failure events record the username and client IP (see Audit Log).
  • Unknown usernames consume the same argon2 verification time as known ones (dummy hash), removing a timing side channel for username enumeration.

Examples

Role matrix

Every operation is gated by role; cross-tenant access is admin-only in all rows.

Operationadminuserviewer
Upload (POST /files, FileService/Upload)yesyesno
Download (GET /files/:id, Download)yesyesyes
Metadata (GET /files/:id/meta, GetManifest)yesyesyes
List (GET /files, ListFiles)yesyesyes
Soft delete (DELETE /files/:id, DeleteFile)yesyesno
Hard purge (DELETE /files/:id?purge=true)yesnono
Tenants (POST/GET /tenants)yesnono
GC trigger (POST /admin/gc)yesnono
Audit query (GET /audit)yesnono
Resync (POST /admin/resync/:id)yesnono

gRPC login and use

JWT=$(grpcurl -plaintext -d '{"username":"admin","password":"admin123"}' \
  localhost:9090 file_storage.v1.AuthService/Login | jq -r .access_token)

grpcurl -plaintext -H "authorization: Bearer $JWT" \
  -d '{"limit": 10}' localhost:9090 file_storage.v1.FileService/ListFiles

The Rust SDK wraps this: client.login("admin", "admin123").await? stores the token and attaches it to subsequent calls automatically; client.refresh() and client.logout() follow the same rotation rules.

  • REST API — request/response shapes for /auth/*.

  • Audit Log — how login events are recorded.
