Encryption at Rest

With encryption.enabled=true, every chunk blob is encrypted with AES-256-GCM using a per-tenant key before it touches the disk, and every read decrypts transparently — REST and gRPC callers see plaintext bytes exactly as uploaded. This page documents the blob format, the key storage model, the clever-but-important storage-key derivation that preserves deduplication without leaking information, and the honest limits of the scheme.

Overview

Each tenant owns a 32-byte AES-256-GCM key materialised as a file in encryption.keys_dir, created on first use with 0600 permissions. Blobs on disk are nonce(12) || ciphertext || tag(16). Because AES-GCM nonces are random, identical plaintext encrypts to different ciphertext — content addressing by ciphertext is impossible. file-storage solves this by deriving the storage key as BLAKE3(tenant_id ‖ plain_hash), keeping dedup exact within a tenant while making cross-tenant dedup (and the information leak it would create) structurally impossible.

How it works

Pipeline position

Encryption sits after compression in the upload pipeline and before the atomic blob write:

 chunk ─► BLAKE3(plain) ─► zstd ─► AES-256-GCM ─► blob write ─► redb transaction
                                      │
        key = <keys_dir>/<tenant_uuid>.key (32 random bytes)

Blob layout

 offset  0                12                          N-16  N
        ┌──────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┬─────────┐
        │ nonce (12 bytes) │ ciphertext (compressed chunk) │ tag 16B │
        └──────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┴─────────┘

The 12-byte GCM nonce is fresh random per write; the 16-byte authentication tag makes any tampering fail decryption. Decryption errors surface as encryption_error (HTTP 500 / gRPC INTERNAL) and never return partial data.

Key storage

PropertyValue
Location<encryption.keys_dir>/<tenant_uuid>.key
Content64 hex characters = 32 random bytes
Creationon first encrypt/decrypt for that tenant (create_new — never overwritten)
Permissions0600 (owner read/write only)
CachingKeys are held in an in-process cache after first load

There is no KMS integration: the key files are the trust root. Protect them the way you protect database credentials — dedicated volume, host-level encryption, restricted access — and back them up with the data (a lost key means permanently unreadable blobs).

Storage-key derivation, and why

Without encryption, a chunk is stored at BLAKE3(plaintext). Random nonces break that, so with encryption enabled the block-store key becomes:

storage_key = BLAKE3( tenant_id ‖ plain_hash )

This one-line change delivers three properties at once:

  1. Deterministic addressing is restored — the same plaintext chunk in the same tenant always maps to the same blob path, so dedup keeps working.
  2. No cross-tenant leakage — two tenants uploading identical content get different storage keys and different ciphertexts. An operator reading both block stores cannot even confirm the files were the same.
  3. Dedup is scoped by construction — within a tenant, dedup remains exact (dedup_chunks == chunk_count on re-upload); across tenants, it never triggers.

The plaintext hash is still recorded per chunk (plain_hash in the manifest’s ChunkRef) and every disk read re-verifies it after decryption, so corruption cannot pass silently.

Configuration

encryption:
  enabled: true
  keys_dir: "/var/lib/file-storage/data/keys"
KeyDefaultMeaning
encryption.enabledfalseMaster switch; encrypts new blobs at upload time
encryption.keys_dirdata/keysWhere per-tenant key files live

What is NOT encrypted

Be precise about the boundary:

  • Metadata and manifests live in the redb database unencrypted: filenames, tags, MIME types, sizes, chunk hashes and audit events.
  • Tenant identity (UUIDs) appears in key filenames and in the derivation input.
  • Only chunk blobs under storage.root are ciphertext.

If filenames are sensitive, combine at-rest encryption with full-disk encryption so the redb file is covered too.

Examples

Key rotation — the honest story

There is no built-in rotation command, and the derivation makes naive rotation dangerous. The storage key depends only on (tenant_id, plain_hash) — not on the key material. Replacing <keys_dir>/<tenant>.key with new key bytes therefore:

  1. Changes the AES key used for all new writes.
  2. Leaves old blobs in place, encrypted under the old key — they now fail authenticated decryption on read (every download of old content errors with encryption_error).

The supported migration is read-and-reupload while the old key is still active:

# 1. while the OLD key is still in place, export every tenant file to plaintext
# 2. hard-purge all tenant files (admin) and let GC remove the old blobs
# 3. replace <keys_dir>/<tenant>.key with a fresh key file (32 random bytes, 0600)
# 4. re-upload the exported data — it is re-encrypted under the new key

Skipping the purge is a footgun: without it, re-uploads would dedup onto the old-key ciphertext blobs and remain unreadable. Two diagnostics help you operate this safely: Encryptor::has_key(tenant) reports whether a key file exists, and the key-file modification time (exposed by the key_age_ms diagnostic) tells you how old the active key is — wire an alert on stale keys if rotation matters to you.

Verifying encryption is active

FS_ENCRYPTION__ENABLED=true cargo run --release -- --config config/default.yaml &
# upload anything, then look at the block store:
xxd data/blocks/$(ls data/blocks/ab/cd | head -1 2>/dev/null) | head -2
# ciphertext: no readable compression headers or plaintext

Also confirm the manifest reports encrypted: true per chunk via GET /api/v1/files/:id/meta?chunks=true (the metadata walkthrough is on the Your First File page in the Getting Started section). Reads need no changes on either transport — REST and gRPC decrypt transparently on the way out.

Consequences to remember

  • Tenant UUIDs are immutable. The key filename and the storage-key derivation both embed the tenant UUID; re-keying a tenant under a new UUID orphans every blob and loses the key mapping.
  • Dedup within a tenant is exact; across tenants it is zero — plan capacity accordingly when you enable encryption on a multi-tenant system with shared content.
  • Enabling encryption on an existing plaintext node does not re-encrypt old blobs; only new writes are encrypted. Migrate the same way as rotation: export, purge, re-upload.

See also

  • Threat Model — which adversary this defends against, and residual risks.
  • Authentication — how tenants are determined per request.
  • Tenants — tenant registration and the immutability contract.
  • Configuration reference — encryption.* keys.
  • Hardening Checklist — key file permissions and rotation cadence.

Tenants

Every file in file-storage belongs to exactly one tenant, and every request is scoped to the tenant embedded in the caller’s token. This page documents the isolation guarantees, how tenants are registered and seeded, usage accounting, and the deliberate limits (notably: quotas are accounted but not enforced).

Overview

Multi-tenancy is enforced at three layers: the name and tag indexes are tenant-prefixed in the redb key space, every handler re-checks that the manifest’s tenant matches the caller’s tenant, and audit events record the acting tenant. Admins are the single exception — they may read and manage any tenant’s data, and every such event remains auditable.

How it works

Isolation guarantees

GuaranteeMechanism
Namespaces cannot collidename_index keys are tenant ‖ 0x00 ‖ name; two tenants can store files with the same name independently
Tag listings are scopedtag_index keys are tenant ‖ 0x00 ‖ tag ‖ 0x00 ‖ file_id
Cross-tenant reads failEvery download/meta/delete/list call checks manifest.tenant_id against the token’s tenant
Admin exceptionadmin role bypasses tenant checks (used for management and support); the check is centralised in one function
Encrypted dedup scopingWith encryption on, storage keys are BLAKE3(tenant ‖ plain_hash) — cross-tenant dedup cannot happen (see Encryption at Rest)

The access check is implemented in src/security/tenant.rs (check_access) and applied by the API middleware (src/api/middleware/tenant.rs) and each gRPC service.

Tenant registration

Tenants enter the system through two routes:

  1. Configuration seed — the tenants: list in YAML is upserted idempotently at boot (TenantStore::seed). This is how the development tenants default, acme and globex exist on a fresh node.
  2. API creation — POST /api/v1/tenants (admin only, body {"name": "..."}) mints a new UUIDv7 tenant record. The same capability exists as AdminService/CreateTenant over gRPC.

A third, automatic route exists for replication: when a replica applies a manifest for a tenant it has never seen, TenantStore::ensure creates a placeholder record so accounting stays correct (ensure-on-apply).

Usage accounting

Each tenant record carries file_count and bytes_used, updated transactionally with the same redb commit that registers or removes manifests — uploads increment, deletes and purges decrement, and the numbers cannot drift from the index. They are visible via GET /api/v1/tenants and AdminService/GetTenant.

Quotas are not enforced. The accounting fields are informational only; there is no quota_bytes key and no code path rejects an upload because a tenant is “full”. If you need hard limits, gate uploads at your API gateway using the accounting numbers.

Viewer role and tenant scoping

The viewer role is read-only and tenant-scoped: it can list, download and read metadata only for its own tenant. Cross-tenant listing requires admin regardless of role. Together with tenant-prefixed indexes this makes viewers safe to hand to external auditors or read-only integrations.

Audit scoping

Every audit event records the acting tenant (tenant_id field, null for system events such as GC). Querying by tenant is possible on both transports (AdminService/QueryAudit{tenant_id}); see Audit Log.

Configuration

tenants:
  - id: "01930000-0000-7000-8000-000000000001"
    name: "default"
  - id: "01930000-0000-7000-8000-000000000002"
    name: "acme"

Rules:

  • Seeding is an idempotent upsert — existing tenants are never overwritten at boot.
  • Tenant names must be 1–128 characters (validated on create).
  • Tenant UUIDs are immutable once blobs exist: with encryption enabled they are part of key filenames and storage-key derivation, and they scope the index namespaces (see Encryption at Rest).
  • Each configured user maps to exactly one tenant_id (see Authentication).

Examples

List isolation: bob cannot see alice’s files

# alice (tenant acme) uploads a file
ALICE=$(curl -s -X POST localhost:8080/api/v1/auth/login \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"username":"alice","password":"alice123"}' | jq -r .access_token)
curl -s -X POST "localhost:8080/api/v1/files?name=acme-doc.txt" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $ALICE" --data-binary 'acme secret' | jq -r .file_id

# bob (tenant globex) sees an empty listing
BOB=$(curl -s -X POST localhost:8080/api/v1/auth/login \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"username":"bob","password":"bob123"}' | jq -r .access_token)
curl -s "localhost:8080/api/v1/files?limit=10" -H "Authorization: Bearer $BOB" | jq '.total, .items'
# 0
# []

# bob also cannot fetch alice's file directly
curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n' -H "Authorization: Bearer $BOB" \
  "localhost:8080/api/v1/files/$FILE_ID"
# 403 (or 404 — never alice's bytes)

Creating and inspecting tenants

ADMIN=$(...admin login...)
curl -s -X POST localhost:8080/api/v1/tenants \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN" -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"name":"initech"}' | jq
curl -s localhost:8080/api/v1/tenants -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN" | jq

Replication ensure-on-apply

When the replica receives replication.apply for tenant 0193...42 which it has never seen, it creates a placeholder tenant record with the primary’s UUID so file_count/bytes_used bookkeeping remains transactional on both sides. The placeholder name is a fallback label; reconcile names at the next config seed.

See also

  • Authentication — the tenant claim and role matrix.
  • Encryption at Rest — tenant-scoped dedup keys.
  • Audit Log — tenant-scoped audit queries.
  • Threat Model — cross-tenant access threats and mitigations.
  • Configuration reference — tenants[] and auth.users[].tenant_id.

Audit Log

file-storage records a structured, append-only audit trail for every security-relevant action: file uploads, downloads, deletes and purges, logins, tenant creation, GC runs and replication activity. This page documents the event format, the full action catalog, how to query the log on both transports, and the retention properties — including what the audit log deliberately does not contain.

Overview

Events are persisted as JSON rows in the redb audit table, keyed by UUIDv7 so the table scan order is chronological. Writing is fire-and-forget with a safety valve: if persisting an event fails, the error is logged and counted in metrics but never fails the user’s request — audit problems degrade observability, not availability.

How it works

Event format

FieldType / valuesMeaning
idUUIDv7Time-ordered primary key
ts_msepoch millisEvent timestamp
actorsystem or usernameWho acted (system for GC, replication, etc.)
tenant_idUUID or nullTenant scope of the action
actionstring from the catalog belowWhat happened
objectstringTarget of the action (file id, tenant id, …)
ipIP string or -Client address (where applicable)
statusok, failure, HTTP-ish status labelsOutcome
detailstringShort, structured extras (e.g. l1 hit, byte counts)

Where it lives

The audit trail is stored in the same redb database file as the index (database.path, default data/index.redb), in the audit table. It is append-only through the API surface: there is no endpoint that updates or deletes audit rows. Consequence: the audit log shares the fate of the data disk — for tamper evidence beyond a compromised-disk adversary, back up the database off-host and/or ship audit events to an external collector (see the hardening checklist).

Retention

There is no automatic rotation or pruning of the audit table. It grows with request volume. Manage retention operationally:

  • Periodically export the audit table (admin query API in a loop, or a copy of the redb file) and archive it externally.
  • Size the data volume with audit growth in mind for your request rate.
  • Restore-point your backups if you need historical queries — see the Backup & Restore page in the Operations section.

Configuration

Query API

TransportCallAccess
RESTGET /api/v1/audit?limit=N → [AuditEntryDto]admin only
gRPCAdminService/QueryAudit{limit, tenant_id} → {entries, total}admin only

limit caps the number of returned events (most recent first). The gRPC variant accepts an optional tenant_id filter; over REST, filter client-side after retrieval.

What is NOT logged

  • Response bodies — no file bytes ever pass through the audit trail.
  • File content — only ids, names-as-object-labels and counts appear in detail.
  • No request headers beyond the client IP; tokens and passwords are never recorded.

Failure behaviour

If the redb write fails (disk full, IO error), the service logs the error (failed to persist audit event), increments the file_storage_audit_events_total{action} counter for the attempted action, and continues serving the request. Monitoring that metric against your request rate reveals audit-logging health.

Examples

Query the trail (REST)

TOKEN=$(... admin login ...)
curl -s "localhost:8080/api/v1/audit?limit=5" -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" | jq

Example output (abbreviated):

[
  {
    "id": "01930000-0000-7000-8000-0000000000e1",
    "ts_ms": 1762700012345,
    "actor": "alice",
    "tenant_id": "01930000-0000-7000-8000-000000000002",
    "action": "file.upload",
    "object": "01930000-0000-7000-8000-0000000000ab",
    "ip": "10.0.0.7",
    "status": "ok",
    "detail": "chunks=3 dedup=0"
  }
]

Query the trail (gRPC)

grpcurl -plaintext -H "authorization: Bearer $ADMIN_JWT" \
  -d '{"limit": 10, "tenant_id": "01930000-0000-7000-8000-000000000002"}' \
  localhost:9090 file_storage.v1.AdminService/QueryAudit

Action catalog

ActionEmitted when
file.uploadA manifest is committed (either transport)
file.downloadA download completes, including 304 and range responses (status carries the outcome, detail e.g. l1 hit)
file.deleteA soft delete tombstones a manifest
file.purgeAn admin hard-purges a manifest
auth.login.successValid credentials issued a token pair
auth.login.failureWrong password or unknown user (both look identical from outside)
tenant.createA tenant is created via the API
replication.applyThe replica applies shipped operations
storage.gcA GC pass runs (actor system)
replication.resyncAn admin triggers a full re-push of a file bundle

The metric file_storage_audit_events_total{action} counts every emitted event, so you can alert on unexpected spikes (for example a burst of auth.login.failure indicating credential stuffing).

See also

  • Authentication — the login lifecycle behind auth.login.* events.
  • Tenants — tenant scoping of events and queries.
  • Threat Model — audit tampering threats and the off-host mitigation.
  • Hardening Checklist — audit export and alerting rows.
  • REST API — the AuditEntryDto shape.
  • gRPC API — AdminService details.

Hardening Checklist

A production-hardening checklist for file-storage, grouped by theme and written to be walked top-to-bottom before go-live and re-verified each audit cycle. Every row names the concrete key, file or procedure involved, so the list doubles as a configuration review sheet. The threats each item addresses are catalogued in the Threat Model.

Overview

The checklist covers 55 items across seven themes: Secrets, Network, Auth, Storage, Limits, Ops and Process. Items marked ☑ built-in are already enforced by the shipped defaults or by code. Items marked ☐ operator action leave the surface open by default and require a change on your side.

How it works

Work through the themes in order: secrets first, then network, auth, storage, limits, and finally the operational and process controls. After each pass, record the date and owner in your change log so the next audit knows what was verified and when.

Usage

Secrets

| Item | Detail | Status | |—|—|—|—| | Replace auth.jwt_secret | openssl rand -hex 32; the default is a placeholder | ☐ operator action | | Replace replication.api_key | openssl rand -hex 24; identical on primary and replica | ☐ operator action | | Remove demo passwords | admin/admin123, alice/alice123, bob/bob123, viewer/viewer123 must not survive | ☐ operator action | | Argon2id hashes only | Use password_hash (file-storage hash-password --password '...'), never password | ☐ operator action | | Key file permissions | Per-tenant keys created 0600 by the service; verify nothing loosened them | ☑ built-in | | Key files off git | data/keys/ and TLS private keys never enter version control | ☐ operator action | | Rotate secrets quarterly | Rotating jwt_secret also invalidates all tokens and the in-memory refresh store | ☐ operator action | | Secret length validation | jwt_secret under 16 chars is rejected at boot | ☑ built-in | | Constant-time key comparison | Internal API keys compared in constant time | ☑ built-in |

Network

| Item | Detail | Status | |—|—|—|—| | TLS termination for REST | Terminate at ingress/LB; the REST listener is plain HTTP by design | ☐ operator action | | gRPC listener TLS | server.grpc_tls.enabled=true with cert/key in untrusted segments | ☐ operator action | | mTLS for replication | grpc_tls.client_ca_path forces CA-signed client certificates | ☐ operator action | | Firewall /internal/* | Restrict the plain-HTTP internal routes to replication peers | ☐ operator action | | Never expose 8080/9090 directly | Publish only the ingress; both ports are unauthenticated or JWT-only | ☐ operator action | | Bind addresses | 0.0.0.0 default; restrict with firewall/network policy, not just config | ☐ operator action | | REST rate limiting | tower_governor per client IP, on by default | ☑ built-in | | gRPC per-tenant token bucket | On by default; streaming RPCs are not limited — size the perimeter for that | ☑ built-in |

Auth

| Item | Detail | Status | |—|—|—|—| | Least-privilege roles | Prefer viewer for read-only consumers; reserve admin for operators | ☐ operator action | | Short access TTL | 900 s default; verification is local with no revocation list | ☑ built-in | | One user, one tenant | Config structure maps each user to exactly one tenant_id | ☑ built-in | | Tenant mapping reviewed | Ensure service accounts are not accidentally admins | ☐ operator action | | No plaintext passwords in prod config | Enforced in review; validation cannot detect it | ☐ operator action | | Login failures audited | auth.login.failure events with IP on every failed attempt | ☑ built-in | | Timing equalisation | Unknown usernames burn argon2 cost via a dummy hash | ☑ built-in | | Session invalidation procedure | Restart or rotate jwt_secret to log everyone out | ☐ operator action |

Storage

| Item | Detail | Status | |—|—|—|—| | Dedicated volumes | Block store, redb index and keys on a dedicated, monitored volume | ☐ operator action | | Single-writer discipline | One process per database.path; never share the data dir over NFS | ☑ built-in | | redb fsync durability | Commits use Durability::Immediate (WAL + fsync) | ☑ built-in | | Atomic blob writes | Temp + rename + dir fsync before the index transaction | ☑ built-in | | GC mode-correct | refcount on primaries, reachability on replicas | ☑ built-in | | Orphan sweep tuned | orphan_min_age_secs 3600 default; raise for slow replication | ☐ operator action | | Grace period matches policy | grace_period_secs 86400 default; set to your real undo window | ☐ operator action | | Encryption at rest | Enable encryption.enabled for confidential data | ☐ operator action | | Backups + restore drills | Back up data/ and rehearse restores; audit history shares the file | ☐ operator action |

Limits

| Item | Detail | Status | |—|—|—|—| | limits.max_file_size sized | 10 GiB default; lower it to your real maximum | ☐ operator action | | limits.max_body sized | 1 GiB cap; multipart needs headroom over the largest file | ☐ operator action | | Rate limits tuned | rate_limit.per_second/burst per workload | ☐ operator action | | gRPC message ceiling awareness | 256 MiB max message; streamed uploads rarely hit it | ☑ built-in | | Decompression bounded | Honours recorded size_original; bombs cannot allocate unboundedly | ☑ built-in | | Replication queue sized | queue_capacity 10000; overload backpressures uploads | ☐ operator action | | Chunking bounds validated | min ≤ avg ≤ max, max ≤ 16 MiB, checked at boot | ☑ built-in |

Ops

| Item | Detail | Status | |—|—|—|—| | Metrics scraped | Prometheus targets both nodes on the REST port | ☐ operator action | | Alert rules | On hash_mismatch_total, replication_failures_total, audit errors, queue depth | ☐ operator action | | Audit export off-host | Ship GET /audit output or database backups off-host | ☐ operator action | | JSON logging at info | logging.format: json, level: info | ☐ operator action | | Graceful shutdown | Both listeners drain on SIGTERM | ☑ built-in | | Health probes wired | /ready as readiness, /health as liveness | ☐ operator action | | Ready-probe semantics | /ready returns 503 while database or storage is unhealthy | ☑ built-in |

Process

| Item | Detail | Status | |—|—|—|—| | Distroless nonroot runtime | Official image runs uid 65532, no shell or package manager | ☑ built-in | | Read-only rootfs compatible | All state lives under the /var/lib/file-storage/data volume | ☑ built-in | | Volume ownership | Prepare data volumes for uid 65532 (fsGroup: 65532 on Kubernetes) | ☐ operator action | | Image signing | Sign and verify the deployed image | ☐ operator action | | Dependency audit | cargo audit against Cargo.lock in CI | ☐ operator action | | Reproducible builds | cargo build --release --locked; Dockerfile builds from the lockfile | ☑ built-in | | Quality gates | cargo fmt --check and zero-warning clippy gate changes | ☑ built-in |

Configuration

Every key cited above is documented with its default in the Configuration reference; secret-handling patterns (env injection, mounted files, rotation) are in Secrets. TLS certificate generation belongs to the TLS page in the Deployment section.

Examples

The first-hour pass

If you have one hour before go-live, do these five in order:

  1. Generate and deploy a fresh jwt_secret and replication api_key (Secrets theme).
  2. Replace the demo users with argon2id-hashed, least-privilege accounts (Secrets + Auth).
  3. TLS for REST at the ingress; gRPC TLS with mTLS for replication (Network).
  4. Right-size max_file_size, max_body and rate limits (Limits).
  5. Wire Prometheus, alerts and an off-host audit export (Ops).

Re-verification snippet

# secrets are not defaults
grep -c "change-me\|admin123" /etc/file-storage/default.yaml   # expect 0
# key files are owner-only
stat -c '%a' /var/lib/file-storage/data/keys/*.key             # expect 600
# gRPC TLS refuses plain connections
grpcurl -plaintext localhost:9090 list                          # expect failure
# ready probe is honest
curl -fsS localhost:8080/ready | jq .status                     # expect "ok"

See also

  • Threat Model — the adversary model behind these rows.
  • Authentication — token lifecycle and roles.
  • Encryption at Rest — at-rest protections and residual risks.
  • Tenants — isolation guarantees.
  • Audit Log — the event trail you are asked to export.
  • Configuration reference — every key cited above.
  • Secrets — storing and rotating secrets.

Throttling & Rate Limits

Status: normative since V5 Wave 2 (audit findings M-05 + I-06). The login lockout is a security control and defaults ON.

The two controls

ControlScopeKeyed byWhere
RPC governor (token bucket)every gRPC call — unary and streaming (Upload, Download)tenant idstate.0.rate_limiter, taken by the unary interceptor and at streaming-handler entry
Login throttle (exponential lockout)POST /api/v1/auth/login, AuthService/Loginusernamesecurity::throttle::LoginThrottle, inside AuthService::login

The REST path keeps its own per-IP governor; the gRPC governor is per-tenant. Both transports share ONE governor instance for unary and streaming calls — there is no streaming bypass (the pre-M-05 defect), and CI + streaming_governor_matches_unary pin that.

RPC governor

  • rate_limit.per_second tokens/s per tenant, rate_limit.burst ceiling (defaults in config.toml); disabled configs pass through.
  • Over rate → 429 Too Many Requests / RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED, refused before any pipeline work.
  • The tenant map is bounded (10 000 entries) with insertion-order oldest-first eviction — never a wholesale clear() (I-06). One flood cannot reset other tenants’ budgets.

Login throttle (M-05)

Config:

[auth.login_throttle]
enabled = true        # security control: defaults ON (fail-closed posture)
base_ms = 1000        # lockout after the first failure
multiplier = 2.0      # exponential: 1s, 2s, 4s, 8s, ...
lockout_cap_ms = 900000
map_capacity = 10000

Semantics:

  1. check runs before the argon2 verify — a locked-out username is refused without spending the 64 MiB verify budget (429 / RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED, message carries retry_after=<ms>ms).
  2. Only InvalidCredentials grows the backoff. Verifier/backend errors never count (a flaky password store must not lock out its user).
  3. Success resets the state.
  4. Throttled logins are audited as auth.login.throttled (distinct from auth.login.failure) with the retry window in the detail field.

Fail-open vs fail-closed (rule 17)

  • The throttle is fail-open: a missing entry, a poisoned lock or any internal surprise degrades to “allow” — the throttle may never become a self-inflicted outage for legitimate users.
  • The auth verifier is fail-closed: a verifier error NEVER authenticates.
  • The two failure modes are asserted by verifier_error_does_not_throttle (tests/regression/m05_throttle.rs).

Regression gates

tests/regression/m05_throttle.rs: grpc_stream_flood_throttled, login_lockout_exponential, login_throttle_correct_password_inside_window_still_rejected, login_throttle_audited, throttle_map_evicts_oldest_not_clear, verifier_error_does_not_throttle, streaming_governor_matches_unary.

Bootstrap & secrets

This page is the C-01 remediation contract in operator form: the service refuses to boot with any secret an attacker could know from the public repository. You provision real secrets, or the process exits non-zero with the full violation list.

Overview

file-storage boots only with operator-supplied secrets. AppConfig::validate() is fail-closed: it rejects every literal shipped in the repository (change-me, admin123, internal-dev-api-key, …), secrets shorter than the policy floor (32 bytes for the JWT secret, 24 for the cluster API key), plaintext password entries in release builds, and non-argon2id password hashes — and it reports all violations at once, not just the first.

How it works

  • The container image contains no configuration file. ENTRYPOINT is the binary alone; CMD points --config at /run/secrets/file-storage.yaml (the mounted secret). A fresh docker run without a mounted config exits non-zero.
  • Users are provisioned as argon2id PHC strings (password_hash). Plaintext password fields are a debug-build convenience and are rejected by validation in release builds.
  • docker-compose.yml binds REST/gRPC to 127.0.0.1 and runs containers as 65532:65532; TLS termination happens at your edge proxy or via server.grpc_tls.

Configuration

WhatWhere
Secret file (git-ignored)secrets/file-storage.yaml
Example templatesecrets/file-storage.yaml.example
Compose wiringsecrets: facility, user: "65532:65532"
Config path override--config PATH or FS_CONFIG=PATH

Examples

Generate the secrets:

openssl rand -hex 32          # auth.jwt_secret
openssl rand -hex 24          # replication.api_key (per node pair)
file-storage hash-password    # reads the password from stdin, prints the PHC string

hash-password without --password reads from stdin so the secret never lands in shell history.

Bootstrap a local compose stack:

cp secrets/file-storage.yaml.example secrets/file-storage.yaml
$EDITOR secrets/file-storage.yaml        # paste the generated values
docker compose up -d --wait
curl -s http://127.0.0.1:8080/health

Verify the fail-closed behavior (this must fail):

docker compose down && rm secrets/file-storage.yaml
docker compose up -d --wait              # exits non-zero; logs name auth.jwt_secret

Verify a release build refuses the shipped dev config:

cargo build --release
./target/release/file-storage --config config/default.yaml   # non-zero, lists violations

See also

  • Configuration reference — every key the secret file accepts
  • Hardening checklist — the full 50+ item production gate
  • Secrets — rotation procedures for each secret class
  • Runbook — incident procedures

Monitoring

All runtime metrics are exposed in Prometheus text format at GET /metrics on the REST port (default 8080); the bundled Compose stack ships Prometheus scraping both nodes every 15 seconds. This page is the metric catalog plus starter alert rules and a Grafana dashboard; when an alert fires, continue in the Runbook.

Overview

Both listeners share one AppState, so one scrape target per node covers both transports. The Compose stack wires Prometheus like this:

# config/prometheus.yml
global:
  scrape_interval: 15s
  evaluation_interval: 15s

scrape_configs:
  - job_name: file-storage-primary
    metrics_path: /metrics
    static_configs:
      - targets: ["primary:8080"]
  - job_name: file-storage-replica
    metrics_path: /metrics
    static_configs:
      - targets: ["replica:8080"]

The prometheus service in docker-compose.yml maps its UI to host port 9099 because host port 9090 already belongs to the primary’s gRPC listener. Open http://localhost:9099 after docker compose up.

flowchart LR
    P["primary:8080 /metrics"] -->|15s scrape| PR["Prometheus :9099"]
    R["replica:8080 /metrics"] -->|15s scrape| PR
    PR --> GR["Grafana dashboards"]
    PR --> AR["Alert rules"]
    AR --> OP["Operator"]
    OP --> RB["Runbook"]

Figure 1: One scrape target per node covers REST and gRPC; alerts route into the runbook.

How it works

One global Prometheus recorder is installed per process (src/metrics.rs); all names are prefixed file_storage_. Two design decisions keep the series count predictable:

Route-label normalization. HTTP metrics label by route template, never by raw path: normalize_path rewrites any 36-character UUID segment to :id and any 64-hex segment to :hash:

GET /api/v1/files/01930000-0000-7000-8000-00000000abcd   ->  route=/api/v1/files/:id
GET /internal/chunk/9f86d081884c7d659a2feaa0c55ad015...  ->  route=/internal/chunk/:hash

Per-file traffic cannot explode cardinality; the route set stays bounded no matter how many files exist.

gRPC transport identification. gRPC calls are recorded in their own families (file_storage_grpc_requests_total, file_storage_grpc_request_duration_seconds) with the full method path as the rpc label (for example file_storage.v1.AuthService/Login) and the gRPC status name as code. REST and gRPC traffic is distinguishable by metric family alone — no shared transport label is needed.

Histograms use the exporter’s default buckets, so histogram_quantile works out of the box.

Configuration

No metric-specific configuration exists; GET /metrics is always on. The catalog:

MetricTypeLabelsMeaning
file_storage_uploads_totalcounterstatusCompleted uploads
file_storage_downloads_totalcounterstatus, sourceCompleted downloads; source is l1, l2, disk or mixed
file_storage_upload_duration_secondshistogramstatusEnd-to-end upload latency
file_storage_download_duration_secondshistogramstatusEnd-to-end download latency
file_storage_bytes_written_totalcounter—Original (logical) bytes accepted
file_storage_bytes_read_totalcounter—Plaintext bytes served
file_storage_dedup_hits_totalcounter—Chunks resolved from an already-stored blob
file_storage_dedup_bytes_saved_totalcounter—Bytes not written because of dedup
file_storage_cache_hits_totalcounterlevelCache hits per level (l1 plaintext, l2 blobs, l3 manifests)
file_storage_chunks_stored_totalcounterdedupedChunks written to the block store
file_storage_replication_queue_depthgauge—Ops waiting in the in-memory replication queue (capacity default 10000)
file_storage_replication_failures_totalcounter—Failed replication flushes on the primary
file_storage_replication_ops_applied_totalcounter—Replication ops applied by the replica
file_storage_replication_flush_secondshistogramstatusFlush duration; status records success or failure
file_storage_hash_mismatch_totalcounter—BLAKE3 verification failures after disk loads (data corruption signal)
file_storage_gc_files_purged_totalcounter—Manifests purged by garbage collection
file_storage_gc_bytes_reclaimed_totalcounter—Bytes freed by garbage collection
file_storage_gc_orphans_removed_totalcounter—Orphan blobs swept
file_storage_http_request_duration_secondshistogrammethod, routeHTTP latency by normalized route

Usage

Suggested alert rules

A practical starter set — only hash_mismatch should page immediately:

groups:
  - name: file-storage
    rules:
      - alert: FileStorageScrapeDown
        expr: up{job=~"file-storage-.*"} == 0
        for: 1m
        labels:
          severity: critical

      - alert: FileStorageNotReady
        # GET /ready returns 503 while the redb ping or storage-root probe
        # fails. Point a blackbox exporter (or your HTTP prober) at /ready.
        expr: probe_success{job="file-storage-ready"} == 0
        for: 2m
        labels:
          severity: critical
        annotations:
          summary: "Node not ready (database/storage probe failing)"

      - alert: FileStorageReplicationFailing
        expr: increase(file_storage_replication_failures_total[5m]) > 0
        for: 5m
        labels:
          severity: warning

      - alert: FileStorageReplicationQueueBacklog
        expr: file_storage_replication_queue_depth > 8000
        for: 5m
        labels:
          severity: warning

      - alert: FileStorageGcStalled
        # Requires node_exporter for filesystem metrics.
        expr: |
          rate(file_storage_gc_bytes_reclaimed_total[6h]) == 0
          and node_filesystem_avail_bytes{mountpoint="/var/lib/file-storage"}
              / node_filesystem_size_bytes{mountpoint="/var/lib/file-storage"} < 0.15
        for: 30m
        labels:
          severity: warning
        annotations:
          summary: "Disk filling while GC reclaims nothing (grace pending?)"

      - alert: FileStorageHashMismatch
        expr: increase(file_storage_hash_mismatch_total[5m]) > 0
        labels:
          severity: critical
        annotations:
          summary: "BLAKE3 verification failed — possible corruption, page now"

      - alert: FileStorageUploadP99Regression
        expr: |
          histogram_quantile(0.99,
            sum by (le) (rate(file_storage_upload_duration_seconds_bucket[5m]))) > 5
        for: 10m
        labels:
          severity: warning

      - alert: FileStorage5xxRate
        expr: |
          sum(rate(file_storage_http_requests_total{status=~"5.."}[5m]))
            / clamp_min(sum(rate(file_storage_http_requests_total[5m])), 0.001) > 0.01
        for: 10m
        labels:
          severity: warning

# Dedup hit ratio per chunk written
