Backup & Restore

A file-storage node keeps all durable state in three places on disk: the redb index file, the block-store directory and (when encryption is on) the per-tenant key files. This page defines what to copy, how to get a consistent copy, how to restore and verify, and what is genuinely not recoverable. For day-to-day disk and GC questions see GC Operations; for replica-side recovery see Replication Ops.

Overview

With the default configuration (storage.root data/blocks, storage.temp_dir data/tmp, database.path data/index.redb) the state to protect is:

ArtifactPathWhy
redb index filedatabase.path (e.g. data/index.redb)Manifests, chunk refcounts, name/tag index, audit log, tenants — the single source of truth
Block storestorage.root (e.g. data/blocks/)Content-addressed blobs ab/cd/<64-hex>.bin
Encryption keysencryption.keys_dir (e.g. data/keys/)Per-tenant AES-256-GCM keys, mode 0600; without them encrypted blobs are undecryptable
Config and secretsconfig/*.yaml, auth.jwt_secret, user hashesNeeded to boot a compatible replacement node

Back up the temp directory never — storage.temp_dir holds multipart spool files mid-upload only.

How it works

The write path makes backups forgiving. Blobs are fsynced before the single redb transaction that references them, and the transaction commits with Durability::Immediate (WAL + fsync). A copy taken at any instant is therefore crash-consistent in the useful direction:

crash-consistent copy states
  blob present, index entry present   -> fine
  blob present, no index entry        -> orphan; GC sweeps after orphan_min_age_secs
  index entry, blob missing           -> impossible by write ordering

You never need to coordinate blob and index copies at the byte level; you need both sides of one point in time. That is what the procedures below guarantee.

flowchart TD
    A["Quiesce: stop node, or snapshot volume"] --> B["Copy index.redb + blocks/ + keys/ + config"]
    B --> C["Ship to backup target"]
    C --> D["Restore onto target node"]
    D --> E["Verify: counts + spot BLAKE3 checks"]
    E --> F["Start node, check GET /ready"]
    F --> G["Resync any files missing on replicas"]

Figure 1: Backup and restore pipeline; verification is a scripted loop, not a built-in feature.

Usage

Consistent-copy procedure

Pick one of these, in order of preference:

  1. Volume snapshot (best). LVM, ZFS or EBS snapshots are atomic and need no downtime. Snapshot once; then copy the snapshot contents at leisure: 
    lvcreate --snapshot -n fs-backup -L 10G /dev/vg0/fs-data
# or on ZFS: zfs snapshot tank/fs-data@nightly
# or on AWS: create an EBS snapshot of the data volume
  2. Quiesce, then copy. Stop the process (systemctl stop file-storage or docker compose stop primary) or block writes at the firewall, run the copy, start again.
  3. fsfreeze. On a plain filesystem without snapshot support, freeze the mount point during the copy: 
    fsfreeze --freeze /var/lib/file-storage/data
rsync ... ; fsfreeze --unfreeze /var/lib/file-storage/data

rsync example

Use hardlink-aware, identity-preserving flags:

rsync -aHAX --numeric-ids --delete \
  /var/lib/file-storage/data/ \
  backup:/srv/backups/fs-primary/data/

-H preserves hardlinks (rare in the block store but safe), -A/-X keep ACLs and extended attributes, --numeric-ids keeps UID/GID mapping stable — the container runs as nonroot and the data directory must be writable by that UID after restore.

Restore procedure

sequenceDiagram
    participant OP as Operator
    participant FS as New node
    participant R as Replica
    OP->>FS: stop process, clear data dir
    OP->>FS: restore index.redb, blocks/, keys/, config
    OP->>FS: fix ownership (nonroot UID)
    OP->>FS: start node
    FS->>OP: GET /ready -> 200
    OP->>OP: verify counts + spot BLAKE3 checks
    OP->>R: resync missing files (per file)

Figure 2: Restore sequence; resync closes the async replication gap.

Step by step:

  1. Stop the target node and empty its data directory.
  2. Restore database.path, storage.root, encryption.keys_dir and the config file with identical relative layout.
  3. Fix ownership to the service UID (in the distroless container: UID 65532).
  4. Start the node and confirm GET /ready returns 200 ({"status":"ok","database":"ok","storage":"ok"}).
  5. Run the verification loop below — compare blob count against index entries and spot-check BLAKE3 hashes. There is no built-in verification job; write your own loop like this: 
    #!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail
BASE=http://localhost:8080/api/v1
TOKEN=$(curl -s -X POST "$BASE/auth/login" -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"username":"admin","password":"admin123"}' | jq -r .access_token)

TOTAL=$(curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" "$BASE/files?limit=1" | jq .total)
BLOBS=$(find /var/lib/file-storage/data/blocks -type f | wc -l)
echo "index files=$TOTAL  blobs=$BLOBS  (blobs <= distinct chunks: dedup shares blobs)"

for id in $(curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" "$BASE/files?limit=20" | jq -r '.items[].file_id'); do
  WANT=$(curl -s -D- -o /tmp/body.bin -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" "$BASE/files/$id" \
    | tr -d '\r' | awk 'tolower($1)=="x-content-hash:"{print $2}')
  GOT=$(b3sum /tmp/body.bin | cut -d' ' -f1)
  [ "$WANT" = "$GOT" ] || echo "MISMATCH $id want=$WANT got=$GOT"
done
  6. If a replica exists, compare listings (GET /api/v1/files on both) and push missing files with POST /api/v1/admin/resync/:id.

Restore-to-replica trick

To validate a backup without touching production, restore it onto a node configured as a replica: set replication.upstream_url to the primary, leave replication.enabled=false, and set retention.gc_mode=reachability. Missing blobs arrive on demand during reads; missing manifests can be pulled per file with POST /api/v1/admin/resync/:id from the primary. A healthy replica can later be promoted by repointing clients at it.

Audit backup

The audit log lives in the redb audit table, inside database.path — copying the index file backs up the audit trail automatically. To review it after a restore, GET /api/v1/audit?limit= (admin) returns the newest entries.

Configuration

RPO/RTO guidance for the strategies above (values are guidance, not guarantees — measure on your hardware):

StrategyRPORTONotes
Sync replication + volume snapshot~0 for acknowledged uploadsminutesSync mode adds upload latency (sync_timeout_ms)
Async replication + snapshotseconds to minutes (un-flushed queue window)minutesSee below: the queue itself is not recoverable
Snapshot onlyone snapshot intervalminutesSecond copy of a single-node deployment
Quiesced rsyncone copy intervalminutesRequires a write pause

Examples

What is not recoverable, stated honestly:

  • In-memory replication queue (async mode). Ops not yet flushed when the primary crashes are lost. The replica misses those files’ manifests; repair with POST /api/v1/admin/resync/:id per file, or re-seed as described in Replication Ops. Sync mode closes the window at the cost of upload latency.
  • Refresh tokens. They live in process memory (rotating, single-use, TTL 7d). After a restore every session is gone and users simply log in again; access tokens are stateless JWTs and expire within auth.access_ttl_secs.
  • Files uploaded after the last snapshot, obviously — size your snapshot interval against how much loss is acceptable.

See also

  • Replication Ops — replica repair, re-seeding and promotion.
  • GC Operations — orphan sweeping that makes crash-consistent copies clean themselves.
  • Runbook — incident procedures including corruption and key rotation.
  • Configuration reference — paths, retention and replication keys.
  • Monitoring — alerting on replication lag so backups and replicas agree.

GC Operations

Garbage collection reclaims space from soft-deleted files and orphaned blobs. On primaries the collector runs in refcount mode every retention.gc_interval_secs (default 300 s); on replicas it runs in reachability mode. This page explains when GC runs, how to trigger it manually, how to monitor reclamation lag and how to free disk in an emergency. The internal mechanics are covered in GC architecture.

Overview

Deletes are tombstones: DELETE /api/v1/files/:id sets deleted_at_ms on the manifest and returns 204 immediately. Space comes back only when a GC pass, running after the grace period, removes the manifest, decrements chunk refcounts and deletes blobs whose refcount reaches zero. The same pass sweeps on-disk orphans — blobs with no metadata row, older than orphan_min_age_secs.

flowchart TD
    A["DELETE file (soft)"] -->|"sets deleted_at_ms"| B["Tombstoned manifest"]
    B -->|"grace_period_secs elapses"| C["GC pass: purge manifest"]
    C --> D["Decrement chunk refcounts"]
    D -->|"refcount reaches 0"| E["Delete blob, count bytes reclaimed"]
    B -->|"still inside grace"| B2["Recoverable: include_deleted shows it"]
    F["Blob with no metadata row"] -->|"older than orphan_min_age_secs"| G["Orphan sweep deletes it"]

Figure 1: Tombstone lifecycle in refcount mode; the orphan sweep runs in the same pass.

How it works

A single background task ticks every gc_interval_secs and runs one pass in the configured mode. Each pass processes at most 5000 manifests/orphans; a large backlog therefore drains over several ticks. Results are logged (background gc pass with the report) and exported as counters (gc_files_purged_total, gc_bytes_reclaimed_total, gc_orphans_removed_total).

ModeWhereWhat it does
refcountPrimaryPurges soft-deleted manifests past the grace period, decrements refcounts, deletes zero-refcount blobs, sweeps index-orphans
reachabilityReplicaPurges expired tombstones, then deletes blobs not referenced by any live manifest (and older than orphan_min_age_secs)
disabledEitherCollector idle; space never reclaimed until you re-enable

Two details worth knowing:

  • Orphan age guard. The pipeline writes blobs before the index transaction, so a crash can leave a freshly written blob with no metadata. The sweeper skips anything younger than orphan_min_age_secs (default 3600) so it never deletes a blob that is mid-write.
  • Dedup interplay. A blob is deleted only when its refcount reaches zero across all files. A chunk shared between a live file and a soft-deleted one stays until the live copy is also gone. bytes_reclaimed therefore often trails manifests_purged.

Usage

Manual trigger (REST)

TOKEN=$(curl -s -X POST localhost:8080/api/v1/auth/login \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"username":"admin","password":"admin123"}' | jq -r .access_token)

curl -s -X POST -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" localhost:8080/api/v1/admin/gc | jq

Response shape:

{"manifests_purged": 3, "chunks_removed": 41, "orphans_removed": 0, "bytes_reclaimed": 57344}

The call runs one pass synchronously and is admin-only; it is safe to call concurrently with traffic.

Manual trigger (gRPC)

# mode: 0 = MODE_UNSPECIFIED (use configured mode), 1 = REFCOUNT, 2 = REACHABILITY
grpcurl -plaintext -H "authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" -d '{"mode": 1}' \
  localhost:9090 file_storage.v1.AdminService/TriggerGC

dry_run: true is not supported: the server answers UNIMPLEMENTED (gc dry_run is not supported yet; run without dry_run) rather than silently running a destructive pass the caller believed was a rehearsal. Do not build tooling around dry_run.

Monitoring GC lag

Tombstones inside the grace period are still queryable — that count is your GC backlog:

# Admin token required: include_deleted is admin-only.
curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN" \
  'localhost:8080/api/v1/files?include_deleted=true&limit=1000' \
  | jq '[.items[] | select(.deleted)] | length'

Watch the metrics too: gc_bytes_reclaimed_total flat while free disk space falls means the collector is not reclaiming — usually because tombstones are still inside grace_period_secs, or retention.gc_mode is disabled. Both are covered by the FileStorageGcStalled alert in Monitoring.

Configuration

KeyDefaultTrade-off
retention.gc_moderefcountreachability on replicas; disabled only for controlled migrations
retention.grace_period_secs86400Shorter = faster reclaim, less undo window for accidental deletes; longer = more headroom
retention.gc_interval_secs300Lower drains a backlog faster; each pass is bounded to 5000 items anyway
retention.orphan_min_age_secs3600Must comfortably exceed upload write duration; too small risks deleting a mid-write blob

No hot reload: changing any retention key requires a restart (env override, e.g. FS_RETENTION__GRACE_PERIOD_SECS=300).

Examples

Freeing disk in an emergency

  1. Trigger a pass immediately — purges only tombstones already past grace: 
    curl -s -X POST -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN" localhost:8080/api/v1/admin/gc | jq
  2. Shorten the grace period and restart so new tombstones purge quickly: 
    FS_RETENTION__GRACE_PERIOD_SECS=300 file-storage --config /etc/file-storage/default.yaml
# then trigger GC again; raise the value back after the incident
  3. Purge specific files directly, skipping the grace window entirely (admin): 
    curl -s -X DELETE -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN" \
  'localhost:8080/api/v1/files/<file_id>?purge=true'
    Note ?purge=true removes that manifest immediately; its zero-refcount blobs are deleted in the next GC pass (or immediately by the purge path where possible).
  4. Expand the volume if reclaim cannot keep pace — GC frees unreferenced space, never live data.

Gotchas

  • Dedup delays reclaim. With content-defined chunking, overwritten files share most chunks with their predecessors. Re-uploading report.pdf ten times leaves nine tombstoned manifests whose unique chunks vanish only after grace + GC; shared chunks vanish only when no live file references them.
  • Replica modes differ. A replica in reachability mode decides by “referenced by any live manifest”, not refcounts — do not copy primary GC expectations onto it.
  • Empty files and tiny files below compression.min_size store raw blobs; reclaim arithmetic (bytes_reclaimed) reflects stored size, not original size.

See also

  • GC architecture — collector internals and write ordering.
  • Monitoring — GC metrics and the stalled-GC alert.
  • Runbook — scenario 1: primary disk full.
  • Configuration reference — full retention section.
  • Troubleshooting — disk-full and redb issues.

Replication Ops

Replication is an async WAL shipper: the primary queues chunk blobs and manifests into a bounded in-memory queue and drains them in batches to a replica, which can also pull missing blobs on demand. This page covers the operational side — health signals, lag monitoring, the three repair procedures, key rotation and partition behaviour. Architecture and op ordering are in Replication architecture; monitoring data sources are in Monitoring.

Overview

A deployment is primary + replica. The primary sets replication.enabled=true, replication.target_url and a shared replication.api_key; the replica stays disabled but points replication.upstream_url at the primary for pull-based repair. Ops are queued (capacity queue_capacity, default 10000) and flushed in batches of batch_size (64) or every flush_interval_ms (5000). Order is preserved per file: chunk blobs first, then the manifest, so a manifest never arrives before its blobs.

sequenceDiagram
    participant C as Client
    participant P as Primary
    participant Q as Queue (10k)
    participant R as Replica
    C->>P: upload file
    P->>Q: enqueue chunk ops, then manifest
    P-->>C: 201 (async mode returns here)
    Q->>R: POST /internal/batch (X-Api-Key)
    R-->>Q: 200 acked
    Note over R: GC mode = reachability
    C->>R: read file (later)
    R->>P: blob missing? GET /internal/chunk/:hash
    P-->>R: blob bytes (on-miss pull)

Figure 1: Ship-and-ack pipeline with the replica’s on-miss pull repair path.

How it works

Health signals

SignalTypeHealthyMeaning
replication_queue_depthgauge~0Ops waiting to ship; growth = lag
replication_failures_totalcounterflatFailed flush attempts; increases during target outages
replication_flush_secondshistogramstableFlush duration per batch, tagged by outcome
replication_ops_applied_totalcounter (replica)risingOps the replica has applied

Lag monitoring

Queue depth is the primary lag signal. Cross-check it with content: compare file counts between nodes via their listings — GET /api/v1/files?limit=1 returns total; a growing difference is lag the queue has not yet shipped, a permanent difference means failed or lost ops (the in-memory queue is lost on primary restart).

curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $T" 'http://primary:8080/api/v1/files?limit=1' | jq .total
curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $T" 'http://replica:8081/api/v1/files?limit=1' | jq .total

Network partition behaviour

On flush failure the worker backs off exponentially — 100 ms, then 500 ms, then 2 s (held) — and retries the same batch. Uploads keep succeeding in async mode until the queue is full; at capacity, send awaits a slot, so uploads slow down as natural backpressure (they do not error). In sync mode a blocked flush fails the upload after sync_timeout_ms (default 5000) with HTTP 503 (service_unavailable). When the partition heals, the queue drains without operator action.

Usage

Repair procedure 1 — automatic on-miss pull

For reads: if a blob is missing on the replica (its storage.root), the read path fetches it from replication.upstream_url, verifies it, stores it and serves the data. Requires nothing but upstream_url + matching api_key. This repairs blobs, not missing manifests.

M-07 verify-then-persist (normative since V5 Wave 2). Pulled bytes are UNTRUSTED. When the replica can verify — plaintext chunks always, encrypted chunks whenever the replica holds the tenant key (the documented compose mode) — the decrypted+decompressed plaintext must BLAKE3-match the chunk reference’s plain_hash BEFORE anything is written to the CAS or L2. On mismatch the hostile bytes are quarantined, the cache entry invalidated, the caller gets corruption, and file_storage_repair_hash_mismatch_total{source="repair"} ticks (page it: a growing rate means a compromised or broken upstream). A replica WITHOUT the tenant key cannot verify; those repairs keep the legacy order with a per-event warn! and file_storage_repair_ciphertext_only_total — treat that series as a posture alarm (see threat model, residual risks).

Quarantine. Rejected bytes land in <storage.root>/../quarantine/<hash>.bin — an ops surface outside the CAS walk, never counted against tenant quota. Bounds: storage.quarantine_max_bytes (default 1 GiB, oldest-mtime eviction) and storage.quarantine_retention_secs (default 7 d, swept at boot; file_storage_startup_quarantine_swept reports how much evidence aged out). Inspect before deleting: the quarantined bytes are the exact bytes the upstream served.

Repair procedure 2 — admin resync per file

Push a complete file bundle (manifest + all chunk blobs) to the replica, bypassing the queue:

curl -s -X POST -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN" \
  http://primary:8080/api/v1/admin/resync/<file_id> | jq
# {"file_id": "...", "replicated": true}

Equivalent gRPC: AdminService/Resync with {"file_id": "..."} returning {file_id, replicated}.

Repair procedure 3 — re-seed a fresh replica

A new (or badly damaged) replica starts with an empty data dir; the primary ships everything file by file:

#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Re-seed a fresh replica from the primary's listing. Paginate for >1000 files.
ADMIN=$(curl -s -X POST http://primary:8080/api/v1/auth/login \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"username":"admin","password":"admin123"}' | jq -r .access_token)

for id in $(curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN" \
    'http://primary:8080/api/v1/files?limit=1000' | jq -r '.items[].file_id'); do
  curl -s -X POST -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN" \
    "http://primary:8080/api/v1/admin/resync/$id" | jq -c .
done

Loop with offset until total files are covered. Verify with the listing comparison above.

gRPC ReplicationService as an ingestion path

The internal gRPC service (guarded by x-api-key metadata) is a documented alternative for seeding or migrating between clusters:

RPCDirectionPurpose
ReplicateBatchclient-streamingShip ReplicationOps (put_chunk / put_manifest / delete_file / flush), receive ReplicationAck{ops_acked, success, error}
PullChunkserver-streamingFetch one raw blob by its 32-byte hash (repair path)
PullManifestunaryFetch a ManifestBundle for one file
 
grpcurl -plaintext -H "x-api-key: cluster-api-key-change-me" \
  -d '{"file_id": "01930000-0000-7000-8000-00000000abcd"}' \
  localhost:9090 file_storage.v1.ReplicationService/PullManifest

api_key rotation

The key is read at boot and must match on both nodes. There is no dual-key acceptance window, so rotate by restart, replica first:

  1. Set the new FS_REPLICATION__API_KEY on the replica and restart it. Replication flushes fail during the mismatch window; the queue absorbs them.
  2. Set the new key on the primary and restart it. The un-flushed queue is lost at restart — run repair procedure 3 (or per-file resync) for anything uploaded since step 1.
  3. Watch replication_failures_total return to flat and queue depth to ~0.

Configuration

KeyDefaultTrade-off
replication.modeasyncsync blocks uploads until the batch acks — ~0 RPO, higher latency; async has a crash window
replication.sync_timeout_ms5000Sync-mode failure budget per upload
replication.batch_size64Larger batches amortise round trips; smaller batches lower per-file lag
replication.flush_interval_ms5000Lower = tighter lag, more small batches; the linger window before a partial batch flushes
replication.queue_capacity10000Backpressure ceiling; raise only with memory headroom
replication.api_key—Shared cluster secret; rotate via restart

Consistent snapshot restore from a replica

The replica holds every acknowledged file, so its volume is a live restore point: take a volume snapshot of the replica (see Backup & Restore), or promote the replica itself by repointing clients, then resync files the old primary had not yet shipped. After promotion, re-seed a fresh primary and flip roles.

Examples

Interpretation notes: PullChunk streams ≤64 KiB pieces, so bulk repairs via gRPC should aggregate client-side; ReplicateBatch acks per batch with success=false plus error text when a batch is rejected (check api_key first). For alert wiring — queue depth above 80% of capacity, failures increasing — see the rules in Monitoring.

See also

  • Replication architecture — queue, batching and ordering internals.
  • Monitoring — replication metrics and alerts.
  • Backup & Restore — snapshots, RPO/RTO and the async queue caveat.
  • Runbook — scenario 2: replica lag growing.
  • Configuration reference — the replication section in full.

Troubleshooting

Twenty common failure reports — symptom, diagnostic commands, fix. Deeper incident procedures (disk full, replica lag, corruption, secret rotation, key compromise) live in the Runbook; error-code semantics in REST API and gRPC API.

Overview

Most issues fall into four buckets — auth (401/403), limits (413/416), transport quirks (gRPC metadata, compression, reflection) and storage health (redb, disk, replication) — and the error body {"error":{"code":"...","message":"..."}} names the bucket. The audit log (GET /api/v1/audit, admin) records who did what; GET /metrics confirms whether the server agrees with you.

How it works

Two diagnostics work everywhere:

curl -s localhost:8080/ready     # {"status":"ok","database":"ok","storage":"ok"}
curl -s localhost:8080/metrics | grep -E 'hash_mismatch|replication_queue|gc_bytes'

The gRPC health service mirrors readiness and is probed every 10 s (redb ping + storage-root check), flipping NOT_SERVING within about 15 s of a real fault.

Usage

Issue catalog — Symptom / Diagnose / Fix:

1. Login returns 401

Symptom. POST /auth/login → 401 unauthorized, or calls fail 401 after a restart. Diagnose. curl -s -X POST localhost:8080/api/v1/auth/login -d '{"username":"admin","password":"admin123"}' -H 'Content-Type: application/json'; check audit auth.login.failure events; compare auth.jwt_secret across nodes and restarts; check the node clock (date -u) — JWT validation allows only 5 s of leeway for iat/exp. Fix. Correct the credentials; set the same FS_AUTH__JWT_SECRET cluster-wide (rotation invalidates all tokens — see Runbook); fix clock skew or move the nodes under NTP.

2. Upload returns 413

Symptom. Large upload rejected, REST code payload_too_large. Diagnose. Compare the file size against limits.max_body (default 1 GiB, the body cap) and limits.max_file_size (default 10 GiB); both are listed in the Configuration reference. Fix. Raise the relevant cap via FS_LIMITS__MAX_BODY / FS_LIMITS__MAX_FILE_SIZE and restart; max_body also caps non-file requests.

3. Upload returns 403

Symptom. forbidden on POST /files or Upload. Diagnose. Check the account’s role: viewer is read-only. Also check cross-tenant behaviour: passing a tenant_id (or uploading into another tenant) requires the admin role. Fix. Use a user-role account for writes; drop the tenant override or authenticate as admin.

4. Download returns 404 after delete

Symptom. File vanishes from default listings but disks do not shrink. Diagnose. Deletes are soft: the manifest keeps deleted_at_ms until GC. As admin: curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $A" 'localhost:8080/api/v1/files?include_deleted=true' | jq '.items[] | select(.deleted)'. Fix. Restore is not exposed — re-upload or restore from backup; wait out retention.grace_period_secs for space (see GC Operations), or purge directly with DELETE ?purge=true (admin).

5. Range request returns 416

Symptom. GET /files/:id/range → 416 with body bytes */size. Diagnose. The request Range: bytes=start-end is inclusive and end is beyond EOF only when start exceeds size; empty files (size 0) cannot satisfy any range, including suffix -N. Fix. Clamp start below the file size from /meta; for empty files use a plain download. gRPC Download clamps end but rejects start >= size with OUT_OF_RANGE.

6. Conditional requests never return 304

Symptom. If-None-Match is always ignored; full body re-downloads. Diagnose. curl -sD- -o /dev/null -H "Authorization: Bearer $T" URL and read the ETag header — it is "<64-hex>" with quotes. Sending the value without quotes does not match. Fix. Return the ETag verbatim in If-None-Match, quotes included; a 304 then carries Cache-Control: private, max-age=3600.

7. gRPC calls fail UNAUTHENTICATED

Symptom. Every RPC after login returns Unauthenticated. Diagnose. Intercepters read authorization: Bearer <jwt> metadata on unary calls; streaming RPCs verify in-handler but the metadata requirement is identical. Dump what you send: grpcurl -v prints outgoing headers. Fix. Attach the metadata after AuthService/Login (SDK login() does this automatically; manual clients must set the header on every call, including streams).

8. gRPC PERMISSION_DENIED on another tenant’s data

Symptom. Valid JWT, correct file id, PermissionDenied. Diagnose. The token’s tenant claim scopes every access; cross-tenant reads/writes require the admin role. Compare the manifest’s tenant_id (via an admin GetManifest) with your claim. Fix. Use an account of the owning tenant, or an admin account.

9. gRPC UNIMPLEMENTED mentioning gzip

Symptom. Client fails with UNIMPLEMENTED / “gzip not supported” although the server accepts gzip in both directions. Diagnose. The negotiation headers disagree: a client that advertises grpc-accept-encoding: gzip must be able to decompress it. In Go this means importing the gzip codec (_ "google.golang.org/grpc/encoding/gzip"); in tonic, accept_compressed/send_compressed must be set symmetrically. Fix. Register gzip client-side, or stop advertising/requesting it on one side.

10. Reflection lists no services

Symptom. grpcurl -plaintext host list returns nothing or errors. Diagnose. Reflection is registered at boot only — check you are on the gRPC port (server.grpc_port, default 9090), not the REST port 8080; docker compose run --rm grpcurl proves the path works inside the network. Fix. Point the client at 9090; ensure any proxy forwards HTTP/2 (h2c) rather than HTTP/1.1.

11. Health reports NOT_SERVING

Symptom. grpc.health.v1.Health/Check returns NOT_SERVING for a service or overall. Diagnose. The 10 s probe pings redb and stats the storage root: check the data volume is mounted (df -h), the disk is writable, and only one process owns the data dir. curl -s localhost:8080/ready shows which component fails. Fix. Remount/repair the volume, free disk, or remove the duplicate process; health flips back within ~15 s.

12. Replication queue depth keeps rising

Symptom. replication_queue_depth grows; replication_failures_total increases. Diagnose. curl -s http://replica:8081/health for reachability; compare FS_REPLICATION__API_KEY on both nodes; read primary logs for replication flush failed. Fix. Restore the target or fix the key mismatch (restart both — see Replication Ops); the queue drains automatically on recovery.

13. Replica reads are slow

Symptom. First read of each file on the replica takes noticeably longer. Diagnose. A fresh replica misses blobs and pulls each one from upstream_url during the read — per-blob latency, measured by download_duration_seconds with source=disk. Fix. Preseed: re-seed via admin resync (procedure 3 in Replication Ops) or rely on the L2 cache and read-ahead after the first pass.

14. Disk full on the primary

Symptom. Uploads fail; filesystem at 100%. Diagnose. df -h /var/lib/file-storage; du -sh data/blocks data/tmp; check tombstone backlog (include_deleted listing) and gc_bytes_reclaimed_total. Fix. Trigger POST /api/v1/admin/gc, shorten grace_period_secs, purge specific files with ?purge=true, or expand the volume — full procedure in Runbook.

15. redb busy / locked errors

Symptom. Boot fails or requests 500 with database_error mentioning the lock. Diagnose. redb is single-writer per file: two processes on the same data dir (forgotten dev server, two replicas on one volume) contend on the lock. Fix. Ensure exactly one process per data directory: pgrep -a file-storage, docker ps; remove the stray process and restart.

16. HashMismatch / corruption errors

Symptom. Downloads return 500 hash_mismatch; hash_mismatch_total rises; gRPC reports DATA_LOSS. Diagnose. BLAKE3 verification failed after a disk load — treat as corruption. Identify the affected file from audit entries and its blob path from /meta?chunks=true (chunk hash → ab/cd/<hash>.bin). Fix. Follow runbook scenario 3: isolate, restore the blob from replica/backup, verify, then check hardware — fsync’d writes make disk-level tearing unlikely, so suspect RAM or device failure.

17. Dedup numbers are zero

Symptom. Re-uploading the same file reports dedup_chunks: 0. Diagnose. Dedup is byte-exact per tenant: different content hashes differently, and with encryption.enabled=true the store key is BLAKE3(tenant_id || plain_hash), so cross-tenant duplication can never hit. Fix. Compare bytes (sha256sum locally) and stay within one tenant; the GC Operations page explains the refcount interplay.

18. Tokens expire “early” on some nodes

Symptom. Intermittent 401s across a cluster with one shared secret. Diagnose. Per-node clock skew against the 5 s JWT leeway: date -u; curl -sI localhost:8080/health | grep -i date. Fix. Sync clocks (chrony/NTP); keep auth.access_ttl_secs (900) realistic for your session flow.

19. Container cannot write the data volume

Symptom. Crash loop at boot with permission denied on /var/lib/file-storage/data. Diagnose. The distroless runtime runs as a nonroot UID (65532); a fresh named volume is root-owned. Fix. Initialise ownership once: docker run --rm -v fs_primary:/d alpine chown -R 65532:65532 /d, or bind-mount a host directory pre-owned by that UID.

20. grpcurl works but the SDK fails

Symptom. grpcurl -insecure succeeds; your client gets TLS errors or connection refused. Diagnose. grpcurl with -insecure skips verification — your SDK does not. Compare the certificate SANs (openssl x509 -in cert.pem -text) with the hostname/IP you dial; also confirm the SDK dials 9090 and speaks h2. Fix. Dial the exact DNS name (or IP SAN) in the certificate and pass the matching CA; see TLS.

Examples

A compact first-response script that labels the four buckets:

#!/usr/bin/env bash
B=http://localhost:8080
curl -s $B/ready
curl -s -o /dev/null -w 'auth-probe=%{http_code}\n' -X POST $B/api/v1/auth/login \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"username":"admin","password":"wrong"}'
curl -s $B/metrics | grep -E 'hash_mismatch_total|replication_queue_depth|gc_bytes_reclaimed_total'

See also

  • Runbook — the five incident scenarios behind issues 12, 14, 16 and more.
  • GC Operations — deletion semantics and space reclaim.
  • Replication Ops — queue, backpressure and repair.
  • Configuration reference — every limit and default quoted above.
  • Monitoring — the metrics used as diagnostic evidence.

Runbook

Five incident scenarios with the full response path: Symptoms → Diagnosis → Mitigation → Resolution → Prevention. Every command assumes the Compose deployment (primary on 8080/9090, replica on 8081/9091) — substitute your hosts and credentials. Symptom-level quick fixes are in Troubleshooting; metric references in Monitoring.

Overview

Triage order for any incident: (1) is the node ready (GET /ready), (2) is data safe (metrics: hash_mismatch_total, disk space), (3) is the cluster consistent (replication queue), (4) is access controlled (auth/audit). Mitigate first — stop bleeding (disk, corruption spread) — then resolve and only then tune prevention.

How it works

Each scenario below is self-contained: the diagnosis commands are copy-pasteable, the mitigation is reversible where possible, and prevention names the alert or config change that stops recurrence. Config changes require a restart (no hot reload); JWT and tenant-key operations deliberately invalidate sessions — that is part of the procedure, not a bug.

Usage

Scenario 1 — Primary disk full

Symptoms. Uploads fail (500 io_error or 503), filesystem near 100%, gc_bytes_reclaimed_total flat.

Diagnosis.

df -h /var/lib/file-storage
du -sh /var/lib/file-storage/data/blocks /var/lib/file-storage/data/tmp
curl -s localhost:8080/metrics | grep -E 'gc_bytes_reclaimed_total|gc_files_purged_total'
# Tombstone backlog (admin):
curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN" \
  'localhost:8080/api/v1/files?include_deleted=true&limit=1000' | jq '[.items[]|select(.deleted)]|length'

Mitigation. Free space at once: trigger a pass (POST /api/v1/admin/gc), and remove spool leftovers in data/tmp older than a day if the multipart spool is the offender.

Resolution. Shorten the grace period so the backlog purges, restart, trigger GC again, then restore the original value:

FS_RETENTION__GRACE_PERIOD_SECS=300 docker compose up -d primary
curl -s -X POST -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN" localhost:8080/api/v1/admin/gc

For targeted space, purge known-obsolete files directly (DELETE /api/v1/files/:id?purge=true, admin) or expand the volume.

Prevention. Alert on filesystem utilisation (node_exporter) and on FileStorageGcStalled (gc_bytes_reclaimed_total flat while disk fills) from Monitoring.

Scenario 2 — Replica lag growing

Symptoms. replication_queue_depth climbs toward the 10000 capacity; replication_failures_total increases; replica total file count falls behind primary.

Diagnosis.

curl -s localhost:8080/metrics  | grep -E 'replication_(queue_depth|failures_total)'
curl -s http://replica:8081/health
docker compose exec primary printenv FS_REPLICATION__API_KEY
docker compose exec replica  printenv FS_REPLICATION__API_KEY

Mitigation. Restore the target: restart the replica container, fix a mismatched api_key, or repair the network path. Uploads are not failing (async mode backpressures near capacity) — do not restart the primary yet: its in-memory queue would be lost.

Resolution. Once flushes succeed (queue draining, failures flat), close any manifest gap: resync per file with POST /api/v1/admin/resync/:id, or re-seed completely (Replication Ops) if the queue was lost.

Prevention. Alert FileStorageReplicationQueueBacklog (depth > 8000) and FileStorageReplicationFailing (failures increasing) — both in Monitoring.

Scenario 3 — Corruption detected

Symptoms. Downloads of specific files return 500 hash_mismatch; hash_mismatch_total increases; gRPC surfaces DATA_LOSS.

Diagnosis. Identify the affected file from the audit log (GET /api/v1/audit), then pull its chunk map to locate the stored blob:

curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN" "localhost:8080/api/v1/files/$ID/meta?chunks=true" | jq
# blob path: $STORAGE_ROOT/<hash[0:2]>/<hash[2:4]>/<hash>.bin (shard_depth=2)
b3sum /var/lib/file-storage/data/blocks/ab/cd/<hash>.bin   # compare against the stored blob's key

Remember: for encrypted tenants the blob is ciphertext keyed by BLAKE3(tenant_id || plain_hash) — compare against the stored form, not the plaintext hash.

Mitigation. Stop writes if more files are failing (stop the process) — writes themselves are verified, but a failing disk or RAM can spread damage. Repoint reads at the replica if it is healthy.

Resolution. Restore the blob from the replica or backup: fetch the correct bytes (GET /internal/chunk/:hash with X-Api-Key from the replica, or ReplicationService/PullChunk, or your backup copy), overwrite the blob file with correct ownership, then re-download the file and confirm the hash checks out. If several files fail, restore from the last volume snapshot (Backup & Restore) and resync forward. Then check hardware: blob writes are fsynced before the index commit, so torn write windows should not exist — persistent mismatches point to RAM, disk or firmware faults; run smartctl -a, memtest, and review controller logs.

Prevention. Run a periodic verification job (write your own loop: list files, download, compare X-Content-Hash with b3sum) and page on any hash_mismatch_total increase.

Scenario 4 — JWT secret rotation

Symptoms. Planned maintenance: every access token must become invalid (suspected leak, staff departure, schedule).

Diagnosis. Confirm scope: which nodes share FS_AUTH__JWT_SECRET; how many active sessions exist (audit auth.login.success rate).

Mitigation. Choose a low-traffic window; generate the new secret (openssl rand -base64 32, ≥16 chars required).

Resolution. Rolling restart — brief re-login wave, no data risk:

openssl rand -base64 32   # new secret
# primary: set FS_AUTH__JWT_SECRET=<new>, restart; repeat on the replica

All access tokens signed with the old secret are rejected immediately. Refresh tokens are in-memory, so the restart clears them — users simply log in again. A blue-green switch sharing one storage directory is not an option: redb is single-writer per data file, so a second process cannot take over the same volume; accept the rolling restart.

Prevention. Schedule rotation (e.g. quarterly), keep auth.access_ttl_secs short (default 900) so the blast radius of any secret stays small, and store secrets in a managed vault rather than compose files.

Scenario 5 — Tenant key compromise

Symptoms. A per-tenant AES-256-GCM key file (encryption.keys_dir) is exposed, or a tenant’s credentials are.

Diagnosis. Confirm exposure window; review what the attacker could read or write:

curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN" 'localhost:8080/api/v1/audit?limit=200' | jq
ls -l /var/lib/file-storage/data/keys/   # mode 0600 per tenant key

Mitigation. Stop the tenant’s writes: rotate its user passwords in config (users are config-defined) and restart. Keys are looked up per tenant id — do not simply swap the key file for the same tenant: existing blobs would become undecryptable.

Resolution. The practical re-encryption path:

  1. With the old key still in place, export the tenant’s data: list its files and download every one (plaintext, as an operator or the tenant’s admin).
  2. Create a new tenant: POST /api/v1/tenants {"name": "..."} (admin) — a fresh key file is materialised for the new tenant id.
  3. Re-upload the exported files under the new tenant.
  4. Delete the old tenant’s files (soft delete), trigger GC until manifests_purged/bytes_reclaimed settle, then remove the old tenant’s key file manually so no future blob can be decrypted with it.
  5. Invalidate sessions: the restarts in step 1 already cleared refresh tokens; outstanding JWTs expire within access_ttl_secs.
  6. Complete the audit review: GET /api/v1/audit for file.upload/file.download anomalies during the exposure window, and record the incident against the tenant name.

Prevention. Restrict keys_dir permissions and host access, audit auth.login.failure spikes, and rehearse this procedure on a staging replica before you ever need it.

Examples

Keep a one-line incident log per scenario; the template matches the structure above:

2026-11-10 SC3 corruption | file 0193...abcd | blob ab/cd/9f86...bin | restored from replica 14:22Z | smartctl clean | follow-up: enable verification loop

See also

  • Troubleshooting — fast symptom lookup.
  • Backup & Restore — restore and verification procedures.
  • Replication Ops — resync and re-seed mechanics.
  • GC Operations — grace-period and purge controls used in scenarios 1 and 5.
  • Monitoring — the alert rules referenced for prevention.

Scenario 6 — Drift reconciliation (M-01)

When: after crash-loop incidents, kill -9 during uploads, disk-full aborts, or as the scheduled hourly hygiene pass. The reconcile pass is the permanent guarantee behind the transaction-sized C-03 crash window: it finds state that should not exist and repairs it.

What it does (rule 16 — DryRun never deletes):

  1. walks every manifest and collects the referenced chunk hashes;
  2. walks the blob directory, classifying each blob as referenced / orphan;
  3. counts phantom rows (index rows whose blob file is missing);
  4. Repair additionally tombstones chunk rows with refcount > 0 but zero manifest references — the decision is made inside ONE Durability::Immediate transaction that re-walks every manifest, so a manifest committed mid-pass keeps its chunks (C-03 race defense) — and then lets the normal GC sweep reclaim the tombstoned blobs.

On demand (audited, admin-only, both transports):

# report only — never deletes
curl -s -X POST "localhost:8080/api/v1/admin/gc?reconcile=true&dry_run=true" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN" | jq

# repair — tombstone + sweep
curl -s -X POST "localhost:8080/api/v1/admin/gc?reconcile=true" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN" | jq

gRPC: AdminService/TriggerGC with reconcile=true (dry_run=true for the read-only report).

Schedule: retention.reconcile_interval_secs (default 3600, 0 disables). Locking: concurrent GC passes and admin triggers queue behind the in-process lock; a second process sharing the data dir gets 503 Unavailable from the run/reconcile.lock flock.

Alerting: file_storage_gc_reconcile_leaked_total counts orphans a Repair pass observed. Expected ~0; steady growth means the crash window is being exercised (crash loops, kill -9 mid-upload) or a peer shares the data dir unsafely.

CI

Status: V5 Wave 2. The matrix gates every fix wave; each job traces back to an audit finding.

Jobs (.github/workflows/ci.yml)

JobWhat it gatesOrigin
