Running the gates locally

cargo fmt --all -- --check
cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings
cargo test --locked
./scripts/check_no_handler_validation.sh

# C-01 boot story (paths overridden for a scratch boot):
cargo build --release --bin file-storage
FS_STORAGE__ROOT=/tmp/fsroot/blocks FS_STORAGE__TEMP_DIR=/tmp/fsroot/tmp \
FS_DATABASE__PATH=/tmp/fsroot/index.redb FS_ENCRYPTION__KEYS_DIR=/tmp/fsroot/keys \
  ./target/release/file-storage --config secrets/file-storage.yaml.example
# (fill the PASTE_ placeholders first; see secrets/file-storage.yaml.example)

# dashboard smoke (seeds traffic itself):
./scripts/dashboard_smoke.sh http://127.0.0.1:8080

Notes

  • The Prometheus exposition is sparse: only touched series render. dashboard_smoke.sh seeds traffic (login/upload/download ×2/admin dry-run reconcile) before asserting, and treats pure alarm counters (hash_mismatch, repair_hash_mismatch_total, gc_reconcile_leaked_total, register_race) as legitimately absent on a healthy node — dashboards query them with rate()/absent().
  • The fuzz targets under fuzz/ run out-of-band (cargo +nightly fuzz, I-04); they are not part of the default workspace on purpose.
  • Background instances: always kill -9 the exact PID in scripts — the binary’s graceful shutdown waits on in-flight replication workers.

Wave 2 Report — P1 Closure + CI Hardening (V5)

Date: 2026-11-12 · Branch protocol: one fix/<ID>-<slug> branch per finding, fix(<ID>): commits, gate test per fix (red where the pre-fix behavior was observable at runtime). Full contract table: REMEDIATION_REPORT.md §“Wave 2”.

What landed

PhaseFindingDesignStatus
W2-AM-04 (validation unification)executed V5 §3 / V4 §5.5fixed — 5 runtime-red gates → green
W2-BM-07 (repair verify-then-persist)executed V5 §4 / V4 §5.8fixed — 4 gates + quarantine units; bench recorded
W2-CM-05 (streaming parity + login lockout)executed V5 §5 / V4 §5.6fixed — 7 gates (3 runtime-red)
W2-DM-01 (reconcile pass)executed V5 §6 / V4 §5.3fixed — 6 gates
W2-ECI-W1..CI-W5executed V5 §7fixed — boot gate + dashboard smoke verified live; size 11.85 MiB < 15 MiB

Plus one Wave-1 regression caught and fixed mid-wave (see below).

The four P1 fixes in one paragraph each

M-04. src/core/validation.rs is now the single source of truth for upload-request shape: name 1..=255 bytes with C0/C1 banned, ≤32 tags of 1..=64 bytes (L-06), mime ≤128 bytes, expected_size capped at max_file_size before admission control on both transports (REST gained ?expected_size=), and include_deleted admin-only with an explicit 403/PERMISSION_DENIED (the old REST silently honored it for viewers — the audit’s leak). GetManifest caps chunk lists at 1000 with the additive chunks_truncated proto field (replication stays full-fidelity), and both transports count list totals through one IndexDb::list_filtered. CI rule no-handler-validation (grep gate, negative-tested) keeps the invariant structural.

M-07. The replica repair path is verify-then-persist: pulled bytes are UNTRUSTED and must decrypt/decompress and BLAKE3-match the chunk reference’s plain_hash before anything reaches the CAS or L2. Mismatches are quarantined (<root>/../quarantine/<hash>.bin, size-bounded with oldest-mtime eviction, boot-swept after the 7-day retention), the L2 entry invalidated, file_storage_repair_hash_mismatch_total{source="repair"} ticks and the caller sees Corruption. Ciphertext-only replicas (no tenant key) keep the legacy order with a per-event warn and a dedicated metric — documented as a degraded posture in the threat model. Gate tests drive a REAL mock upstream over the replication wire protocol.

M-05. One per-tenant token-bucket governor lives in AppState; the unary interceptor and the streaming Upload/Download handlers take tokens from the same instance (pointer-identity gate + behavioral flood gate), so the previously documented streaming bypass is closed. LoginThrottle locks out usernames exponentially (1s base, ×2, 15m cap, default ON) BEFORE the argon2 verify; only real credential failures grow the backoff, verifier errors never do (rule 17 asserted), success resets, and every throttled attempt is audited as auth.login.throttled with retry_after. I-06’s wholesale clear() is gone: governor and throttle share a BoundedMap with insertion-order oldest eviction.

M-01. The reconcile pass (hourly by default, admin-triggerable on both transports, audited) walks every manifest, classifies blobs/rows, and in Repair mode tombstones rows with refcount>0 but zero manifest references — decided inside ONE Durability::Immediate transaction that re-walks every manifest (the C-03 race defense) — then reclaims them through the C-03-safe victim sweep. Dry-run is read-only (rule 16, asserted). In-process GC/reconcile mutual exclusion plus a cross-process lock file (1h stale-steal, MSRV-safe). file_storage_gc_reconcile_leaked_total makes leak rate observable; the README crash-safety sentence is now true.

Wave-1 regression fixed mid-wave

race_gc_upload_x100 (flaky ~1-in-3 full-suite runs under parallel load) exposed a genuine residual C-03 interleaving: the Wave-1 sweep re-checked references BEFORE the unlink, so an upload could register in the gap and lose its blob under a live row. Fixed with a two-phase GC delete barrier (finalize in-txn → atomic .gc-pending rename claim → resurrection re-check restores → delete only when unreferenced), recorded as regression(W1-C-03) in REMEDIATION_REPORT.md. Green ×5 consecutive runs + full suite.

Numbers

  • Tests: 206 → 251 green (0 failed), 2 ignored (nightly-only).
  • clippy -D warnings clean · fmt clean · cargo audit 0 non-allowlisted advisories · cargo deny check clean.
  • Bench (post-M-07, same 2-vCPU sandbox): cdc_chunk_4mb 1176 MB/s · zstd_128kb 1878 MB/s · upload_2mb 253 MB/s · download_2mb_cold 29.1 µs — no cold-path regression (repair only fires on disk-miss-with-upstream).
  • Binary (musl release): 12,421,616 B = 11.85 MiB (< 15 MiB guard).

Deferred to V6 (ticketed, unchanged)

M-09 (route-scoped /internal body limit), M-10 (audit actor plumbing), M-12 (replication supervisor), M-16 (tenant-scoped dedup keys), P2 (M-03, M-08, M-11, group-commit, smalls, WAL), P3. Reasons per row in REMEDIATION_REPORT.md.

Verification checklist (V5 §11 deltas)

  • Pre-flight green (206/0; clippy/fmt/audit/deny clean; git baseline deviation recorded: Wave-1 commits squashed to f47c474 on session recycle — substance verified by the pre-flight suite).
  • M-04/M-07/M-05/M-01 rows marked fixed with gate lists.
  • CI-W1..CI-W5 landed; boot gate + dashboard smoke verified live locally; binary size verified.
  • Rules 15/16/17 asserted by tests (grep gate negative-tested; dry-run-never-deletes; verifier-error-does-not-throttle).
  • audit-repro/ evidence preserved; PoCs remain expect-fail smokes.
  • Bench before/after recorded (M-07 perf-touching).
  • Re-audit scope: all CRITICAL/HIGH + M-01/M-04/M-05/M-07 closed — the surface is GO-ready for the V3 re-audit; remaining P1 (M-09/ M-10/M-12/M-16) explicitly V6.

Rust Client

The official SDK ships with the service crate: file_storage::grpc::client::FileStorageClient wraps the generated tonic clients with streaming upload from any AsyncRead, streaming download into any AsyncWrite, token management and a retry policy for unary calls. It is the same API exercised by the project’s 18 gRPC end-to-end tests, so every snippet below is tested behaviour. Protocol details are in gRPC API and the design rationale in SDK Design.

Overview

One struct covers files, auth and admin; a separate ReplicationClient exposes the internal replication protocol with x-api-key authentication. The SDK negotiates gzip in both directions, raises message limits to the server’s 256 MiB ceiling, and attaches authorization: Bearer <jwt> metadata to every call — including streaming RPCs, where interceptors do not run.

ConcernSDK answer
Transportconnect (plaintext) / connect_tls (TLS with optional CA pinning)
Authlogin / refresh / logout, token stored client-side
Uploadupload(header, impl AsyncRead) — 1 MiB pieces, expected_size guard
Downloaddownload / download_range into impl AsyncWrite, returns DownloadHeader
RetryRetryPolicy — 3 attempts, 100 ms → 2 s capped backoff, transport codes only

How it works

upload builds a 32-slot bounded channel, sends the UploadHeader first, then spawns a reader task that copies 1 MiB pieces from your AsyncRead. The sender is moved into that task, so when the reader hits EOF the stream closes and the server observes end-of-stream — holding the sender open anywhere else would hang the server. download iterates the server stream, writes data frames to your writer and returns the header that arrived first; DownloadHeader.original_hash is the raw 32-byte BLAKE3 of the full plaintext, which you should verify locally.

Retries wrap unary calls only (manifests, listings, deletes, GC, tenants, audit). The default policy retries Unavailable, DeadlineExceeded and Aborted — business rejections such as NotFound or PermissionDenied surface immediately because retrying them cannot succeed.

Configuration

Add the dependency (the crate is published from the service repository; adjust to your registry):

[dependencies]
file-storage = { path = "../file-storage" }
# or: file-storage = { git = "https://github.com/your-org/file-storage", branch = "main" }
tokio  = { version = "1", features = ["macros", "rt-multi-thread", "io-util"] }
blake3 = "1"

Generated protocol types live at file_storage::grpc::generated::pb (aliased pb below); the raw generated tonic clients (FileServiceClient, AuthServiceClient, AdminServiceClient) are exported from the same module when you need streaming control the facade does not expose. RetryPolicy::new(max_attempts, initial_backoff, max_backoff) replaces the default via with_retry_policy.

Examples

Complete working program — connect, login, upload, download, verify the hash, delete:

use file_storage::grpc::client::FileStorageClient;
use file_storage::grpc::generated::pb;

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
    let mut client = FileStorageClient::connect("http://127.0.0.1:9090").await?;
    client.login("alice", "alice123").await?;

    let payload = b"hello file-storage".to_vec();
    let header = pb::UploadHeader {
        name: "hello.txt".into(),
        mime_type: "text/plain".into(),
        expected_size: payload.len() as u64, // oversize streams fail fast
        tenant_id: String::new(),            // own tenant; admin may override
        tags: vec!["demo".into()],
    };
    let resp = client
        .upload(header, std::io::Cursor::new(payload.clone()))
        .await?;
    let file_id = resp.manifest.as_ref().unwrap().file_id.clone();
    println!("uploaded {file_id} dedup_chunks={} stored={}", resp.dedup_chunks, resp.stored_bytes);

    let mut downloaded = Vec::new();
    let hdr = client.download(&file_id, &mut downloaded).await?;
    let mut hasher = blake3::Hasher::new();
    hasher.update(&downloaded);
    assert_eq!(hdr.original_hash, hasher.finalize().as_bytes().to_vec());
    assert_eq!(downloaded, payload);

    client.delete_file(&file_id, false).await?; // soft delete
    Ok(())
}

Range download and verification of a slice:

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
let mut sink = tokio::fs::File::create("part.bin").await?;
// half-open range [1024, 2048): matches gRPC Download{start,end}
let hdr = client.download_range(&file_id, Some(1024), Some(2048), &mut sink).await?;
assert_eq!(hdr.size, 1024);
}

TLS with a pinned private CA (endpoint must use a SAN listed in the certificate):

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
let ca = tokio::fs::read("certs/ca.pem").await?;
let mut client = FileStorageClient::connect_tls("https://fs.example.com:9090", Some(&ca)).await?;
}

Error handling: every method returns tonic::Status; map status.code() against the catalog in Errors (NotFound → 404 not_found, ResourceExhausted → 413 payload_too_large, and so on). Status implements Display and carries details().

Usage notes

  • login() stores the access token for all subsequent calls; refresh(refresh_token) rotates the pair (the old refresh token is single-use). Tokens are in-memory — persist the refresh token if your process restarts.
  • trigger_gc(mode, dry_run) mirrors the admin API: dry_run=true returns UNIMPLEMENTED; use false.
  • list_files(limit, offset, tag, name, include_deleted) — server clamps limit to 1..1000; include_deleted is admin-only.
  • Streaming uploads are not retried by the policy; wrap them yourself if your storage layer tolerates re-sends (same-name upload replaces atomically).

See also

  • gRPC API — messages, streaming semantics, metadata.
  • SDK Design — why the streaming protocol looks like this.
  • Errors — full gRPC/REST error mapping.
  • TLS — server-side certificates and mTLS.
  • Authentication — token lifetimes and roles.
  • CLI Recipes — shell equivalents for quick checks.

Go Client

The gRPC API is defined in package file_storage.v1 under proto/file_storage/v1/, with go_package = "github.com/your-org/file-storage/api/v1;v1" already declared in the protos. Generate the Go stubs once, then implement upload as a client stream of 1 MiB pieces and download as a server-streaming loop. Message shapes and streaming semantics are documented in gRPC API.

Overview

A Go client needs four things: generated stubs, a channel (plaintext or TLS), the bearer token from AuthService/Login attached as authorization metadata, and the header-first streaming convention — the first UploadChunk message carries the header, every subsequent one carries bytes. Errors are inspected with status.FromError and the standard codes package.

How it works

Generate stubs with either toolchain from the repository root:

# buf (buf.yaml lives next to the protos; buf.gen.yaml below)
buf generate

# or plain protoc
protoc -I proto \
  --go_out=gen/go --go_opt=paths=source_relative \
  --go-grpc_out=gen/go --go-grpc_opt=paths=source_relative \
  proto/file_storage/v1/common.proto \
  proto/file_storage/v1/auth.proto \
  proto/file_storage/v1/file.proto \
  proto/file_storage/v1/admin.proto

# buf.gen.yaml
version: v1
plugins:
  - plugin: go
    out: gen/go
    opt: paths=source_relative
  - plugin: go-grpc
    out: gen/go
    opt: paths=source_relative

go get google.golang.org/grpc

Configuration

Dependencies for the example below:

go get google.golang.org/grpc@latest google.golang.org/protobuf@latest

If your server runs behind server.grpc_tls, load the CA into a x509.CertPool and dial with credentials.NewClientTLSFromCert(pool, "") — the name you dial must match a SAN in the certificate.

Examples

A complete client: dial, login, upload a file from an io.Reader in 1 MiB chunks, download it back to disk, and map errors.

package main

import (
	"context"
	"io"
	"log"
	"os"

	pb "github.com/your-org/file-storage/gen/go/file_storage/v1"
	"google.golang.org/grpc"
	"google.golang.org/grpc/codes"
	"google.golang.org/grpc/credentials/insecure"
	"google.golang.org/grpc/metadata"
	"google.golang.org/grpc/status"
)

const pieceSize = 1 << 20 // 1 MiB

func dial() (*grpc.ClientConn, error) {
	// Plaintext (internal). For TLS: credentials.NewClientTLSFromCert(pool, "").
	return grpc.NewClient("localhost:9090", grpc.WithTransportCredentials(insecure.NewCredentials()))
}

func main() {
	conn, err := dial()
	if err != nil {
		log.Fatal(err)
	}
	defer conn.Close()
	ctx := context.Background()

	// 1. Login; token travels as authorization metadata from here on.
	auth := pb.NewAuthServiceClient(conn)
	lr, err := auth.Login(ctx, &pb.LoginRequest{Username: "alice", Password: "alice123"})
	if err != nil {
		log.Fatal(err)
	}
	ctx = metadata.AppendToOutgoingContext(ctx, "authorization", "Bearer "+lr.AccessToken)
	files := pb.NewFileServiceClient(conn)

	// 2. Upload: header message first, then 1 MiB data pieces.
	src, _ := os.Open("report.pdf")
	defer src.Close()
	info, _ := src.Stat()
	stream, err := files.Upload(ctx)
	if err != nil {
		log.Fatal(err)
	}
	if err := stream.Send(&pb.UploadChunk{Payload: &pb.UploadChunk_Header{
		Header: &pb.UploadHeader{Name: "report.pdf", MimeType: "application/pdf",
			ExpectedSize: uint64(info.Size()), Tags: []string{"q1"}},
	}}); err != nil {
		log.Fatal(err)
	}
	buf := make([]byte, pieceSize)
	for {
		n, rerr := src.Read(buf)
		if n > 0 {
			if serr := stream.Send(&pb.UploadChunk{Payload: &pb.UploadChunk_Data{Data: buf[:n]}}); serr != nil {
				log.Fatal(serr)
			}
		}
		if rerr == io.EOF {
			break
		}
		if rerr != nil {
			log.Fatal(rerr)
		}
	}
	resp, err := stream.CloseAndRecv()
	if err != nil {
		log.Fatal(err)
	}
	id := resp.Manifest.FileId
	log.Printf("uploaded %s (dedup_chunks=%d stored=%d)", id, resp.DedupChunks, resp.StoredBytes)

	// 3. Download: server streams header then ordered data.
	dl, err := files.Download(ctx, &pb.DownloadRequest{FileId: id})
	if err != nil {
		log.Fatal(err)
	}
	dst, _ := os.Create("copy.pdf")
	defer dst.Close()
	for {
		msg, rerr := dl.Recv()
		if rerr == io.EOF {
			break
		}
		if rerr != nil {
			log.Fatal(rerr)
		}
		switch p := msg.Payload.(type) {
		case *pb.DownloadChunk_Header:
			log.Printf("total_size=%d mime=%s", p.Header.TotalSize, p.Header.MimeType)
		case *pb.DownloadChunk_Data:
			if _, werr := dst.Write(p.Data); werr != nil {
				log.Fatal(werr)
			}
		}
	}
	// hdr.OriginalHash (32-byte BLAKE3 of the full plaintext) is available on the
	// header message for end-to-end verification; verify with golang.org/x/crypto/blake2b
	// or your preferred BLAKE3 implementation.

	// 4. Soft delete; NOT_FOUND on an unknown id is expected and safe to ignore.
	if _, err := files.DeleteFile(ctx, &pb.DeleteFileRequest{FileId: id, Purge: false}); err != nil {
		if st, ok := status.FromError(err); ok && st.Code() == codes.NotFound {
			log.Println("already gone")
		} else {
			log.Fatal(err)
		}
	}
}

grpcurl interop check

Before writing Go, confirm reachability and shapes from the shell — this catches port, TLS and metadata mistakes instantly:

grpcurl -plaintext localhost:9090 list                      # reflection must list 4 services + health
grpcurl -plaintext -d '{"username":"alice","password":"alice123"}' \
  localhost:9090 file_storage.v1.AuthService/Login
grpcurl -plaintext -H "authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  -d '{"file_id": "<id>"}' localhost:9090 file_storage.v1.FileService/GetManifest

Usage notes

  • Ranges: DownloadRequest.Start is inclusive, End exclusive (Go slice semantics); out-of-range start >= size yields OUT_OF_RANGE.
  • Chunk pieces are arbitrary sizes — 1 MiB matches the server’s framing guidance and the Rust SDK; pieces ≤ 64 KiB per message arrive on PullChunk only.
  • PermissionDenied on another tenant’s file is by design; use an admin account for cross-tenant work.
  • For production, wrap unary calls with your own retry on codes.Unavailable only.

See also

  • gRPC API — every RPC and message field.
  • Authentication — token TTLs, refresh rotation, roles.
  • Errors — gRPC codes and their REST equivalents.
  • TLS — enabling server.grpc_tls and mTLS.
  • CLI Recipes — grpcurl patterns for debugging.
  • Rust Client — the reference SDK implementation.

Python Client

Python talks to the service through grpcio and stubs generated from proto/file_storage/v1/. This page walks through generating the modules, a complete synchronous client (login, streaming upload, streaming download, error handling), and an asyncio sketch with grpc.aio. The wire protocol is identical to the one documented in gRPC API, so Rust, Go and Python clients interoperate freely.

Overview

Three commands get you started:

python -m pip install grpcio grpcio-tools grpcio-health-checking grpcio-reflection
mkdir -p gen
python -m grpc_tools.protoc \
  -Iproto \
  -I"$(python -c 'import grpc_tools, os; print(os.path.join(os.path.dirname(grpc_tools.__file__), "_proto"))')" \
  --python_out=gen --pyi_out=gen --grpc_python_out=gen \
  proto/file_storage/v1/common.proto \
  proto/file_storage/v1/auth.proto \
  proto/file_storage/v1/file.proto \
  proto/file_storage/v1/admin.proto

The extra -I path supplies the well-known google/protobuf/timestamp.proto from the grpc_tools bundle. The result is importable as gen/file_storage/v1/{common,auth,file,admin}_pb2*.py (set sys.path to gen or package it).

How it works

Streaming follows the header-first rule. Upload is a request iterator whose first yielded message sets UploadChunk.header and whose remaining messages set UploadChunk.data — send 1 MiB pieces to keep memory flat. Download iterates the response stream: the first message carries DownloadChunk.header (including the 32-byte original_hash for verification) and the rest carry ordered plaintext bytes. The bearer token from Login must be attached as authorization: Bearer <jwt> metadata on every call, streams included.

Examples

Synchronous client

#!/usr/bin/env python3
import sys
sys.path.insert(0, "gen")

import hashlib
import grpc
from file_storage.v1 import auth_pb2, auth_pb2_grpc, file_pb2, file_pb2_grpc

TARGET = "localhost:9090"

with grpc.insecure_channel(TARGET) as channel:
    auth = auth_pb2_grpc.AuthServiceStub(channel)
    login = auth.Login(auth_pb2.LoginRequest(username="alice", password="alice123"))
    metadata = (("authorization", f"Bearer {login.access_token}"),)

    files = file_pb2_grpc.FileServiceStub(channel)

    # --- upload: yield the header, then byte chunks ---
    payload = b"hello from python" * 1000

    def requests():
        yield file_pb2.UploadChunk(
            header=file_pb2.UploadHeader(
                name="hello.txt",
                mime_type="text/plain",
                expected_size=len(payload),  # oversize streams fail fast
                tags=["demo"],
            )
        )
        for i in range(0, len(payload), 1 << 20):
            yield file_pb2.UploadChunk(data=payload[i : i + (1 << 20)])

    resp = files.Upload(requests(), metadata=metadata)
    file_id = resp.manifest.file_id
    print(f"uploaded {file_id} dedup_chunks={resp.dedup_chunks} stored={resp.stored_bytes}")

    # --- download: header message first, then data ---
    hasher = hashlib.blake2b(digest_size=32)  # or a BLAKE3 package
    with open("hello-copy.txt", "wb") as out:
        for msg in files.Download(file_pb2.DownloadRequest(file_id=file_id), metadata=metadata):
            kind = msg.WhichOneof("payload")
            if kind == "header":
                print(f"total_size={msg.header.total_size}")
            elif kind == "data":
                out.write(msg.data)
                hasher.update(msg.data)
    # Compare hasher.digest() with msg.header.original_hash for full verification.

    # --- soft delete ---
    files.DeleteFile(file_pb2.DeleteFileRequest(file_id=file_id), metadata=metadata)

Error handling

Every stub method raises grpc.RpcError; inspect e.code() against the catalog in Errors:

try:
    files.GetManifest(file_pb2.GetManifestRequest(file_id="0193-unknown"), metadata=metadata)
except grpc.RpcError as e:
    if e.code() == grpc.StatusCode.NOT_FOUND:
        print("missing:", e.details())
    elif e.code() == grpc.StatusCode.PERMISSION_DENIED:
        print("wrong tenant — needs an admin account")
    else:
        raise

asyncio sketch

grpc.aio mirrors the same API with coroutines; the request iterator becomes an async generator:

import asyncio
import grpc.aio

async def main():
    async with grpc.aio.insecure_channel(TARGET) as channel:
        auth = auth_pb2_grpc.AuthServiceStub(channel)
        login = await auth.Login(auth_pb2.LoginRequest(username="alice", password="alice123"))
        metadata = (("authorization", f"Bearer {login.access_token}"),)
        files = file_pb2_grpc.FileServiceStub(channel)

        async def requests():
            yield file_pb2.UploadChunk(header=file_pb2.UploadHeader(name="async.bin"))
            yield file_pb2.UploadChunk(data=b"chunk-1")
            yield file_pb2.UploadChunk(data=b"chunk-2")

        resp = await files.Upload(requests(), metadata=metadata)
        stream = files.Download(
            file_pb2.DownloadRequest(file_id=resp.manifest.file_id), metadata=metadata
        )
        async for msg in stream:
            if msg.WhichOneof("payload") == "data":
                print(len(msg.data), "bytes")

asyncio.run(main())

Configuration

Operational pointers for the Python side:

  • Health checks — use grpcio-health-checking against grpc.health.v1.Health; the server serves checks for file_storage.v1.FileService, AuthService, AdminService, ReplicationService and the overall service name "", probed internally every 10 s: 
    from grpc_health.v1 import health_pb2, health_pb2_grpc
health = health_pb2_grpc.HealthStub(channel)
resp = health.Check(health_pb2.HealthCheckRequest(service="file_storage.v1.FileService"))
print(resp.status == health_pb2.HealthCheckResponse.SERVING)
  • Reflection — grpcio-reflection can list services and resolve descriptors without generated stubs, mirroring grpcurl list; reflection is registered at boot together with the health service.
  • TLS — pass grpc.ssl_channel_credentials(root_certificates=ca_pem) to secure_channel; the dial name must match a SAN in the certificate (see TLS).
  • Keep pieces at 1 MiB; the server’s maximum message size is 256 MiB but large single messages defeat HTTP/2 flow control.

See also

  • gRPC API — full message and streaming reference.
  • Authentication — roles, refresh rotation, expiry.
  • Errors — status codes and REST equivalents.
  • SDK Design — why the header-first protocol exists.
  • CLI Recipes — grpcurl equivalents for smoke tests.
  • Quickstart — boot the server first.

CLI Recipes

Copy-paste recipes for driving both transports from the shell: curl against the REST API on port 8080 and grpcurl against gRPC on 9090. These are the fastest tools for smoke tests, CI probes and the first minute of an incident; each recipe maps one-to-one onto an endpoint documented in REST API or gRPC API. Authentication details are in Authentication.

Overview

Two conventions run through this page: capture the JWT once into $TOKEN (or $ADMIN for admin calls), and pipe everything through jq for readable output. REST errors are JSON {"error":{"code","message"}}; gRPC errors surface as Error: ... code = NotFound desc = ... in grpcurl output.

How it works

Login and basic REST calls

BASE=http://localhost:8080/api/v1
TOKEN=$(curl -s -X POST $BASE/auth/login -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"username":"alice","password":"alice123"}' | jq -r .access_token)

Upload — raw streamed body and multipart:

head -c 64M /dev/urandom > /tmp/f.bin

# raw body; name/mime/tags as query parameters
curl -s -X POST "$BASE/files?name=f.bin&mime=application/octet-stream&tags=demo,raw" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" -H 'Content-Type: application/octet-stream' \
  --data-binary @/tmp/f.bin | jq

# multipart; the file field is named `file`
curl -s -X POST $BASE/files -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  -F name=report.pdf -F tags=q1,finance -F file=@report.pdf | jq

Both return 201 with {file_id, name, size, size_stored, chunk_count, content_hash, dedup_chunks, dedup_bytes, ...} — capture the id:

FILE_ID=$(curl -s -X POST ... -F file=@f.bin | jq -r .file_id)

Download, range and conditional requests:

curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" $BASE/files/$FILE_ID -o out.bin        # full
curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" -H 'Range: bytes=0-1023' \
  $BASE/files/$FILE_ID/range -o part.bin                                          # 206
curl -s -D- -o /dev/null -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  -H "If-None-Match: \"<etag-from-headers>\"" $BASE/files/$FILE_ID                # 304 (quotes required)

Metadata, list, delete, purge, GC and audit:

curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" "$BASE/files/$FILE_ID/meta?chunks=true" | jq '.chunks[0]'
curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" "$BASE/files?tag=q1&limit=20" | jq '.total, .items[].name'
curl -s -X DELETE -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" $BASE/files/$FILE_ID                     # soft, 204
curl -s -X DELETE -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN" "$BASE/files/$FILE_ID?purge=true"        # hard, 204
curl -s -X POST -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN" $BASE/admin/gc | jq
curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN" "$BASE/audit?limit=20" | jq '.[] | {action, actor, object, status}'

grpcurl recipes

Discovery, health and auth (no proto files needed — reflection is registered on the server):

grpcurl -plaintext localhost:9090 list                        # 4 services + health
grpcurl -plaintext localhost:9090 describe file_storage.v1.FileService
grpcurl -plaintext -d '{"service": ""}' localhost:9090 grpc.health.v1.Health/Check
grpcurl -plaintext -d '{"username":"admin","password":"admin123"}' \
  localhost:9090 file_storage.v1.AuthService/Login

Authenticated unary calls — pass the JWT as authorization metadata:

TOKEN=$(grpcurl -plaintext -d '{"username":"alice","password":"alice123"}' \
  localhost:9090 file_storage.v1.AuthService/Login | jq -r .access_token)

grpcurl -plaintext -H "authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  -d '{"limit": 10, "offset": 0}' localhost:9090 file_storage.v1.FileService/ListFiles

grpcurl -plaintext -H "authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  -d '{"mode": 1}' localhost:9090 file_storage.v1.AdminService/TriggerGC    # 1 = REFCOUNT, 2 = REACHABILITY

Streaming RPCs need the proto files plus -d @ to read newline-delimited JSON messages from stdin:

# Upload (client-streaming): first message = header, then data messages.
# Large files: feed base64 "data" lines from a generator, or use the SDKs —
# grpcurl buffers stdin, so it is a smoke tool, not a bulk uploader.
grpcurl -plaintext -H "authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  -import-path proto -proto proto/file_storage/v1/file.proto \
  -d @ localhost:9090 file_storage.v1.FileService/Upload <<'EOF'
{"header": {"name": "cli.txt", "mime_type": "text/plain"}}
{"data": "aGVsbG8gZmlsZS1zdG9yYWdl"}
EOF

# Download (server-streaming) and AdminService/Resync
grpcurl -plaintext -H "authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  -d '{"file_id": "<id>"}' localhost:9090 file_storage.v1.FileService/Download
grpcurl -plaintext -H "authorization: Bearer $ADMIN" \
  -d '{"file_id": "<id>"}' localhost:9090 file_storage.v1.AdminService/Resync

Internal replication calls authenticate with x-api-key metadata instead of a JWT:

grpcurl -plaintext -H "x-api-key: cluster-api-key-change-me" \
  -d '{"file_id": "<id>"}' localhost:9090 file_storage.v1.ReplicationService/PullManifest

jq one-liners

curl -s "$BASE/files?limit=1000" -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  | jq -r '.items[] | [.file_id, .name, (.size|tostring)] | @tsv'        # TSV inventory
curl -s $BASE/files/$FILE_ID/meta?chunks=true -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  | jq '[.chunks[] | .size_stored] | add'                                  # stored bytes
curl -s localhost:8080/metrics | grep file_storage_replication_queue_depth # lag gauge

Configuration

Compose grpcurl profile

docker-compose.yml ships a one-shot grpcurl service under the grpc-test profile — service discovery from inside the compose network:

docker compose run --rm grpcurl          # runs: grpcurl -plaintext primary:9090 list

Scripting tips

  • Always propagate request identity: curl -s -D- -o /dev/null -H "x-request-id: $(uuidgen)" ... — the server echoes/propagates it, and the id appears in logs and audit detail, which makes support tickets tractable.
  • For CI probes prefer GET /health (unauthenticated) over login flows; use grpc.health.v1.Health/Check with {"service": "file_storage.v1.FileService"} for the gRPC side.
  • Capture file_id and ETag into variables immediately; UUIDs are the only stable handle across renames.
  • Keep x-request-id + Content-Length in -D- header dumps when filing reproduction steps.
  • For TLS deployments, add --insecure (grpcurl) or --cacert ca.pem (curl against a REST ingress) as appropriate; SANs must match dial names — see TLS.

Examples

A complete smoke test suitable for a post-deploy job:

#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail
BASE=http://localhost:8080/api/v1
TOKEN=$(curl -sf -X POST $BASE/auth/login -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"username":"viewer","password":"viewer123"}' | jq -r .access_token)
test "$(curl -sf -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" $BASE/files?limit=1 | jq .total)" -ge 0
curl -sf localhost:8080/ready | jq -e '.status == "ok"' >/dev/null && echo "REST OK"
grpcurl -plaintext -d '{"service": ""}' localhost:9090 grpc.health.v1.Health/Check | grep -q SERVING && echo "gRPC OK"

See also

  • REST API — parameter and response reference for every curl above.
  • gRPC API — the RPCs behind the grpcurl calls.
  • Authentication — users, roles and token TTLs.
  • Errors — decoding the error bodies and status codes.
  • Quickstart — installing and booting the stack.

SDK Design

This page documents the design patterns implemented by the Rust SDK in src/grpc/client/ and the ones worth copying when you write a client in any other language. They are not stylistic preferences — each one exists because the protocol is streaming, HTTP/2-backed and auth-metadata-driven, and getting them wrong produces deadlocks, unbounded memory or unretryable-looking failures. Protocol surface: gRPC API; reference implementation walkthrough: Rust Client.

Overview

The SDK’s job is to hide four protocol realities behind a small facade: (1) streams start with a typed header message, (2) backpressure is HTTP/2 flow control, so client memory must be bounded by design, (3) authentication travels as authorization metadata that must be attached manually to streaming RPCs, and (4) only transport-level failures are safe to retry. Everything else — TLS, error mapping, tests — follows from those constraints.

upload(file, AsyncRead)                         download(file_id, AsyncWrite)
    |                                                |
    v                                                v
[ 32-slot bounded channel ]            server stream (HTTP/2 window 4 MiB)
    | 1 MiB UploadChunk frames         header frame -> data frames
    v                                        |
reader task holds the sender                 v
EOF drops sender -> stream closes     writer receives until stream ends

Figure 1: Bounded producer/consumer on both transports; EOF is signalled by dropping the sender, not by a flag.

How it works

Transport-agnostic facade

FileStorageClient wraps the three generated clients (FileServiceClient, AuthServiceClient, AdminServiceClient<Channel>) behind one type with plain methods (login, upload, download, get_manifest, list_files, delete_file, trigger_gc, list_tenants, query_audit). Generated tonic clients are cheap to clone, so each method clones its inner client per call — no shared mutable state. Copy this layering in other languages: generated stubs stay mechanical; the facade owns token state, retries and framing defaults.

Header-first streaming protocol

UploadChunk and DownloadChunk are oneof payload { header | data }, and the first message must be the header. Why: the server can authenticate, validate the name and enforce expected_size (failing with RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED) before it has buffered a single byte of content; on download the header carries original_hash first, so the client can plan verification while bytes flow. Never send a bare data message first — the server rejects the stream.

Bounded channels for backpressure

The upload facade reads your AsyncRead in 1 MiB pieces into a 32-deep bounded channel. HTTP/2 flow control (4 MiB stream window, 8 MiB connection window on the server) applies backpressure to the gRPC call; the channel applies it to the reader task; the file is never fully buffered. This maps application-level streaming onto transport-level windows without custom flow-control code.

Move the sender into the reader task

The most instructive bug the SDK fixed: if the upload sender is held by the calling future, the stream never sees half-close/EOF and the server waits forever for the final frame — a deadlock. The fix is structural: tokio::spawn the reader task and move the sender into it. When the task ends (EOF or error), the sender drops and the gRPC stream half-closes. In Go this is CloseSend(); in Python, exhausting the request iterator. Whatever the language, EOF must be derived from the data source, never from a timer or a flag.

Metadata-based auth for streaming RPCs

tonic interceptors run only for unary RPCs, so the SDK attaches authorization: Bearer <jwt> (marked sensitive) to the Request<T> carrying the stream itself. The server correspondingly verifies JWT and api-key inside streaming handlers. Client lesson: any language whose interceptor layer skips streams needs the header attached at call construction — the Go metadata.AppendToOutgoingContext and Python metadata= patterns in the client pages exist for this reason.

Retry policy: transport codes only, idempotent targets

RetryPolicy retries exactly Unavailable, DeadlineExceeded and Aborted with exponential backoff doubling from 100 ms, capped at 2 s, three attempts by default. Business rejections (NotFound, PermissionDenied, InvalidArgument) surface immediately — retrying cannot fix them. Retries wrap unary calls only; streaming uploads are not retried by the facade. Safety comes from server-side idempotency:

OperationIdempotency guarantee
Upload (same name)Replaces the previous manifest atomically; old one soft-deleted
DeleteIdempotent — deleting a missing file is 404/NOT_FOUND, not an error to unwind
GC triggerPure function of current state; running it twice is harmless

Error surface

Map gRPC codes to your language’s exception taxonomy once, in the facade: NotFound → not-found error, PermissionDenied → authz error, ResourceExhausted → quota/size error, DataLoss → corruption, Unavailable → transient. The complete code ↔ HTTP mapping is in Errors.

TLS and CA pinning

connect_tls(endpoint, ca_pem) pins a private CA when given, otherwise uses native roots; the dial name must match a SAN in the certificate. The same discipline applies to every client: pin in internal clusters, verify names in external ones, and never disable verification to “fix” a SAN mismatch — see TLS.

Usage

Testing strategy

The SDK is validated by tests/grpc_test.rs, and the harness pattern is worth copying for your own clients:

  • Ephemeral-port server fixture. Bind TcpListener to 127.0.0.1:0, serve on it, extract the real port — no fixed ports, no cross-test interference.
  • wait_for_ready loop. Poll TCP connect with a bounded retry (100 × 20 ms) instead of sleep(1) guesses.
  • Concurrent upload matrix. Upload the same payload from N tasks simultaneously; dedup means exactly one writer wins per chunk and everything else is a dedup hit — a strong race detector target.
  • Round-trip assertions on both layers. Byte-identical content plus DownloadHeader.original_hash equality; the header check catches framing bugs that content comparison alone can mask.

Proto versioning and breaking changes

The file_storage.v1 package follows the standard compatibility contract:

  • Field numbers are forever — never reuse or renumber; add new fields only.
  • Retired fields get reserved (number and name) so accidental reuse fails at codegen.
  • Wire-compatible additions (new optional fields, new oneof branches) are minor changes; any change to a field’s type, number or streaming contract is major and requires a v2 package.
  • Run buf breaking against the last released descriptor set in CI; the server’s reflection serves the current descriptors, so clients built from stale protos fail loudly rather than silently.

Examples

The retry policy, condensed to its decision table:

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
// src/grpc/client/retry.rs — the shape to copy
RetryPolicy::new(3, Duration::from_millis(100), Duration::from_secs(2))
// attempt 1 fails Unavailable -> sleep 100ms
// attempt 2 fails Unavailable -> sleep 200ms ... doubling, capped at 2s
// attempt 3 fails Unavailable -> return the error
// any NotFound/PermissionDenied/InvalidArgument -> return immediately, no sleep
}

And the half-close discipline in pseudocode for any language:

spawn reader task:
    loop { n = read(source, 1 MiB) }
        n == 0 -> break            # EOF
        send(UploadChunk{data})
    drop sender                    # <-- half-close; server finishes the upload
await response

See also

  • gRPC API — messages, windows, server tuning values.
  • Rust Client — the implemented facade in full.
  • Errors — the mapping table referenced above.
  • Authentication — token model behind the metadata rules.
  • CLI Recipes — grpcurl, the fastest way to test a client change.

Benchmarks

The service ships two measurement paths: criterion micro/macro benchmarks for the pipeline internals (benches/throughput.rs) and an ignored end-to-end smoke test that produces HTTP-level throughput numbers. This page records the measured results with their environment caveats, explains how to reproduce them, and how to read criterion output when you change a hot path. Related knobs are in Tuning; finding where time goes is in Profiling.

Overview

Measured results (release build, opt-level=3, thin LTO off in this measurement, 8-way chunk pipeline, zstd level 3). Environment disclaimer: 2-vCPU sandbox with an NVMe-backed overlayfs — treat the ratios between rows as meaningful and the absolute numbers as machine-dependent; re-run on your hardware before publishing comparisons.

MetricMeasuredNote
CDC chunking (pure)1134 MB/s4 MiB input, 8 KiB/32 KiB/128 KiB min/avg/max
zstd compress (pure)1824 MB/s128 KiB blocks, level 3
Upload pipeline (in-process, 2 MiB)273 MB/schunk → hash → compress → blob fsync → redb txn
Upload via HTTP (64 MiB)119 MB/srandom bytes (incompressible → stored raw); fsync-dominated
Download via HTTP, cold399 MB/sdisk → verify → decompress
Download via HTTP, warm1107 MB/sL1 plaintext cache hits
Range (1 MiB slice)0.9 mscross-chunk slice
Retrieval path (L1 hit, in-process)~29 µs / 2 MiBdownload_2mb_warm criterion bench

How it works

The criterion file defines four benches: cdc_chunk_4mb (pure chunker on 4 MiB of pseudo-random data), zstd_compress_128kb (compressor at level 3 with min_size 1024), upload_pipeline/upload_2mb (a real AppState over a tempdir, streaming 2 MiB through pipeline.process) and retrieval/download_2mb_cold / download_2mb_warm (a seeded file streamed back through the retrieval path — the warm variant hits L1). The smoke test (perf_smoke in tests/integration/perf.rs) boots a real HTTP server, uploads 64 MiB of pseudo-random bytes, downloads it cold and warm, times a 1 MiB range read and prints dedup ratio from index statistics.

Expected gRPC framing overhead: every gRPC message carries a 5-byte length-prefixed frame plus protobuf tags, on top of HTTP/2 headers. For bulk transfers with 1 MiB pieces this amortises to well under 1%, but it is not zero — do not expect gRPC upload numbers to match raw HTTP body streaming byte-for-byte, and measure both if the transport matters to you.

Usage

Run your own numbers:

# criterion pipeline/retrieval benches
cargo bench --bench throughput

# HTTP end-to-end smoke (ignored by default; prints MB/s and dedup ratio)
cargo test --release --test integration_test perf_smoke -- --ignored --nocapture

Interpreting criterion output:

upload_pipeline/upload_2mb
                        time:   [7.25 ms 7.31 ms 7.38 ms]
                        change: [-3.1% -1.4% +0.2%] (p = 0.11 > 0.05)
                        No change in performance detected.
  • The middle number is the median estimate; the brackets are the confidence interval.
  • change compares against the previous run stored in target/criterion — regressions larger than the noise band (a few percent) are real.
  • The upload_pipeline group uses sample_size(10) because each iteration commits redb transactions; expect wider intervals than the pure benches.
  • Save and compare baselines across branches: 
    cargo bench --bench throughput -- --save-baseline main
# on your branch:
cargo bench --bench throughput -- --baseline main

Configuration

Dedup measurement recipe

Dedup is verifiable in one round trip — re-upload the same 512 KiB file and check that every chunk was a dedup hit:

head -c 524288 /dev/urandom > /tmp/dup.bin
A=$(curl -s -X POST "localhost:8080/api/v1/files?name=dup.bin" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" --data-binary @/tmp/dup.bin)
B=$(curl -s -X POST "localhost:8080/api/v1/files?name=dup.bin" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" --data-binary @/tmp/dup.bin)
echo "$B" | jq '{chunk_count, dedup_chunks, dedup_bytes, size_stored}'
# dedup_chunks == chunk_count and size_stored stays flat: the second upload stored nothing.

Uniform content (all zeros, all one byte) collapses to a single chunk per hash — that is the pathological best case, not the realistic one; pseudo-random files give honest dedup numbers.

Examples

Benchmark-to-optimisation loop, concretely:

  1. Establish a baseline (--save-baseline main).
  2. Make one change (for example raise PIPELINE_CONCURRENCY in the bench harness).
  3. cargo bench --bench throughput -- --baseline main — read change per bench.
  4. Confirm with the HTTP smoke test, since fsync effects dominate there; see the worked analysis in Profiling.

Expected relationships to sanity-check your environment: pure chunking and compression are CPU-bound and far above disk speed; the in-process pipeline sits below them because of blob fsyncs and the redb commit; the HTTP upload number is lowest because body handling, multipart spooling and per-chunk durability all sit on the same 2 vCPUs. Warm downloads at ~1.1 GB/s confirm the L1 cache is doing its job (see Caching for the cache hierarchy behind that number).

See also

  • Tuning — every knob these benchmarks exercise.
  • Profiling — criterion deep dive, perf, flamegraphs, strace.
  • Chunking — why CDC throughput sets the ceiling.
  • Caching — L1/L2/L3 sizing behind warm-read numbers.
  • Monitoring — production latency metrics that mirror these benches.

Tuning

Every performance knob in the configuration, with its trade-off and a recommended starting value. The defaults (FastCDC 8 KiB/32 KiB/128 KiB, zstd level 3, 256 MiB L1 + 512 MiB L2 caches, shard depth 2, 8-way pipeline) are sensible for mixed workloads; deviate when your workload is measurably different, using Benchmarks and Profiling to justify the change. Full key reference: Configuration reference.

Overview

Tuning questions in priority order: (1) is the CPU or the disk the bottleneck for your content, (2) is dedup granularity right for your file shapes, (3) are your caches sized for your hot set, (4) are the durability and replication settings matched to your consistency needs. Config changes require a restart — there is no hot reload.

How it works

Chunking

KeyDefaultTrade-off
chunking.min8192Lower bound on chunk size; smaller min → finer boundaries → better dedup on edited documents, more chunks to index
chunking.avg32768Target size; the dominant lever on chunk count (a 1 GiB file ≈ 32k chunks at default avg)
chunking.max131072Hard cap; validation allows max ≤ 16 MiB and min ≤ avg ≤ max

Constraint: max ≤ 16 MiB (validator-enforced). Smaller chunks mean better dedup granularity for repeatedly-edited files (FastCDC re-syncs boundaries near edits) but one chunks row and one ChunkRef per chunk — more redb bytes, larger manifests, more per-chunk work. Larger chunks shrink metadata and amortise fsyncs but dedup only on large identical regions.

Compression

KeyDefaultTrade-off
compression.level3 (range 1..=22)Each step up buys some ratio for real CPU; 3 keeps the compressor roughly matched to chunking throughput; 9+ is archive-grade, not ingest-grade
compression.min_size1024Chunks smaller than this are stored raw; also falls back to raw automatically when compression does not shrink — avoids wasting CPU on incompressible data

Caches

KeyDefaultTrade-off
cache.l1_max_bytes268435456 (256 MiB)Plaintext chunks; bigger = more warm-read hits (up to ~1100 MB/s observed), direct RAM cost
cache.l1_ttl_secs300Eviction pressure relief; chunk content is immutable so TTL affects memory, not correctness
cache.l2_max_bytes536870912 (512 MiB)Compressed blobs (pre-decrypt); the read-ahead target
cache.l2_ttl_secs3600Same immutability argument as L1
cache.manifest_capacity10000L3 manifest entries; manifests are small, so this is cheap and rarely needs tuning
cache.manifest_ttl_secs60Manifest staleness window on listings/reads

Read-ahead prefetches blobs of chunks N+1..N+4 into L2 only — sizing L2 matters most for sequential reads of large files.

Storage layout and pipeline

KeyDefaultTrade-off
storage.shard_depth2 (range 0–4)Blob path fan-out ab/cd/<hash>.bin; 0 puts every blob in one directory (directory-entry pressure at millions of files), higher depth adds path/stat cost per blob
PIPELINE_CONCURRENCY (env)8Concurrent compress/encrypt/write tasks; chunking and hashing stay sequential by design. More concurrency = better disk/CPU overlap, more memory

fsync trade-off — design decision. Blobs are written tmp → rename → dir fsync, and the index transaction commits with Durability::Immediate (WAL + fsync). This is deliberately not configurable: it is what makes crash-consistent backups trivial (see Backup & Restore in the Operations chapter) and corruption detection honest. Upload throughput is fsync-dominated on slow storage (~119 MB/s vs 273 MB/s in-process on the benchmark sandbox); the lever is disk quality, not config. What is tunable is replication overhead: batch_size/flush_interval_ms control how much network work piggybacks per upload, and sync mode’s cost is bounded by sync_timeout_ms.

Limits and rate limiting

KeyDefaultTrade-off
rate_limit.per_second / burst500 / 1000 (per client IP, REST)Burst must cover your clients’ fan-out per interval; too small throttles legitimate bulk uploads, too large weakens the limiter. gRPC uses a per-tenant token bucket; streaming RPCs are not rate limited
gRPC message caps256 MiB max message, 4 MiB stream / 8 MiB connection windowsRaise client windows only with matching memory; the server values are fixed

Usage

Recommended starting values by workload:

KnobMixed files (default)Backup archiveHot CDN-ishVM images / containers
chunking.avg327682621443276832768
chunking.max1310724194304131072131072
compression.level31933
cache.l1_max_bytes256 MiB64 MiB1 GiB256 MiB
cache.l2_max_bytes512 MiB256 MiB2 GiB512 MiB
PIPELINE_CONCURRENCY8488

Rationale: archives optimise ratio and metadata volume (bigger chunks, high level, small caches since access is cold); hot read-heavy deployments buy RAM for L1/L2 — read-ahead is already 4 blobs and needs no config; VM images are exactly the workload where the default CDC shines (fixed 4 KiB/64 KiB chunks would resync nothing after a header edit).

Examples

Applying a profile without editing the YAML — env overrides with FS_ + __ nesting:

FS_CHUNKING__AVG=262144 \
FS_CHUNKING__MAX=4194304 \
FS_COMPRESSION__LEVEL=19 \
FS_CACHE__L1_MAX_BYTES=67108864 \
FS_CACHE__L2_MAX_BYTES=268435456 \
file-storage --config /etc/file-storage/default.yaml

Then validate the change with the same method every time:

cargo test --release --test integration_test perf_smoke -- --ignored --nocapture
cargo bench --bench throughput

Compare against your saved baseline and keep the winning profile in config management; revert anything that wins in a microbenchmark but loses in the HTTP smoke test.

See also

  • Benchmarks — how to measure before and after.
  • Profiling — attributing time when a knob does not help.
  • Chunking — FastCDC mechanics behind the chunking knobs.
  • Caching — the three-level hierarchy being sized above.
  • Configuration reference — the complete key list with defaults.

Profiling

How to attribute time when a benchmark regresses or production latency climbs: criterion for repeatable comparisons, perf and flamegraphs for the running binary, strace for syscall-level costs such as fsync, and an honest note on runtime and allocator tooling that is not wired in by default. The pipeline stages you will see in every profile run chunking → BLAKE3 hashing → zstd → fdatasync. Measurement baselines live in Benchmarks; the knobs you would turn afterwards in Tuning.

Overview

The upload path is sequential through chunking and hashing, concurrent (8 tasks) through compression, encryption and blob writes, and ends in exactly one redb transaction per file. The download path is L1 → L2 → disk with BLAKE3 verification after each disk load. Each stage has a distinct profile signature, so a flamegraph usually identifies the stage before you read a single line of code.

upload:  FastCDC ──> BLAKE3 ──> zstd ──> AES-GCM (opt.) ──> blob tmp+rename+fsync ──> redb txn (1/file)
         sequential  sequential  8-way concurrent (compress/encrypt/write)
download: L1 hit ─┐
          L2 hit ─┼─> assemble ──> serve (disk loads are BLAKE3-verified; N+1..N+4 read-ahead into L2)
          disk  ─┘

Figure 1: Where profile samples will accumulate on each path.

How it works

Criterion deep dive

cargo bench --bench throughput            # runs cdc_chunk_4mb, zstd_compress_128kb,
                                          # upload_pipeline/upload_2mb, retrieval/download_2mb_cold|warm
cargo bench --bench throughput -- --save-baseline pre
cargo bench --bench throughput -- --baseline pre    # after your change

HTML reports with per-iteration distributions are written under target/criterion/report/index.html. The pipeline benches use sample_size(10) (each iteration commits redb transactions), so trust only changes outside the ±3–5% noise band; the pure chunking/compression benches are far tighter.

perf on the release binary

# kernel.perf_event_paranoid <= 1 and access to /proc/sys/kernel/kptr_restrict
# (in containers: run with --cap-add=PERFMON or --privileged for sampling)
perf record -F 99 -g -- target/release/file-storage --config config/default.yaml
# drive load in another terminal (bench, perf_smoke, or curl loop)
perf report -g --no-children              # then: perf report --sort=dso,symbol
perf stat -e cycles,instructions,cache-misses,branch-misses -p $(pgrep file-storage)

The release profile builds with strip=symbols; for readable stacks either profile the binary before stripping in your CI artefact or build locally with debug = true added to [profile.release].

Flamegraphs

cargo install cargo-flamegraph
cargo flamegraph --release --bin file-storage -- --config config/default.yaml
# drive load, Ctrl-C; opens flamegraph.svg

strace for fsync dominance

strace -c -f -e trace=fsync,fdatasync,rename -p $(pgrep file-storage)
# drive one upload, then read the call counts and time share

If fdatasync/fsync dominates % time, the upload is durability-bound — that is the intended design (blobs fsync before the index commit), not a bug.

Runtime and heap tooling — optional, not shipped

Two tools require instrumentation that the shipped binary does not include; document honestly before promising them:

  • tokio-console needs the console-subscriber installed as the tracing subscriber plus the tokio_unstable cfg at build time. There is no subscriber wiring in this codebase; if task stalls are your target, add the wiring in a private build, run tokio-console, and keep that build out of production.
  • jemalloc heap profiling is not linked by default (the binary uses the system allocator). To heap-profile, link jemallocator in a private build or LD_PRELOAD libjemalloc with MALLOC_CONF=prof:true,lg_prof_sample:19, then read the output with jeprof.

Usage

Worked example: fsync dominance in upload throughput

The benchmark table shows upload via HTTP at 119 MB/s (64 MiB, incompressible) versus 273 MB/s for the in-process pipeline (2 MiB). Profiling explains the gap:

  1. strace -c -f -e trace=fsync,fdatasync,rename during an HTTP upload shows one fdatasync per blob write (tmp + rename + dir fsync per chunk) plus the redb commit — the syscall share of wall time is the majority.
  2. The pure benches bound the CPU stages: chunking ~1134 MB/s and zstd ~1824 MB/s are both far above the observed 119 MB/s, so CPU is not the constraint.
  3. The in-process number (273 MB/s) shares the same fsyncs but drops HTTP body handling and serves a smaller file with better cache locality; the difference between 119 and 273 is dominated by syscall and HTTP overhead on the same 2 vCPUs, not by a config knob.
  4. Conclusion path: on this hardware the only meaningful lever is faster storage (or accepting durability as-is — see Tuning); raising compression level or concurrency will not move the number, which a quick --baseline bench run confirms.

Interpreting hotspots by stage

Signature in profileStageUsual levers
Rolling-hash / boundary search heavyChunking (FastCDC)Larger chunking.avg (fewer boundary checks) — see Chunking
Wide zstd frames, compression.level highCompressionDrop compression.level; widen compression.min_size for incompressible content
fdatasync/fsync dominantBlob write + redb commitFaster storage; PIPELINE_CONCURRENCY overlap; nothing else (durability is fixed)
BLAKE3 wide but cold downloads slowDisk read pathCache sizing (l2_max_bytes), read-ahead already fixed at N+1..N+4
Lock/syscall contention on redbIndex commitsFewer, larger uploads (batching at the client); not tunable per request

Examples

A production-safe low-overhead snapshot of a live process:

perf stat -p $(pgrep -f 'file-storage') -- sleep 30   # IPC and cache-miss ratio only
strace -c -f -p $(pgrep -f 'file-storage') &          # syscall mix (attach briefly)

Then correlate with metrics from Monitoring: rising upload_duration_seconds P99 with flat CPU points at storage; rising CPU with flat latency points at compression settings.

See also

  • Benchmarks — the numbers and baselines this page explains how to dissect.
  • Tuning — the configuration levers for each hotspot.
  • Chunking — FastCDC internals.
  • Caching — cache levels and read-ahead behaviour.
  • Configuration reference — keys mentioned above with defaults.

Migration v0 → v1

Version 1 is the first release with the gRPC listener running alongside the REST API, and the upgrade is deliberately boring: the on-disk formats are unchanged, the REST API is unchanged, and every new configuration key is additive with a default. If you were running a pre-release v0 build, the migration is “back up, stop the old binary, start the new one on the same data directory.”

Overview

What v1 adds on top of v0:

  • gRPC listener on server.grpc_port (default 9090) — same process, same state; see gRPC and ADR 0008 — gRPC alongside REST.
  • server.grpc_tls config block (server certificate, optional client CA for mTLS).
  • ReplicationService over gRPC — additive; the built-in WAL shipper still uses the REST /internal/* endpoints by default, and both are guarded by the same replication.api_key.
  • gRPC health + reflection services for probes and grpcurl.
  • Behavioral change: uploading under an existing name now replaces the previous file — the old manifest is soft-deleted and remains visible in listings only with include_deleted=true (admin).

Metadata and audit semantics are unchanged: audit actions, event fields, tenant scoping and the error catalog mean the same thing on both transports.

How it works

Config compatibility. The config struct deserializes with serde defaults, so an existing v0 YAML file keeps working untouched: missing keys (server.grpc_port, server.grpc_tls, …) are filled with defaults (9090, TLS off). All new keys are additive; nothing was renamed or removed. See the Configuration reference.

Wire-format compatibility. v0 adopters were pre-release, and the redb schema has not changed since: tables (chunks, files, name_index, tag_index, audit, tenants), the block-store layout (storage.root shards of 64-hex blobs) and manifests are identical. There is no data-conversion step.

REST compatibility. Paths, request/response shapes, error codes and status semantics of /api/v1 and the probes are byte-for-byte what they were. The only observable REST-level difference is the same-name-replacement behavior above.

Configuration

New keys you may want to set after upgrading:

KeyDefaultNotes
server.grpc_port9090Exposing the port is opt-in: inside the container image it is EXPOSEd, but nothing listens on it externally until you publish/firewall it
server.grpc_tls.enabledfalseTurn on with cert_path/key_path (mTLS adds client_ca_path)
replication.*unchangedThe gRPC ReplicationService reuses replication.api_key — no new secret

Usage

Upgrade procedure (assumes a single binary deployment; containers do the same dance with image + restart):

  1. Back up first — stop nothing yet; take a filesystem-level backup of storage.root, database.path, and encryption.keys_dir (the keys are mandatory backups when encryption is on). See Backup & Restore.
  2. Stop the old v0 binary. In-flight uploads abort; queued replication ops live in memory and are lost — re-push anything important with resync after the upgrade.
  3. Start the new binary on the same data directory, pointing at your existing YAML:
cp -a data data.bak.$(date +%F)          # 1. backup (or snapshot the volume)
systemctl stop file-storage              # 2. stop v0
./file-storage-v1 config/default.yaml    # 3. start v1 on the same data dir
curl -s localhost:8080/ready             # 4. verify probes
grpcurl -plaintext localhost:9090 grpc.health.v1.Health/Check
  1. Verify: /health, /ready both healthy; upload a test file, list it, download it, delete it; confirm the gRPC listener answers (health check above); confirm replication is shipping (file_storage_replication_ops_applied_total in Monitoring).
  2. Publish or firewall grpc_port deliberately — do not expose it accidentally.

Rollback

Because no data format changed, rollback is symmetric: stop the v1 binary and start the v0 binary on the same data directory. Two caveats: (a) files created under v1 are ordinary manifests and blobs, readable by v0; (b) soft-deleted “replaced” files are hidden from v0 listings exactly like other soft deletes; (c) if you enabled encryption or changed retention under v1, revert those config keys to the v0 values before starting the old binary, and remember refresh tokens from the v1 process are gone (they were in memory). If anything looks wrong, restore the backup from step 1 instead.

Examples

v0 YAML (still valid under v1, unchanged):

server:
  host: "0.0.0.0"
  port: 8080
storage:
  root: "data/blocks"
auth:
  jwt_secret: "change-me-in-production-0123456789abcdef"
  users:
    - username: admin
      password: "admin123"
      tenant_id: "01930000-0000-7000-8000-000000000001"
      role: admin

Same file after opting into gRPC with TLS:

server:
  host: "0.0.0.0"
  port: 8080
  grpc_port: 9090
  grpc_tls:
    enabled: true
    cert_path: "/etc/file-storage/tls/server.crt"
    key_path: "/etc/file-storage/tls/server.key"

Checking that replaced files still exist (admin only):

curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN" \
  'localhost:8080/api/v1/files?name=report.pdf&include_deleted=true'
